Nonabelian Fourier kernels on SL 2 {\mathrm{SL}}_{2} and GL 2 {\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
Zhilin Luo, Bao Châu Ngô

Abstract
. For G = SL 2 G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2} or GL 2 {\mathrm{GL}}_{2} , we present explicit formulas for the nonabelian Fourier kernels on G G , as conjectured by A. Braverman and D. Kazhdan. Additionally, we furnish explicit formulas for the orbital Hankel transform on G G , a topic investigated by the second author, and provide an explicit formula for the stable orbital integral of the basic function. These results are applicable to local fields with residual characteristics other than two.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 11F70, 11F70, 22E50; Secondary 11F27, 43A32, 43A80

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Historical context

In his celebrated thesis (
[
Tat50
]
), J. Tate established the basic analytical properties (the Hecke theory) of the Hecke
L
-functions through Fourier analysis on the additive group. The Hecke
L
-functions
L(s,\chi)
associated to Grössencharacters
\chi
of the idele group
{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}
are expressed as the poles of the global zeta integrals attached to
\chi
and Schwartz-Bruhat functions on the adele ring
{\mathbb{A}}
. The meromorphic continuation and the functional equation are consequences of the Fourier transform and the Poisson summation formula.

Developments in the modern theory of automorphic forms rest in a set of conjectures formulated by R. Langlands in
[
Lan70
]
with the reciprocity and functoriality conjectures being the two pillars. The concept of the automorphic
L
-function
L(s,\pi,\rho)
attached to an automorphic representation
\pi
of a reductive group
G
over a global field
k
and an
L
-homomorphism
\rho:{}^{L}G\to{\mathrm{GL}}(V_{\rho})
of the Langlands dual group, which is a vast generation of the Riemann zeta function, plays a central role in these conjectures. The functions
L(s,\pi,\rho)
are defined via the Euler product, which only converges on a half-plane. One of the major implications of the functoriality conjecture is that these
L
-functions have a meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane satisfying a functional equation analogous to the Riemann zeta function.

It is natural to ask if one can establish the basic analytical properties for general automorphic
L
-functions
L(s,\pi,\rho)
for general reductive groups
G
over
k
via harmonic analysis on
G({\mathbb{A}}_{k})
following the approach of the Tate thesis, thus bypassing the functoriality conjecture. By the Fourier analysis on the vector space of
n\times n
matrices, R. Godement and H. Jacquet (
[
GJ72
]
) generalized the work of Tate to
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{n}
over a central simple algebra and
\rho
the standard representation of its dual group. Around 2000, A. Braverman and D. Kazhdan (
[
BK00
]
) formalized Tate and Godement-Jacquet’s approaches in a series of conjectures aiming at the meromorphic continuation and functional equation general automorphic
L
-functions. These proposals were elaborated by L. Lafforgue (
[
Laf16
]
) and given in a more precise form by the second author (
[
Ngô20
]
).

The local aspect of the Braverman-Kazhdan program stipulated the existence of a space of
\rho
-Schwartz functions, generalizing the space of usual Schwartz functions on the space of matrices, and the
\rho
-Fourier transform generalizing the usual Fourier transform on the space of matrices. Over a local field, the expected form of the
\rho
-Fourier transform is the convolution with a stably invariant distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on
G(F)
.
The main focus of this work is to construct the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
, which we call the nonabelian Fourier kernel.

The distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
depends on a nontrivial additive character
\psi
of the local field, such that after regularization, the action of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on any irreducible admissible representation
\pi
of
G(F)
is well-defined and is equal to the multiplication by the Langlands local gamma factor
{\gamma}(\cdot,\pi,\rho,\psi)
. The distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
is expected to be represented by a locally integrable function, which is smooth over the regular semisimple locus. Because it is stably invariant, it descends to a function on the Steinberg quotient, which, in the case
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, is the affine line of the trace variable. The main result of this paper is to provide a formula for
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
in the case of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
(and
{{\mathrm{GL}}}_{2}
), as a function of the trace (and the determinant) variables, thus answering a question of Braverman and Kazhdan in
[
BK00
]
. Beyond the
{\rm SL}_{2}
and
{\rm GL}_{2}
cases, we elaborate a general framework based on Langlands’ stable transfer (
[
Lan13
]
) and what we call the Lafforgue transform (
[
Laf14
]
). These transforms, which exist in general, can be made explicit in the case of
{{\mathrm{GL}}}_{2}
and
{{\mathrm{SL}}}_{2}
.

The finite fields analog of the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
is an equivariant perverse sheaf. Its construction was conjectured in
[
BK00
, §9]
and studied for
G
of semi-simple rank one in
[
BK03
]
. The conjecture has been verified by S. Cheng and the second author in
[
CN18
]
for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{n}
, by G. Laumon and E. Letellier for general
G
in
[
LL19
]
, and by T.-H. Chen in
[
Che22
]
for general
G
when the characteristic of the underlying finite field is large and
\mathcal{D}
-module setting.

Much less is known in the
p
-adic case. When
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{n}
and
\rho
is the standard representation, this is the case studied by Godement and Jacquet (
[
GJ72
]
), and
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\psi({\mathrm{tr}}(\cdot))
is the standard Fourier kernel. The Fourier kernel is also known in the case of tori as suggested by Braverman and Kazhdan (
[
BK00
]
) and made more precise by the second author (
[
Ngô20
]
). The first author calculates the spherical part of the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
for any
G
and
\rho
in
[
Luo19
]
over a local field of characteristic zero. In
[
JLZ22
]
, Jiang, Zhang, and the first-named author give a formula for
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
for
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}
and the standard representation of
{}^{L}G
.

Motivated by the work of Godement and Jacquet, the second author (
[
Ngô20
]
) speculated that the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,G}
should be patched from
\{\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,T}\}_{T\subset G}
where
T
runs over all maximal tori of
G
. However, as pointed out by L. Lafforgue (
[
Laf14
]
), the naive patching construction fails to provide the correct kernel for the
\rho
-Fourier transform for
\rho={\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}
of
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
because it does not act on principal series representations with the correct gamma factors. For
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, Lafforgue proposed a recipe to correct the naive patching construction so that the action of the resulting distribution on the principal series representations is given by the multiplication by the correct Langlands local gamma factor. However, whether the resulting distribution acts on supercuspidal representations by the correct gamma factors or not is unclear. Comparing with the result of
[
JLZ22
]
, one can show that the recipe of Lafforgue works for
\rho={\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}
of
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, but it fails for supercuspidal representations and for higher symmetric powers
\rho
. The main result of this paper is to provide an explicit formula of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
in the case
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
and for an arbitrary representation
\rho
under the assumption that the residual characteristic of the underlying local field
F
is not equal to two. When
\rho
is the standard representation attached to
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, the formula found in this paper matches with the formula of
[
JLZ22
]
. We will show in this work that the naive patching construction and the Lafforgue transform, although not sufficient, are still essential ingredients in the construction of the correct kernel of the
\rho
-Fourier transform.

We also discuss the
\rho
-orbital Hankel transform considered by the second author (
[
Ngô20
]
), which is the descent of the
\rho
-Fourier transform to the level of stable orbital integrals of
G(F)
. Everling has calculated the orbital Hankel transform for the usual Fourier transform, i.e., when
\rho
is the standard representation, and found rather complicated formulae (
[
Eve98
]
). It is, therefore, a good surprise that the stable orbital
\rho
-Hankel transform can be given a nice formula.

The nonabelian Fourier and Hankel transform will be given in completely explicit integral formulae, which can be better understood only from the representation theoretic perspective. In particular, they are given in terms of Langlands’ stable transfers in combination with their canonical partial splittings, and also the Lafforgue transform, which are new ingredients we introduce in this work.

1.2.
Organization of the paper

As we want to state our result in its proper context, we will review the contents of different sections of the paper in order. In Section
2
, we review broadly the Braverman-Kazhdan proposal following the presentation of
[
Ngô20
]
. In subsection
2.2
, we rigorously build the local part of the Braverman-Kazhdan program for tori, particularly the Fourier kernel in this case. These abelian Fourier kernels will be later used to construct the nonabelian Fourier kernel. In subsection
2.3
, we supply explicit formulae for the nonabelian Fourier kernels in some low symmetric power representations
\rho
. The case of the symmetric square representation follows the work of Jiang-Luo-Zhang (
[
JLZ22
]
), which treats the case of symplectic group and the standard representation of its dual group. In subsection
2.4
, we present explicit formulae for the nonabelian Fourier kernels when
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
.

In Section
3
, we recall the normalization of orbital and stable orbital integrals of a reductive group
G
over a local field
F
following the work of Frenkel-Langlands-Ngô (
[
FLN10
]
). They are, on one hand, interpreted both as functions on the space of conjugacy classes or the stable conjugacy classes, aka the Steinberg-Hitchin base, which are equipped with the canonical measure. On the other hand, they can be interpreted as elements of the cocenter
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin}={\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
of the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
of
G(F)
. We introduce the Kazhdan cocenter
{\mathcal{C}}
, which is a completion of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin}
, in a similar way as the Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}
is a completion of the subalgebra
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm fin}
of regular functions on the Bernstein variety
\Omega(G(F))
supported by finitely many components. We introduce the stable cocenter
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin,st}
and formulate Langlands’ concept of stable transfer in terms of the stable cocenter that is a canonical linear map

{\mathcal{T}}_{\varphi}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin,st}(G(F))\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin,st}(H(F))

in the presence of a homomorphism of
L
-groups
\varphi:\,\!\!^{L}H\to\,\!\!^{L}G
. Because elements of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin,st}(G(F))
can be interpreted as functions of the Steinberg-Hitchin space
{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}(F)
, we expect that there is a nice integral giving rise to the stable transfer factor. In the case,
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
H
is a one-dimensional torus associated with a quadratic extension
E/F
, this integral transform

(1.2.1)

{\mathcal{T}}_{E}(f)=\lambda_{E/F}\pi_{E}^{*}({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\eta_{E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(f)))

where
\eta_{E}
is the quadratic character of
F^{\times}
associated with the quadratic extension
E/F
,
\pi_{E}:E^{1}\to F
is the trace map, and
\lambda_{E/F}
is the Weil constant appearing in
[
JL70
]
. This integral formula for the stable transfer is equivalent to the Gelfand-Graev character formula to be recalled in the following section. We also review the stable Bernstein center in the case of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, and show that it is a subalgebra of the usual Bernstein center which is known to experts.

The Gelfand-Graev character formula first appears in
[
GG63
]
but also in the famous book of Gelfand-Graev-Piatetsky-Shapiro (
[
GGPS69
]
). In modern languages, it expresses the character of the dihedral representation of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
or the corresponding stable character of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
as an additive convolution in the trace variable. One of the side outcomes of this work is that from staring at different formulas, the additive structure in the trace variable plays a major role in the representation theory of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
, a fact which is not easy to explain from the theoretical standpoint. In Section
4
, we recall the construction of the dihedral representations from the Weil representation, and derive the Gelfand-Graev character formula, at least formally. We recall the Weil representation and the dihedral representations which will be later used in the proof of our descent (to tori) formula. In subsection
4.3
, we connect the Gelfand-Graev character formula with Langlands’ stable transfer factor.

In Section
5
, we construct the Lafforgue transform
\mathrm{Laf}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
from the Kazhdan cocenter to the Bernstein center. For every element
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
we can define
{\mathrm{tr}}(c,\pi)
the trace of
c
on every irreducible representation of
\pi
. Similarly, each element
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
acts on every irreducible representation
\pi
by a scalar
\gamma(z,\pi)
. The Lafforgue transform
{\rm Laf}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
is uniquely defined by the equation

\gamma(\mathrm{Laf}(c),[\pi])={\mathrm{tr}}(c,\pi)

for every point
[\pi]
in the Bernstein variety
\Omega(G)
such the inertial equivalence class
[\pi]
contains a unique irreducible representation
\pi
. In general, since
\mathrm{Laf}
is not injective, there are different choices of sections for
\mathrm{Laf}
. When
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, we introduce the following section
\mathrm{LafSec}:{\mathcal{Z}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
which factors through the subspace
{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp,\text{triv}}
of
{\mathcal{C}}
that are orthogonal to the trivial character, i.e.
{\mathrm{tr}}(c,\mathrm{triv})=0
. Notice that after localization,
\mathrm{Laf}
is an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module away from the Steinberg component, hence
\mathrm{LafSec}
is uniquely determined.
The Lafforgue transform
\mathrm{Laf}
induces map from the stable Kazhdan center to the stable Bernstein center

\mathrm{Laf}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{st}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}.

The main claim in this section is that, when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and restricted to the finite stable part
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
,
\mathrm{LafSec}
is given by the integral transform which induces an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
-modules
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}\simeq{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv},\mathrm{fin}}
,

(1.2.2)

\mathrm{LafSec}(z)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\left(|.|^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(z)\right)

where both
z
and
\mathrm{LafSec}(z)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
are regarded as functions of the trace variable and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}
is the Fourier transform with respect to additive character
\psi
. We expect that a similar formula exists for all reductive groups. In this paper we prove it only for
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
. Even in those cases, we don’t know how to prove it directly by deriving it from what we call the descent formula. Although we prove it as a consequence of the descent formula, we stress that the above formula for the Lafforgue transform supplies a proper theoretical framework for the descent formula. It turns out that over a real field, the Lafforgue transform can be traced back to a simple relation between Chebyshev polynomials of first and second kind: Up to conjugation by the square-root of the Weyl discriminant, the derivative of Chebyshev polynomials of first kind is Chebyshev polynomials of second kind. Section
5
also contains the more or less known result that the stable Bernstein center for
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
is a subalgebra. We also recall some explicit calculations of Moy-Tadic (
[
MT02
]
) to justify the convergence of the integral representation of the Lafforgue transform over the stable locus.

Section
6
contains the statement and the proof of the descent formula. Given an element
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st}
represented as a function of the trace variable, we want to calculate its action on a dihedral representation. Because
z
lies in the stable Bernstein center, its action on the dihedral representation associated with a character
\chi
of
E^{1}
where
E/F
is a quadratic extension is a scalar. For
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
that scalar can be computed by using first the sections to the Lafforgue transform
\mathrm{LafSec}:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}
composed with the stable transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{E}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}(E^{1})
, and finally paired with
\chi
:

\int_{E^{1}}\chi(e)\big({\mathcal{T}}_{E}\circ\mathrm{LafSec}(z)\big)(e)\,\mathrm{d}e.

The descent formula from
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
to the torus
E^{1}
is equivalent to an explicit formula for
{\mathcal{T}}_{E}\circ\mathrm{LafSec}(z)

(1.2.3)

{\mathcal{T}}_{E}\circ\mathrm{LafSec}(z)=\lambda_{E/F}\pi_{E}^{*}\left({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\left(|.|^{-1}\eta_{E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(z)\right)\right).

where
\pi_{E}:E^{1}\to F
is the trace map, and
\eta_{E}
is the quadratic character associated to the quadratic extension
E/F
. This formula can be derived from the combination of the Gelfand-Graev formula for the stable transfer (
1.2.1
) and the integral formula for the Lafforgue transform (
1.2.2
). Still, we prove it directly using the Weil representation. In fact, we derive the integral formula (
1.2.2
) from the descent formula (
1.2.3
).

Section
7
focuses on a new feature of the cocenter and stable transfers. We show that if
E/F
is a nonsplit quadratic extension the stable transfer map
{\mathcal{T}}_{E}:{\mathcal{C}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))\to{\mathcal{C}}(E^{1})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
admits a canonical right inverse

{\mathcal{E}}_{E}:{\mathcal{C}}(E^{1})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}\to{\mathcal{C}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))

where
{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
is generated by the unique involution attached to
E/F
.
The construction of this right inverse is based on the elliptic orthogonality of character and stable characters, which in particular demonstrates the existence of another unitary structure on the affine line. It gives rise to the direct summand of
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))
corresponding to the quadratic extension
E^{1}
. If
E^{\prime}
is another nonsplit quadratic extension of
F
, then
{\mathcal{T}}_{E^{\prime}}\circ{\mathcal{E}}_{E}\neq 0
but the image is generated by the trivial character and the nontrivial quadratic character of
{E^{\prime}}^{1}
. If
E_{0}
is the split quadratic extension, we have
{\mathcal{T}}_{E_{0}}\circ{\mathcal{E}}_{E}=0
. From these remarks, we is a unique right inverse

{\mathcal{E}}_{E_{0}}:{\mathcal{C}}(E_{0}^{1})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}\to{\mathcal{C}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))

of
{\mathcal{T}}_{E_{0}}:{\mathcal{C}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))\to{\mathcal{C}}(E_{0}^{1})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
such that for all nonsplit quadratic extensions
E
of
F
we have
{\mathcal{T}}_{E}\circ{\mathcal{E}}_{E_{0}}=0
. We also have an explicit albeit complicated integral formula for
{\mathcal{E}}_{E_{0}}
. We thus obtain an “almost” direct decomposition of
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))
with summands corresponding to the four quadratic extensions of
F
. This is only an almost direct decomposition because the three summands corresponding to three nonsplit quadratic extension intersect in a two-dimensional vector space generated by the normalized elliptic character of the Steinberg representation and the normalized elliptic stable character of the unique
L
-packet with four elements discovered by Labesse and Langlands in
[
LL79
]
corresponding to the nontrivial quadratic character of
E^{1}
for each nonsplit quadratic extension
E
of
F
.

Armed with the operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{E}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{E}
and the almost direct decomposition of the cocenter, we can write down explicit formulae for the kernel of the nonstandard Fourier transform, the Hankel transform of stable orbital integrals, and also stable orbital integrals for all spherical functions, including the basic function. These are discussed in Section
8
, Section
9
and Section
10
.

1.3.
Explicit formulae

We now present explicit formulae that can be derived from the structures presented above. In fact, we sometimes need to prove the explicit formulae first and derive the structures from those explicit formulae.

Descent formula

The Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}
of
G(F)
, defined in
[
Ber84
]
, consists of invariant distributions on
G(F)
which are essentially of compact support. Every element
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
acts on each irreducible representation
\pi
of
G(F)
by a scalar that we denote by
\gamma(z,\pi)
. For every
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}
, the function
\pi\mapsto\gamma(z,\pi)
is an algebraic function on the Bernstein variety
\Omega(G(F))
classifying irreducible representations up to the inertial equivalence. We denote by
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm fin}
the nonunital subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Z}}
consisting of elements
z
such that the function
\pi\mapsto\gamma(z,\pi)
is supported by finitely many components of the Bernstein variety.

Let
z
be a stably invariant distribution on
G(F)={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
belonging to the nonunital subalgebra
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm fin}
.
Using some results of Moy and Tadic in
[
MT02
]
, we can prove that there exists a locally integrable function
z_{\mathfrak{c}}
on the Steinberg-Hitchin
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
base such that

z=(z_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c})\,\mathrm{d}g

where
\mathbf{c}={\mathrm{tr}}
is the trace map. It turns out that based on the explicit calculations in
[
MT02
]
, the function
z_{\mathfrak{c}}
enjoys the following characterization:

(1.3.1)

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(z_{\mathfrak{c}})
is globally bounded, smooth and vanishes at zero. Moreover,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(z_{\mathfrak{c}})/|\cdot|
is absolutely integrable.

Based on the above characterization, we establish the following descent formula.

Theorem 1.3.1
.

Let
E
be an étale quadratic
F
-algebra with
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}
the Weil representation on the space
{\mathcal{S}}(E)
of Schwartz-Bruhat functions on
E
. For every character
\chi
of
E^{1}={\mathrm{Ker}}(\mathrm{Nr}:E^{\times}\to F^{\times})
and for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
, the following identity holds

{\gamma}\big(z,{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)\big)=\int^{\mathrm{reg}}_{G(F)}z(g)\big({\mathcal{W}}_{E}(g)\phi\big)={\lambda}_{E/F}\bigg(\int_{E^{1}}\chi(e)\big({\mathcal{T}}_{E}\circ\mathrm{LafSec}(z)\big)({\mathrm{tr}}(e))\,\mathrm{d}_{E,1}e\bigg)\phi.

Here
\int^{\mathrm{reg}}_{G(F)}
is the regularized integral introduced in Section
6
,
{\lambda}_{E/F}={\lambda}_{E/F,\psi}
is the Weil constant, and

\mathrm{LafSec}(z)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(|\cdot|^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(z_{\mathfrak{c}})\big).

As a corollary, by the Gelfand-Graev character identity, we deduce the following fact:

Corollary 1.3.2
.

With the above notation the following identity holds

{\gamma}\big(z,{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)\big)={\mathrm{tr}}\big(\mathrm{LafSec}(z),{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)\big).

Parallel results hold for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
.

Inversion formulae

Given a stable distribution
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{fin}}
, the descent formula above provides an element in the cocenter
c=\mathrm{Laf}(z)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv},\mathrm{fin}}
that are connected by the following simple integral formulae

c=\mathrm{LafSec}(z)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(|\cdot|^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(z)\big),\quad z=\mathrm{Laf}(c)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(|\cdot|{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(c)\big).

On the other hand, starting from
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{fin}}
, for any étale quadratic
F
-algebra
E
, the Langlands’ stable transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{E}
provides an element in the cocenter of the maximal torus
E^{1}
determined by
E
,

z_{E}=c_{E}={\mathcal{T}}_{E}(c)={\lambda}_{E/F}\pi^{*}_{E}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\eta_{E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(c))\big)\in{\mathcal{C}}(E^{1})^{\mathrm{fin}}\simeq{\mathcal{Z}}(E^{1})^{\mathrm{fin}}.

A natural question is to reproduce
z
and
\mathrm{LafSec}(z)=c
from the datum
\{z_{E}=c_{E}\}_{E/F}
. When the residual characteristic of
F
is not equal to two, any irreducible representation of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
is a subquotient of a dihedral representation. Hence the datum
\{c_{E}\}_{E/F}
determines
z
and
c=\mathrm{LafSec}(z)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv},\mathrm{fin}}
uniquely. Moreover,
z=\mathrm{Laf}(c)
. Hence we are reduced to reproduce
c
from
\{c_{E}={\mathcal{T}}_{E}(c)\}_{E/F}
.

To state the inversion formulae, we introduce the following notation. Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be the set parametrizing stable conjugacy classes of maximal tori
\{E^{1}_{\alpha}\}_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}
in
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
,

{\mathcal{I}}=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}\{0\}&F={\mathbb{C}}\\
\{0,1\}&F={\mathbb{R}}\\
\{0,1,\pm 1/2\}&F\text{ non. archi.}\end{matrix}

Here
E_{0}=F\times F
corresponds to the split quadratic algebra,
T_{1}
corresponds to the unramified quadratic extension (resp. complex extension) when
F
is non-archimedean (resp. real), and
T_{\pm 1/2}
correspond to the two ramified extension of
F
. For every
\tau_{\alpha}
-invariant function
c_{\alpha}
on
E^{1}_{\alpha}
, where
\tau_{\alpha}
is the unique nontrivial involution attached to
E_{\alpha}/F
,
c_{\alpha}
descends to a unique function
\nu_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha})
on
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})
with the property that

c_{\alpha}=\pi^{*}_{\alpha}\big(\nu_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha})\big)

where
\pi_{\alpha}=\pi_{E_{\alpha}}
.

Theorem 1.3.3
.

With the above notation, for
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
and
c_{\alpha}={\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(c)\in{\mathcal{C}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
, the following statements hold.

(1)

When
F={\mathbb{C}}
,
c=\nu_{0}(c_{0})
;

(2)

When
F={\mathbb{R}}
,
c=\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha})
;

(3)

When
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic,

c-c^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}\cdot\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\Theta^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}=\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha}).

Here:

•

For
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(E_{1}^{\alpha})^{\tau_{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\to{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}

\displaystyle c_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha})=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{ell}}\cdot\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\cdot{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}\bigg(\frac{\nu_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha})}{\mathbf{v}{\Delta}}\bigg)

where
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\cdot)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(\eta_{\alpha}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\cdot)\big),

{\Delta}
is the square-root of Weyl discriminant,
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{ell}}
is the characteristic function of elliptic locus, and
\mathbf{v}:{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{ell}}(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}
is the function supported on elliptic regular semisimple locus such that
\mathbf{v}(\xi)={\mathrm{vol}}(E_{\alpha}^{1})^{-1}
whenever
\xi\in E^{1}_{\alpha}
;

•

{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
is the map from
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(E_{0}^{1})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
to functions on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
that is given by the following formulas

c_{0}\mapsto\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}\nu_{0}(c_{0})&F={\mathbb{C}}\\
\nu_{0}(c_{0})-{\mathcal{E}}_{1}({\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\nu_{0}(J_{0})))&F={\mathbb{R}}\\
\nu_{0}(c_{0})-{\mathcal{E}}_{1}({\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\nu_{0}(c_{0})))-\sum_{{\alpha}=\pm 1/2}{\mathcal{E}}^{++}_{{\alpha}}({\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(\nu_{0}(c_{0}))^{++})&F\text{ non-archi.}\end{matrix}

where
{\mathcal{E}}^{++}_{\alpha}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(\nu_{0}(c_{0}))^{++}
are the restriction of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(\nu_{0}(c_{0}))
to the subspace
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(E^{1}_{\alpha})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
consisting of functions whose Mellin transform evaluated at the trivial character and the unique quadratic character vanishes;

•

\Theta^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}
is the elliptic part of the Steinberg character, and

c^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}=\langle c,\Theta^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}\rangle=\int_{{\mathfrak{c}}(F)}c(\xi)\Theta^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}(\xi){\omega}_{\mathfrak{c}}.

When
F
is archimedean, it is also convenient to replace the space of smooth and compactly supported functions by Schwartz functions.

Theorem
1.3.3
reproduces
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
in turns of the descent datum
\{c_{\alpha}={\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(c)\}_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}
, except when
F
is non-archimedean (of odd residual characteristic), the descent datum is not sufficient due to the existence of the Steinberg representation.

In the following, we introduce the following two extended sections from
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(E^{1}_{0})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
to functions on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
:

Theorem 1.3.4
.

With the notation from Theorem
1.3.3
, when
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic, consider the following map from
c_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(E^{1}_{0})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
to functions on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\perp\mathrm{St}}_{0}(c_{0})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{0}(c_{0})-\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{ell}}\cdot\langle\nu_{0}(c_{0}),1-\phi_{{\delta}^{1/2}_{B}}\rangle

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\perp\mathrm{triv}}_{0}(c_{0})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{0}(c_{0})-\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{ell}}\cdot\langle\nu_{0}(c_{0}),1\rangle

where
1
is the constant function on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
and
\phi_{{\delta}_{B}^{1/2}}=\nu_{0}\big(\frac{{\delta}_{B}^{1/2}+{\delta}_{B}^{-1/2}}{{\Delta}}\big)
with
{\delta}_{B}
the modular character for the Borel subgroup of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
. Then

(1)

For
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin},\perp\mathrm{St}}
,

c={\mathcal{E}}^{\perp\mathrm{St}}_{0}(c_{0})+\sum_{{\alpha}\neq 0}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha});

(2)

For
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin},\perp\mathrm{triv}}
,

c={\mathcal{E}}^{\perp\mathrm{triv}}_{0}(c_{0})+\sum_{{\alpha}\neq 0}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}(c_{\alpha}).

where
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin},\perp\mathrm{St}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin},\perp\mathrm{triv}}
are the subspaces that are perpendicular to the Steinberg character and the trivial character, respectively.

As a corollary, for distributions
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
, by (
1.3.1
), as functions on the Steinberg-Hitchin base, they are orthogonal to the trivial character. Hence the following theorem holds.

Theorem 1.3.5
.

With the above notation, for
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
with
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}\circ\mathrm{LafSec}(z)=z_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{C}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}}
, the following identities hold.

(1)

When
F={\mathbb{C}}
,

z=\mathrm{Laf}\big(\nu_{0}(z_{0})\big);

(2)

When
F={\mathbb{R}}
,

z=\mathrm{Laf}\bigg(\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(\nu_{\alpha}(z_{\alpha})\big)\bigg);

(3)

When
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic,

z=\mathrm{Laf}\bigg({\mathcal{E}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv}}_{0}\big(\nu_{0}(z_{0})\big)+\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(\nu_{\alpha}(z_{\alpha})\big)\bigg).

Nonabelian Fourier kernels

Up to multiplying a smooth and compactly supported function along the determinant factor, one can assume that the conjectural nonabelian Fourier kernels
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
land in
{\mathcal{Z}}
. Moreover, through comparing the (nonstandard) Langlands local gamma factors for diehdral representations and abelian Langlands local gamma factors on maximal tori, one can show that up to constant multiples of the Weil constant depending only on
\rho
,
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}
for any
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}
, where
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}
(up to constant multiples of the Weil constant) are smooth functions on the corresponding maximal tori constructed in subsection
2.2
. Hence Theorem
1.3.4
implies the following corollary.

Corollary 1.3.6
.

The following identities hold.

(1)

When
F={\mathbb{C}}
,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\mathrm{Laf}\big(\nu_{0}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,0})\big);

(2)

When
F={\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\mathrm{Laf}\bigg(\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(\nu_{\alpha}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}})\big)\bigg);

(3)

When
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\mathrm{Laf}\bigg({\mathcal{E}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv}}_{0}\big(\nu_{0}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,0})\big)+\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(\nu_{\alpha}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}})\big)\bigg).

Notice that Theorem
1.3.4
works for distributions supported only on finitely many Bernstein components. Although nonabelian Fourier kernels are supported on the whole Bernstein variety, for the purpose of local
L
-factors, it suffices to examine the action of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on
K
-finite vectors for smooth and compactly supported (or Schwartz if
F
is archimedean) functions. Hence we are reduced to the situation where the distribution lands in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
. For details see subsection
2.4
.

Stable orbital integral of spherical functions

Let
h\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))
be a spherical function with stable orbital integral
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
with the normalization introduced by Frenkel-Langlands-Ngô (
[
FLN10
]
) revisited in subsection
3.2
. It is straightforward to see that
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)
is orthogonal to the Steinberg character, i.e.
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin},\perp\mathrm{St}}
. It turns out that the following theorem holds.

Theorem 1.3.7
.

With the above notation, the following statements hold:

(1)

Let
h_{\alpha}={\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}({\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h))
. Then

(a)

h_{0}=\mathrm{Sat}(h)
;

(b)

h_{\alpha}=0
for
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0,1\}
;

(c)

When
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic,
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin},\perp\mathrm{St}}
.

(2)

{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}h_{0}=\mathrm{Sat}(h)&F={\mathbb{C}}\\
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(h_{0})&F={\mathbb{R}}\\
{\mathcal{E}}^{\perp,\mathrm{St}}_{0}(h_{0})&F\text{ non-archi.}\end{matrix}

Remark 1.3.8
.

The fact that
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)={\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\perp,\mathrm{St}}(h_{0})
and
h_{0}={\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)|_{\mathbf{c}(E^{1}_{0})}
reflects the heuristic that, at least over the local function fields, there should exist a perverse
t
-structure on the stacky quotient
[G/{\mathrm{Ad}}(G)]
, such that
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)
can be realized as the trace of Frobenius of a perverse sheaf on
[G/{\mathrm{Ad}}(G)]
which is the intermediate extension of its restriction to the split locus (
[
BKV22
]
).

Orbital Hankel transform

For a reductive group
G
with
\rho
an
L
-homomorphism of the Langlands dual group of
G
, let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))
be the conjectural
\rho
-Schwartz space (see subsection
2.1
for a description) intertwined by the conjectural
\rho
-Fourier transform
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
. In
[
Ngô20
]
, the second author asks if one plugs the global
\rho
-Schwartz functions into the Arthur-Selberg trace formula, can one establish the functional equation of the global automorphic
L
-functions attached to the pair
(G,\rho)
from the geometric side of the Arthur-Selberg trace formula.
To understand the geometric side of the Arthur-Selberg trace formula for these
\rho
-Schwartz functions, one first need to understand the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{st.orb.}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{st.orb.}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\rho}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{H}}^{\rho}_{\psi}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\rho}(G(F))}

Here the vertical arrows are given by taking the stable orbital integrals, and
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\rho}(G(F))
is defined to be the space of stable orbital integrals of the conjectural
\rho
-Schwartz functions. The lower horizontal arrow is given by the induced transform
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
, which we refer to the
\rho
-orbital Hankel transform
.
A natural question is if one can derive an explicit formula for the operator
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
. Motivated by Theorem
1.3.3
, we are going to study the descent of
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\rho}(G(F))
to the maximal tori when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
. Precisely, we show that the following diagram is commutative and the operator
{\mathcal{H}}_{z}
is well-defined for any
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z}
st. orb.
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
st. orb.
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{z}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(T_{\alpha}(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(T_{\alpha}(F))}

Here
{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
is the space of smooth and compactly supported functions on
G(F)
when
F
is non-archimedean, and is the space of Schwartz algebra consisting functions of rapidly decreasing when
F
is archimedean. The upper (resp. lower) horizontal operator is given by

h\in{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))\mapsto z*h^{\vee}\quad(\text{resp. $h_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{S}}(T_{\alpha}(F))\mapsto z_{\alpha}*h^{\vee}_{\alpha}$})

where
z_{\alpha}={\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}\circ\mathrm{LafSec}(z)
. Specializing to the
\rho
-orbital Hankel transform, it turns out that we have the following commutative diagram

(1.3.6)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\rho}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{H}}^{\rho}_{\psi}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\rho}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T_{\alpha}(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T_{\alpha}(F))}

where the pair
({\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T_{\alpha}(F)),{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}})
is introduced in subsection
2.2
that is of abelian nature.

Finally, based on Theorem
1.3.3
, we derive the following explicit formula for
{\mathcal{H}}_{z}
in turns of the descent datum. We present the formula for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
.

Theorem 1.3.9
.

With the above notation, the following identities hold.

(1)

When
F={\mathbb{C}}
,

{\mathcal{H}}_{z}(f)=\nu_{0}\big(z_{0}*{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(f)^{\vee}\big);

(2)

When
F={\mathbb{R}}
,

{\mathcal{H}}_{z}(f)=\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(z_{\alpha}*({\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(f))^{\vee}\big);

(3)

When
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic,

{\mathcal{H}}_{z}(f)={\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\rm HK}(z_{0},f)+\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(z_{\alpha}*({\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(f))^{\vee}\big)

where

{\mathcal{E}}^{\rm HK}_{0}(z_{0},f)={\mathcal{E}}_{0}\big(z_{0}*({\mathcal{T}}_{0}(f))^{\vee}\big)-\big({\mathcal{H}}_{z}(f)\big)^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}\cdot\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{ell}}.

\big({\mathcal{H}}_{z}(f)\big)^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\mathrm{St}}
is the constant given by the following formula

={\gamma}(z_{0},{\delta}_{B}^{1/2})\langle f,\Theta_{\mathrm{St}}\rangle-\langle\nu_{0}\big(z_{0}*{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(f)^{\vee}\big),\phi_{{\delta}^{1/2}_{B}}-1\rangle.

It would be desirable if the formula above can be used to establish a global Poisson summation formula for
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on the Steinberg-Hitchin base.

1.4.
Notation and conventions

Throughout the paper, fix a local field of residual characteristic not equal to two. In the archimedean case,
F
is either
{\mathbb{R}}
or
{\mathbb{C}}
. When
F
is nonarchimedean, let
{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}
be the ring of integers with maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}_{F}
generated by a fixed uniformizer
\varpi_{F}
, and
{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}/{\mathfrak{p}}_{F}=\kappa_{F}\simeq{\mathbb{F}}_{q_{F}}
is a finite field with
q_{F}
elements.

We normalize the additive invariant measure
\,\mathrm{d}x
on
F
as follows: In the archimedean case
F={\mathbb{R}}
we choose the usual Lebesgue measure. For
F={\mathbb{C}}
, we choose the measure
\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y
where
x
and
y
are real and imaginary parts. When
F
is nonarchimedean, we normalize
\,\mathrm{d}x
so that the ring of integers
{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}
of
F
has volume one.

We fix a nontrivial additive character
\psi
of
F
. There is then a unique additive Haae measure
\,\mathrm{d}x
on
F
, which is autodual with respect to
\psi
in the following sense.
Let
{\mathcal{S}}(F)
be the space of Schwartz-Bruhat functions on
F
. We have the Fourier transform

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}:{\mathcal{S}}(F)\to{\mathcal{S}}(F)

given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(f)(y)=\int_{F}f(x)\psi(xy)\,\mathrm{d}x.

The additive measure
\,\mathrm{d}x
is said to be auto-dual if we have
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}={\rm id}_{{\mathcal{S}}(F)}
.
In the case
F
is nonarchimedean, we assume that
\psi
is trivial on
{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}
but nontrivial on
{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}_{F}=\varpi^{-1}_{F}{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}
then the auto-dual measure
\,\mathrm{d}x
assigns
{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}
the measure one. If
F
is Archimedean, we assume that

\psi(x)=\begin{cases}\exp(2\pi ix),&F={\mathbb{R}};\\
\exp(2\pi i(x+\overline{x})),&F={\mathbb{C}}.\end{cases}

Then the auto-dual measure will be the usual Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
.

The action of
F^{\times}
on
F
induces an action of
F^{\times}
on additive invariant measures given rise to the norm
x\mapsto|x|\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. When
F
is archimedean, we have

|x|=\begin{cases}\text{absolute value of }x,&F={\mathbb{R}}\\
x\overline{x},&F={\mathbb{C}}\end{cases}.

For nonarchimedean local fields
F
, we have
|x|=q_{F}^{-{\mathrm{ord}}_{F}(x)}
where
{\mathrm{ord}}_{F}:F\to{\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{\infty\}
is the valuation on
F
such that
{\mathrm{ord}}_{F}(\varpi_{F})=1
. In both archimedean and nonarchimedean cases, we denote

\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x=|x|^{-1}{\,\mathrm{d}x},

which is a Haar measure on
F^{\times}
. When
F
is non-archimedean, and if
{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}_{F},\,\mathrm{d}x)=1
, then we have
{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F},\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x)=1-q^{-1}_{F}
.

Let
E
be a quadratic étale
F
-algebra. Let
\mathrm{Tr}=\mathrm{Tr}_{E/F}={\mathrm{tr}}
and
\mathrm{Nr}=\mathrm{Nr}_{E/F}=\det
be the trace and norm map, respectively. Let
\eta=\eta_{E}=\eta_{E/F}:F^{\times}\to\{\pm 1\}
be the corresponding quadratic character attached to
E/F
via class field theory. Let
{\lambda}_{E}={\lambda}_{E/F}={\lambda}_{E/F,\psi}
be the Weil constant defined in
[
Wei64
, Thm. 2]
and
[
JL70
, Lem. 1.1]
. Let
\iota=\iota_{E}=\iota_{E/F}
be the nontrivial involution on
E
fixing
F
. Let
\psi_{E}=\psi\circ\mathrm{Tr}_{E/F}
and let
\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x
be the additive Haar measure on
E
that is self-dual with respect to
\psi_{E}
. In particular when
F
is non-archimedean,
{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}_{E},\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x)=|{\mathfrak{D}}_{E/F}|^{1/2}
where
{\mathfrak{D}}_{E/F}
is the discriminant for
E/F
. Let
E^{1}=\ker(\mathrm{Nr}:E^{\times}\to F^{\times})
which fits into a short exact sequence

1\to E^{1}\to E^{\times}\to\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})\to 1.

Equip
\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})
with the restriction of the multiplicative Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x
on
F^{\times}\supset\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})
. Combining with the multiplicative Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}_{E}x={|\mathrm{Nr}(x)|}^{-1}{\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x}
on
E^{\times}
, the induced Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}_{E,1}e
on
E^{1}
is equal to
\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x
if
E/F
is split, and has the property that

(1.4.1)

{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E,1}e)=\frac{{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{E},\,\mathrm{d}_{E})[{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F}:\mathrm{Nr}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{E})]}{{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F},\,\mathrm{d}x)}=\frac{(1-q_{E}^{-1})|{\mathfrak{D}}_{E/F}|^{\frac{1}{2}}e(E\mid F)}{(1-q_{F}^{-1})}

when
E/F
is a field extension and
F
is non-archimedean. Here
e(E\mid F)
is the ramification index of
E/F
.

Let
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
(resp.
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
) be the group scheme of
2\times 2
invertible matrices (resp. matrices with determinant one). Let
\mathbf{c}:G\to{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}=G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}{\mathrm{Ad}}(G)\simeq{\mathbb{G}}_{a}\times{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
(resp.
{\mathbb{G}}_{a}
) be the Steinberg map
[
Ste74
, p87]
sending
g\in G
to
(c,a)=({\mathrm{tr}}(g),\det(g))
(resp.
c={\mathrm{tr}}(g)
). Let
{\mathrm{D}}^{G}
be the norm of the Weyl discriminant of
G
and
{\Delta}
be its square root which can be considered as functions on
\mathfrak{c}_{G}(F)

\Delta(c,a)=|c^{2}-4a|^{1/2}\mbox{ resp }\Delta(c)=|c^{2}-4|^{1/2}.

Haar measures on
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
can be expressed by coordinates given by the triangular decomposition.
For a generic element
g\in{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
lying in the open dense Bruhat cell, we write

(1.4.2)

g=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\quad u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}.

Fix the following measure
\,\mathrm{d}g
on
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
inherited from the decomposition (
1.4.2
)

(1.4.3)

\,\mathrm{d}g=|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v.

By direct calculation, we see that this formula provides a Haar measure on
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
. About this Haar measure,
the following volume calculation will be needed later.

Lemma 1.4.1
.

Let
F
be non-archimedean. Then

{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\mathfrak{o}}_{F}),\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}g)=1-q_{F}^{-2}

Proof.

Write

g=\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}t&tu\\
tv&t^{-1}+tuv\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\det g=1
,
g\in{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\mathfrak{o}}_{F})
if and only if all of its coordinates lie in
{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}
. Equivalently

t\in{\mathfrak{o}}_{F},\quad tu,tv\in{\mathfrak{o}}_{F},\quad 1+t^{2}uv\in t{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}.

When
t\in{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F}
,
u,v\in{\mathfrak{o}}_{F}
, and hence under the measure (
1.4.3
), it provides a total mass
{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F},\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}t)=1-q^{-1}_{F}
. When
t\in{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}_{F}\backslash{\mathfrak{p}}^{k+1}_{F}=\varpi^{k}_{F}{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F}
for some
k\geq 1
,

u,v\in\varpi^{-k}_{F}{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F},\quad\varpi^{2k}_{F}uv\in-1+{\mathfrak{p}}^{k}_{F}

and hence under the measure (
1.4.3
), it provides a total mass

|\varpi_{F}^{k}|^{2}|\varpi^{-k}_{F}|^{2}{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F},\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}t){\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}_{F},\,\mathrm{d}u){\mathrm{vol}}(-1+{\mathfrak{p}}^{k},\,\mathrm{d}v)=(1-q_{F}^{-1})^{2}q_{F}^{-k}.

In conclusion, we have

{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\mathfrak{o}}_{F}),\,\mathrm{d}g)=1-q_{F}^{-1}+(1-q_{F}^{-1})^{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}q_{F}^{-k}=1-q_{F}^{-2},

which is the desired formula.
∎

Similarly, for
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
lying in the open dense Bruhat cell, write

(1.4.4)

g=\begin{pmatrix}a&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix},\quad u,v\in F,\quad a,t\in F^{\times}.

Fix the following measure
\,\mathrm{d}g
on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
inherited from the decomposition (
1.4.4
)

(1.4.5)

\,\mathrm{d}g=|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}a\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v.

By direct calculation the trivial extension of (
1.4.5
) to
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
provides a Haar measure on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
. Based on Lemma
1.4.1
, when
F
is non-archimedean,

(1.4.6)

{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathfrak{o}}_{F}),\,\mathrm{d}g)=(1-q_{F}^{-1})(1-q_{F}^{-2}).

In particular,
\,\mathrm{d}g
coincides with
{|\det x|^{-2}}{\,\mathrm{d}x}
where
\,\mathrm{d}x
is the restriction of the additive Haar measure on the space of
2\times 2
matrices
{\mathrm{M}}_{2\times 2}(F)
normalized by
{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathrm{M}}_{2\times 2}({\mathfrak{o}}_{F}),\,\mathrm{d}g)=1
.

1.5.

We thank Jayce Getz, Hervé Jacquet, Dihua Jiang, Daniel Johnstone, David Kazhdan, Robert Kottwitz, Robert Langlands, Gérard Laumon, Yiannis Sakellaridis, Freydoon Shahidi, Zhiwei Yun, and Don Zagier for stimulating conversations related to this work. Both authors were partially supported by the second author’s Simon Investigator grant. The second author was also partially supported by the NSF grants 1702380 and 2201314.

2.
Local aspects of the Braverman-Kazhdan program

In this section, we review the local aspects of the Braverman-Kazhdan program. In subsection
2.2
, we establish the proposal for the torus case.

2.1.
An abstract of the local proposal

Let
G
be a reductive group over a local field
F
. Following
[
Ngô20
, §4.2]
, we will assume that
G
fits into the following short exact sequence

(2.1.1)

1\to G^{\prime}\to G\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to 1

with
\nu:G\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
a nontrivial homomorphism playing the role of the determinant. Dually there is a homomorphism
\nu^{\vee}:{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\to{}^{L}G
where
{}^{L}G
is the Langlands dual group of
G
. Let us restrict to the case when
\rho:{}^{L}G\to{\mathrm{GL}}(V_{\rho})
is irreducible and
\rho\circ\nu^{\vee}
is the scalar multiplication of
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
on
V_{\rho}
as in
[
Ngô20
, §4.2]
, which can always be achieved by adding an extra
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
factor to
G
. Set

(2.1.2)

L(\pi,\rho)=L(-\frac{n_{\rho}-1}{2},\pi,\rho),\quad n_{\rho}=\langle 2\eta_{G},{\lambda}_{\rho}\rangle+1

where
2\eta_{G}
is the sum of positive roots of
G
and
{\lambda}_{\rho}
is the highest weight of
\rho
.
Here

L(s,\pi,\rho)=L(\pi\otimes|\nu|^{s+\frac{n_{\rho}-1}{2}},\rho)

is the unramified local
L
-factor attached to an unramified representation
\pi
of
G(F)
, which is defined via
L(s,\pi,\rho)=\det\big({\mathrm{Id}}_{V_{\rho}}-\rho(c(\pi))q^{-s}\big)^{-1}
with
c(\pi)
the Satake parameter of
\pi
,

2\eta_{G}
is the sum of positive roots of
G
and
{\lambda}_{\rho}
is the highest weight of
\rho
.

The local aspect of the conjecture of Braverman-Kazhdan can be stated as follows:

Conjecture 2.1.1
.

(1)

There exists a Schwartz space
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))
which contains the space of smooth and compactly supported functions
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G(F))
and is contained in the space of smooth functions
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(G(F))
, such that the zeta integral

(2.1.3)

{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\phi,\varphi_{\pi})=\int_{G(F)}\phi(g)\varphi_{\pi}(g)|\nu(g)|^{s+\frac{n_{\rho}-1}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}g,\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F)),\varphi_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\pi)

is absolutely convergent for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
sufficiently large, with a meromorphic continuation to
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Here
\,\mathrm{d}g
is a fixed Haar measure on
G(F)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(\pi)
is the set of smooth matrix coefficients of an irreducible admissible representation
\pi
of
G(F)
.

The zeta integral
{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\phi,\varphi_{\pi})
is a holomorphic multiple of the Langlands local
L
-factor
L(s,\pi,\rho)
. Moreover, when
F
is non-archimedean, the set

{\mathcal{I}}_{\pi}=\{{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\phi,\varphi_{\pi})\mid\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F)),\varphi_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\pi)\}

is a finitely generated nonzero fractional ideal of
{\mathbb{C}}[q^{s},q^{-s}]
with generator
L(s,\pi,\rho)
. When
F
is archimedean, let
S_{a,b}=\{s\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid a<{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<b\}
be the vertical strip for any
a<b
. If
P(s)
is a polynomial in
s
such that
s\in{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto P(s)L(s,\pi,\rho)
is bounded in the vertical strip
S_{a,b}
, then
s\in{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto P(s){\mathcal{Z}}(s,\phi,\varphi_{\pi})
is also bounded in the same vertical strip
S_{a,b}
;

The Schwartz space
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))
contains a distinguished vector, the
\rho
-basic function
{\mathbb{L}}^{\rho}\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))
, which satisfies the following properties

(a)

\{{\mathcal{Z}}(s,{\mathbb{L}}^{\rho},\varphi_{\pi})\mid\varphi_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\pi)\}
is nonzero only when
\pi
is unramified;

(b)

For
\pi
unramified and
\varphi_{\pi}^{\circ}
the zonal spherical function of
\pi

{\mathcal{Z}}(s,{\mathbb{L}}^{\rho},\varphi_{\pi}^{\circ})=L(s,\pi,\rho).

(2)

There exists an invariant distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on
G(F)
which is locally integrable and smooth over generic locus, such that the following properties hold

(a)

When
F
is non-archimedean, for any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{G_{n}}
lies in the Bernstein center of
G(F)
, where
\mathbbm{1}_{G_{n}}
is the characteristic function of the set
G_{n}=\{g\in G(F)\mid|\nu(g)|=q^{-n}\}
. Let
\gamma_{n}(\pi,\rho,\psi)
be the regular function on the Bernstein variety of
G(F)
defined by
\pi(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n})=\gamma_{n}(\pi,\rho,\psi){\mathrm{Id}}_{\pi}
, then the following Laurent series

s\in{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\sum_{n}\gamma_{n}(\pi\otimes|\nu|^{s},\rho,\psi)

is convergent for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
sufficiently large, with a meromorphic continuation to
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and is equal to the Langlands local gamma factor
{\gamma}(-\frac{n_{\rho}-1}{2}-s,\pi^{\vee},\rho,\psi)
. Here
\pi^{\vee}
is the smooth contragredient of
\pi
;

(b)

When
F
is archimedean, for any delta sequence
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G(F))
tending to the delta mass supported at identity of
G(F)
, the following limit holds

\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}(\pi\otimes|\nu|^{s})(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}*{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}^{\vee})\mapsto{\gamma}(-\frac{n_{\rho}-1}{2}-s,\pi^{\vee},\rho,\psi){\mathrm{Id}}_{\pi}

as long as
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
is sufficiently large depending only on
\pi
;

(3)

The
\rho
-Fourier transform

{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\phi)(g):=|\nu(g)|^{-n_{\rho}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}*\phi^{\vee})(g),\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G(F))

extends to an invertible operator sending
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))
to itself, and enjoys the following properties

(a)

{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi^{-1}}={\mathrm{Id}}
;

(b)

{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
extends to a unitary operator on
L^{2}(G(F),|\nu(g)|^{n_{\rho}}\,\mathrm{d}g)
;

(c)

{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}({\mathbb{L}}^{\rho})={\mathbb{L}}^{\rho}
;

After meromorphic continuation, the following functional equation holds

(2.1.4)

{\mathcal{Z}}(1-s,{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\phi),\varphi_{\pi}^{\vee})={\gamma}(s,\pi,\rho,\psi){\mathcal{Z}}(s,\phi,\varphi_{\pi}),\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F)),\varphi_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{C}}(\pi).

Remark 2.1.2
.

(1)

The Schwartz space is expected to be connected with the reductive monoid
M^{\rho}
attached to the pair
(G,\rho)
. Over an algebraically closed field, the theory of reductive monoid is developed by E. Vinberg, M. Putcha, and L. Renner
(
[
Vin95
]
[
Ren05
]
)
. Over a non-algebraically closed field, an explicit construction of
M^{\rho}
is given in
[
Ngô20
]
. To make it precise, for every
L
-homomorphism
\rho:{}^{L}G\to{\mathrm{GL}}(V_{\rho})
, as shown in
[
Ngô20
, Prop. 5.1]
, one can construct a monoid
M^{\rho}
containing
G
as the open subset of invertible elements. Over a non-archimedean local field of positive characteristic, the basic function
{\mathbb{L}}^{\rho}
can be interpreted as the trace of Frobenius on the sought-for intersection complex of the arc space of
M^{\rho}
as shown in
[
BNS16
]
[
BNS17
]
. In general, it is expected that a basis of the space of
\rho
-Schwartz functions can be constructed by nearby cycles (
[
BBFK23
]
).

(2)

In the short exact sequence (
2.1.1
), if one set
G^{\prime}={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, then for any dominant cocharacter
{\lambda}_{n}=(a\in{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\mapsto a^{n}\in{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
, Vinberg’s theory of universal monoid provides the following explicit description of
M_{\rho}
and
G
fitting into (
2.1.1
):

(a)

\displaystyle M^{{\lambda}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\{(a,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}\times{\mathrm{M}}_{2\times 2}\mid{\lambda}_{n}(a)=\det g\}

\displaystyle G^{{\lambda}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\{(a,g)\in{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\mid{\lambda}_{n}(a)=\det g\};

(b)

G^{{\lambda}_{n}}\simeq\bigg\{\begin{matrix}{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}&n\text{ odd}\\
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}&n\text{ even}\end{matrix}

(c)

When
n
is odd,
{}^{L}G^{{\lambda}_{n}}\simeq{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\rho_{{\lambda}_{n}}:(\det)^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\otimes{\mathrm{Sym}}^{n}:{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\to{\mathrm{GL}}_{n+1}({\mathbb{C}})
; When
n
is even,
{}^{L}G^{{\lambda}_{n}}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\times{\mathrm{SO}}_{3}({\mathbb{C}})
and
\rho_{{\lambda}_{n}}
is the tensor product of the scaling action of
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
and the unique irreducible representation of
{\mathrm{SO}}_{3}({\mathbb{C}})
of dimension
n+1
. The result is known to experts, and explicit calculations can be found in
[
Luo
, Ex. 5.1.7]
.

(3)

For the normalization of the action of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on
\pi
, i.e. the property (3) above, and how is it connected with the functional equation (
2.1.4
), see
[
Luo19
]
and
[
Luo
, (5.2.1)]
for a precise derivation.
Up to unramified twist there is no harm to replace
n_{\rho}
by any complex number. In section
10
and section
9
, we will take
n_{\rho}=0
in order to present a cleaner formula for the orbital Hankel transforms and stable orbital integral of basic functions for
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
and arbitrary
\rho
.

(4)

The conjecture holds for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{n}
over an
F
-central simple algebra and
\rho
the standard representation, which is due to Godement-Jacquet
[
GJ72
]
and
[
Jac09
]

(
Over an archimedean local fields,
[
GJ72
]
only treated
K
-finite vectors in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))
and
{\mathcal{C}}(\pi)
, which was completed in
[
Jac09
]
)
. Based on
[
JLZ22
]
, the conjecture is also known for
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}
,
\rho=\rm std
and
F
is non-archimedean of charateristic zero. Finally, the conjecture is also known when
G
is a torus with mild assumptions on
\rho
, which is announced in
[
Ngô20
, §5.2]
, and the details will be provided in subsection
2.2
.

(5)

When
F
is archimedean, similar as the non-archimedean case, we expect that for any test function
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(F^{\times})
,
(f\circ\nu)\cdot\phi
lies in
{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
, which is the space of Schwartz functions on
G(F)
in the sense of
[
Wal88a
, 7.1.2]
that are rapidly decreasing under the action of any algebraic differential operators on
G(F)
(and also
[
AG08
]
). Moreover,
\big((f\circ\nu)\cdot\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\big)*{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))\subset{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
.

(6)

Following Godement-Jacquet (
[
GJ72
]
), when
F
is archimedean, it suffices to consider
K\times K
-finite functions in
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(G(F))
(and
{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
), which is enough to produce all the local
L
-factors. Here
K
is a fixed maximal compact subgroup of
G(F)
.

(7)

There are conjectures on the construction of the general
\rho
-Fourier kernels
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
proposed in
[
BK00
]
and
[
Ngô20
, §6.2]
, which will be briefly revisited in subsection
2.3
.

2.2.
Torus case

When
G
is a torus, Conjecture
2.1.1
is known for any
\rho
. Here, we follow the exposition in
[
Ngô20
, §5.2,§6.1]
and provide more details.

General setup

First let us consider the case when
T
is a split torus over
F
, and let
\rho:T^{\vee}\to{\mathrm{GL}}(V_{\rho})
be a finite dimensional representation where
T^{\vee}
is the dual torus of
T
over
{\mathbb{C}}
. Suppose that
\rho
decomposes as a direct sum of characters

V_{\rho}=\oplus_{i=1}^{r}{\mathbb{C}}_{\mu_{i}}

where
\mu_{i}\in{\Lambda}={\mathrm{Hom}}({\mathbb{G}}_{m},T)\simeq{\mathrm{Hom}}(T^{\vee},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
are characters of
T^{\vee}
which are not necessarily distinct. Following
[
Ngô20
, Prop. 5.1]
, we assume that the cone
\xi(\rho)\in{\Lambda}_{\mathbb{R}}
generated by
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
is strictly convex. This assumption is implied by the exact sequence (
2.1.1
) and the assumption on the dual action of
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
on the representation
\rho
. Then there is a toric variety
M^{\rho}_{T}
containing
T
as a dense open subset, which is characterized by the property that a homomorphism
{\lambda}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to T
extends to a morphism
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\to M^{\rho}_{T}
if and only if
{\lambda}\in{\Lambda}\cap\xi(\rho)
.

The cocharacters
\{\mu_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
induce a homomorphism of tori
\rho_{T}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{r}\to T
via

(2.2.1)

\rho_{T}(x_{1},...,x_{r})=\mu_{1}(x_{1})...\mu_{r}(x_{r})

Each
\mu_{i}
extends to a homomorphism of monoids
\mu_{i}:{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\to M^{\rho}_{T}
and (
2.2.1
) extends to a homomorphism of monoids
\rho_{M_{T}}:{\mathbb{A}}^{r}\to M^{\rho}_{T}
such that the following commutative diagram is Cartesian

\textstyle{{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{T}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}^{r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{M_{T}}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\rho}_{T}}

For a general torus
T
over
F
, let
{\Lambda}
be the group of cocharacters of
T
over an algebraic closure of
F
, then the Galois group
{\Gamma}_{F}
of
F
acts on
{\Lambda}
through a finite quotient. The Langlands dual group
{}^{L}T
is equal to
=T^{\vee}\rtimes{\Gamma}_{F}
where
T^{\vee}={\mathrm{Hom}}({\Lambda},{\mathbb{C}}^{\times})
. Let
\rho:{}^{L}T\to{\mathrm{GL}}(V_{\rho})
be an
r
-dimensional algebraic representation of
{}^{L}T
satisfying the assumptions in
[
Ngô20
, Prop. 5.1]
. The restriction
\rho|_{T^{\vee}}
is a direct sum of characters, possibly with multiplicities

\rho|_{T^{\vee}}=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}{\lambda}_{1}^{r_{i}}

where
\{{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in{\Lambda}
are distinct characters of
T^{\vee}
and the multiplicities
\sum_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}=r
. The weights
\{{\lambda}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
given with multiplicities
\{r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
determine a finite subset

R_{\rho}=\{({\lambda}_{1},1),...,({\lambda}_{1},r_{1}),...,({\lambda}_{m},1),...,({\lambda}_{m},r_{m})\}

of
{\Lambda}\times{\mathbb{N}}
. Since the representation
\rho|_{T^{\vee}}
extends to
T^{\vee}\rtimes{\Gamma}_{F}
, the subset
R_{\rho}
is invariant under the action of
{\Gamma}_{F}
on
{\Lambda}\times{\mathbb{N}}
. Over an algebraic closure
\overline{F}
, there is a homomorphism
\rho_{T}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{R_{\rho}}\to T_{\overline{F}}
by (
2.2.1
). As this homomorphism is equivariant with respect to the action of
{\Gamma}_{F}
, it can be descended to a homomorphism
\rho_{T}:D^{\rho}\to T
between tori over
F

where
D^{\rho}
is the unique torus defined over
F
satisfying
D^{\rho}\otimes_{F}\overline{F}={\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{R_{\rho}}
and such that the induced action of
{\Gamma}_{F}
on
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{R_{\rho}}
coincides with the one derived from the action of
{\Gamma}_{F}
on
R_{\rho}
. Let
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}
denote the affine space over
F
satisfying
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}\otimes_{F}\overline{F}={\mathbb{A}}^{R_{\rho}}
and such that the induced action of
{\Gamma}_{F}
on
{\mathbb{A}}^{R_{\rho}}
coincide with the action of
{\Gamma}_{F}
on the set of indices
R_{\rho}
. The homomorphism
\rho_{T}
extends to a morphism of monoids
\rho_{M_{T}}:{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}\to M^{\rho}_{T}
such that the following commutative diagram is Cartesian:

(2.2.2)

\textstyle{D^{\rho}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{T}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{M_{T}}}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\rho}_{T}}

\rho
-Schwartz space

We explain the construction of
\rho
-Schwartz space for the pair
(T,\rho)
.

Let
U
be the kernel of
\rho_{T}
and consider the quotient stack
{\mathcal{M}}^{\rho}_{T}=[{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}/U]
. In general, as explained in
[
Ngô20
, §5.2, §6.1]
, the geometry of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\rho}_{T}
gives rise to the space of Schwartz functions and the nonabelian Fourier transforms attached to
\rho
. For the purpose of this paper, let us consider the
F
-points of the diagram (
2.2.2
) and consider the following commutative diagram, which in general constitutes only part of the
F
-point of the stack
{\mathcal{M}}^{\rho}_{T}
:

(2.2.3)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(M^{\rho}_{T}(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(M^{\rho}_{T}(F))}

Here by construction,
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)
is an étale
F
-algebra.
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
is the space of Schwartz-Bruhat functions on
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}
is the standard Fourier transform intertwining the space
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
. The morphism
(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}
is the push-forward map and
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
is the induced nonabelian Fourier kernel. In particular,
{\mathcal{S}}(M^{\rho}_{T}(F))
can be viewed as
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))_{U(F)}
, the
U(F)
-coinvariants of
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
. Not surprisingly, we define
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F)):={\mathcal{S}}(M^{\rho}_{T}(F))
.

Alternatively, for any
t\in T(F)\subset M^{\rho}_{T}(F)
, by the Cartesian diagram (
2.2.2
),
(\rho_{M_{T}})^{-1}(t)=(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)
is a
U(F)
-torsor, which is a closed subset of
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)
. The restriction of a Schwartz-Bruhat function to a closed subset is still a Schwartz function (
[
BZ76
]
[
AG08
]
). Hence

t\in T(F)\mapsto(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\phi)(t)=\int_{x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x,\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))

is absolutely convergent and defines a smooth function on
T(F)
, where
\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x
is the measure on the fiber
(\rho^{-1}_{T})(t)
which is inherited from the
U(F)
-torsor structure. Then
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F)):=\big\{(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\phi)\mid\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}\big({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)\big)\big\}
. Through pulling back the zeta integrals and
\rho
-Schwartz functions to
D^{\rho}(F)\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)
, it is immediate to see that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))
satisfies Part (1) of Conjecture
2.1.1
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

We have the following remarks about
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))={\mathcal{S}}(M^{\rho}_{T}(F))
:

(1)

In general, functions in
{\mathcal{S}}(M^{\rho}_{T}(F))
are only defined on
T(F)
. They may tend to infinity near the boundary
M^{\rho}_{T}(F)\backslash T(F)
;

(2)

As the group homomorphism
\rho_{T}
might not be surjective on
F
-points, when restricted to
T(F)
, the functions in
{\mathcal{S}}(M^{\rho}_{T}(F))
are only supported on
\rho_{T}(D^{\rho}(F))
. Comparing with Part (1) of Conjecture
2.1.1
, we may artificially add all the space of smooth and compactly supported functions
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F))
into
{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))
. But for the purpose of the local theory of zeta integrals for
(T,\rho)
, this is actually not necessary;

(3)

In the following, when discussing test functions on
T(F)
, we restrict ourselves to those functions contained in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\rho_{T}(D^{\rho}(F)))
.

\rho
-Fourier transform

We explain the construction of
\rho
-Fourier transform for the pair
(T,\rho)
.

For any
\Phi\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))
, by definition, there exists
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
such that
\Phi=(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\phi)
. Let us define
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\Phi):=(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi))\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))
.

First let us show that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\Phi)
is independent of the choice of the representative
\phi
. Suppose that there are
\phi_{i}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
,
i=1,2
, such that
\Phi=(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\phi_{i})
. For any character
\chi:T(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, based on the assumption made in (
2.1.1
), the following identity holds

(2.2.4)

{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\Phi,\chi)=\int_{T(F)}\Phi(t)\chi(t)|\nu(t)|^{s}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t=\int_{D^{\rho}(F)}\phi_{i}(x)\big(\chi\circ\rho_{T}\big)(x)|\det(x)|^{s}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}x

where
\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t
and
\,\mathrm{d}^{*}x
are fixed Haar measures on
T(F)
and
D^{\rho}(F)
, respectively, and
n_{\rho}
is taken to be
1
. In particular if the character
\chi
is taken to be unitary, then by Tate’s thesis (
[
Tat50
]
), the above integral is absolutely convergent for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)>1
with a meromorphic continuation to
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and the poles are captured by the local
L
-factor
L(s,\chi\circ\rho_{T})
. After meromorphic continuation, the following functional equation holds,

(2.2.5)

{\gamma}(s,\chi\circ\rho_{T},\psi){\mathcal{Z}}(s,\Phi,\chi)={\mathcal{Z}}(1-s,(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi_{i})\big),\chi^{-1}),\quad i=1,2.

Taking the subtraction of the above identities for
i=1,2
, we get, after meromorphic continuation and changing variables

{\mathcal{Z}}(s,(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2})\big),\chi)=0

for any unitary character
\chi
of
T(F)
. Hence the Mellin transform of the smooth function
(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2})\big)|\nu(\cdot)|^{s}
is identically zero for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
large independent of the unitary character
\chi
. By Pointryagin duality and smoothness,
(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2})\big)=0
identically. Therefore the definition of
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\Phi)
is independent of the choice of
\phi_{i}
and hence is well-defined. In particular, properties (a), (b), (c) and (
2.1.4
) in Part (3) of Conjecture
2.1.1
follows immediately after pulling back the zeta integrals to
D^{\rho}(F)
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

Following Part (2) and (3) of Remark
2.2.1
, we may artificially extend
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F))
via zero extension, or using the
\rho
-Fourier kernel constructed below via convolution. For convenience we still restrict ourselves to test functions supported on
\rho_{T}(D^{\rho}(F))\subset T(F)
.

\rho
-Fourier kernel

It remains to show the existence of the invariant distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
representing
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
as conjectured in Part (3) of Conjecture
2.1.1
and establish the properties listed in Part (2) of Conjecture
2.1.1
. We follow the techniques developed in
[
JL22
]
.

\rho
-Fourier kernel: Non-archimedean case

First let us consider the case when
F
is non-archimedean. Let us write
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}E_{i}
with
E_{i}
an
F
-étale algebra. Let
{\mathfrak{o}}_{E_{i}}
be the ring of integers of
E_{i}
with the maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}
and a fixed uniformizer
\varpi_{i}
. Let
{\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}
which is an ideal of
{\mathfrak{o}}^{\rho}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\mathfrak{o}}_{i}
. Fix an additive character
\psi^{\rho}
of
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)
that is trivial on
{\mathfrak{o}}^{\rho}
but nontrivial on
({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-1}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}^{-1}
. In particular
\psi^{\rho}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\psi_{i}
where
\psi_{i}
is an additive character of
E_{i}
that is trivial on
{\mathfrak{o}}_{i}
but nontrivial on
{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}^{-1}
. Fix the Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}x
on
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)
that is self-dual with respect to
\psi^{\rho}
. Consider the following sequence
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
:

(2.2.6)

{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{{\mathrm{vol}}(K_{n},\,\mathrm{d}x)}\mathbbm{1}_{K_{n}}(x),\quad K_{n}={\mathrm{Id}}_{D^{\rho}(F)}+({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{n}.

Then
\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}={\delta}_{{\mathrm{Id}}_{D^{\rho}(F)}}
which is the delta mass supported at the identity of
D^{\rho}(F)
. By direct calculation,

{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})(x)=\psi^{\rho}(x)\mathbbm{1}_{({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n}}(x).

Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
, the function

(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})\big)

lies in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(T(F))
. Following
[
JL22
, §3]
, we consider the following limit:

\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})\big).

The following lemma is analogous to
[
JL22
, Lem. 3.3.]
.

Lemma 2.2.3
.

For any
t\in T(F)
, the limit

\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})\big)(t)

is stably convergent.

Proof.

For any
n_{1}\geq n_{2}
sufficiently large, consider the subtraction

(2.2.7)

(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n_{1}}-{\mathfrak{c}}_{n_{2}})\big)(t).

In order to establish the lemma, it suffices to show that for fixed
t\in T(F)
, (
2.2.7
) vanishes as long as
n_{1}\geq n_{2}
is sufficiently large.

Following (
2.2.6
), (
2.2.7
) is equal to the following absolutely convergent integral

(2.2.8)

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\\
x\in({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\psi^{\rho}(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x.

If
t\in T(F)
does not lie in the image of
\rho_{T}
, or the kernel
U(F)
is compact, the lemma holds automatically. Hence in the following let us assume that
t\in T(F)
is in the image of
\rho_{T}
and
U(F)
is non-compact.

Set
U_{1}=K_{1}\cap U(F)
, which is an open compact subgroup of
U(F)
. For any
u\in U_{1}
, since
u\in K_{1}
(resp.
\in U(F)
), its left multiplication fixes
({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n}
(resp.
(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)
). It follows that for any
u\in U_{1}
,

(2.2.9)

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\\
x\in({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\psi^{\rho}(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\\
x\in({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\psi^{\rho}(ux)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x.

Integrating both sides of (
2.2.9
) over
U_{1}
with respect to the Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}u
on
U(F)
, we get

(2.2.10)

{\mathrm{vol}}(U_{1},\,\mathrm{d}u)\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\\
x\in({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\psi^{\rho}(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x=\int_{u\in U_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}u\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\\
x\in({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\psi^{\rho}(ux)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x.

Notice that one can switch the integration order on the right hand side above due to the compactness of
U_{1}
:

(2.2.11)

\int_{u\in U_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}u\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\\
x\in({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\psi^{\rho}(ux)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\\
x\in({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x\int_{u\in U_{1}}\psi^{\rho}(ux)\,\mathrm{d}u.

Therefore, to show that (
2.2.8
) (equivalently, the left hand side of (
2.2.10
)) vanishes for
n_{1}\geq n_{2}
sufficiently large, it suffices to establish the following statement:

(2.2.12)

For any
x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)\cap\big(({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\big)
, the integral

\int_{u\in U_{1}}\psi^{\rho}(ux)\,\mathrm{d}u

vanishes.

By
[
BH06
, Prop. 1.8]
, as long as
n_{1}\geq n_{2}
is large, for
x\in\big(({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\big)
,

(2.2.13)

u\in K_{1}\mapsto\psi^{\rho}\big(x(u-1)\big)

is a nontrivial character whose kernel is contained in
K_{n_{1}}
. Since
\{K_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a family of open compact neighborhoods of
{\mathrm{Id}}_{D^{\rho}(F)}
tending to
{\mathrm{Id}}_{D^{\rho}(F)}
,
\{K_{n}\cap U(F)\}_{n\geq 1}
is also a family of open compact neighborhoods of
{\mathrm{Id}}_{D^{\rho}(F)}={\mathrm{Id}}_{U(F)}
contained in
U(F)
tending to identity. Therefore as long as
n_{2}
is sufficiently large, the restriction of the character (
2.2.13
) to the closed subgroup
U_{1}=U(F)\cap K_{1}
is still a nontrivial character since
U(F)
is assumed to be noncompact and
n_{2}
can be arbitrarily large. It follows that for
n_{2}\geq n_{1}
sufficiently large and
x\in\big(({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{1}}\backslash({\mathfrak{p}}^{\rho})^{-n_{2}}\big)
,

\int_{u\in U_{1}}\psi^{\rho}\big(x(u-1)\big)\,\mathrm{d}u=0\quad\text{and hence}\quad\int_{u\in U_{1}}\psi^{\rho}(xu)\,\mathrm{d}u=0.

It follows that the statement (
2.2
) holds and we finish the proof of the lemma.
∎

As a corollary, the stably convergent limit

\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})\big)

defines a smooth function on
T(F)
.

Definition 2.2.4
.

With the above notation, define

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(t)=\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})\big)(t),\quad t\in T(F).

It remains to show that on
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F))\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(\rho_{T}(D^{\rho}(F)))
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
is represented by the smooth function
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
as mentioned in Part (3) of Conjecture
2.1.1
, and enjoys the properties listed in Part (2) of Conjecture
2.1.1
.

To show that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
is represented by
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
, we make the following observation: For any
\Phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F))\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))
, we can assume that
\Phi=(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\phi)
,
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
