. By definition,

{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\Phi)(t)=(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi)\big)(t),\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi)(x)=|\det(x)|^{-1}(\psi^{\rho}*\phi^{\vee})(x).

Since
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
, it is fixed under the translation action by open compact subgroups
\{K_{n}\}_{n\geq n_{1}}
for
n_{1}
large. Therefore

{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi)(x)=|\det(x)|^{-1}\big(\psi^{\rho}*({\mathfrak{c}}_{n_{1}}*\phi^{\vee})\big)(x)=|\det(x)|^{-1}\big((\psi^{\rho}*{\mathfrak{c}}_{n_{1}})*\phi^{\vee}\big)(x).

Since
\rho_{T}:D^{\rho}(F)\to T(F)
is a group homomorphism, by direct calculation, the push-forward map
(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}
respects the convolution of functions and the involution
\phi\mapsto\phi^{\vee}
. Therefore

\displaystyle(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi))(t)=

\displaystyle|\nu(t)|^{-1}\big((\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}((\psi^{\rho}*{\mathfrak{c}}_{n_{1}}))*(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\phi^{\vee})\big)(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\nu(t)|^{-1}\big((\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}((\psi^{\rho}*{\mathfrak{c}}_{n_{1}}))*\Phi^{\vee}\big)(t)

Now let us take the limit
n_{1}\mapsto\infty
. Since
\Phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F))
, we derive the desired identity

(2.2.14)

{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\Phi)(t)=|\nu(t)|^{-1}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}*\Phi^{\vee})(t),\quad t\in T(F).

It follows that the operator
{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}
is represented by
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F))\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))
.

Remark 2.2.5
.

For a general function
\Phi\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\rho}(T(F))
, the operator
\Phi\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\Phi)
can still be represented by the smooth function
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
. But the convolution on the right hand side of (
2.2.14
) need regularization. See
[
JLZ22
]
and
[
JL22
]
for particular cases treated.

Next let us establish the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Let
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}
, where
\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}=\{t\in T(F)\mid|\nu(t)|=q^{-n}\}
. Then
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}
lies in the Bernstein center of
T(F)
.

Proof.

For any
\Phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F))
, since
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
is supported on
\rho_{T}(D^{\rho}(F))
, there is no harm to assume that
\Phi
is also supported on
\rho_{T}(D^{\rho}(F))
, and hence
\Phi=(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\phi)
with
\phi\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
. It suffices to show that

|\nu(t)|^{-1}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}*\Phi^{\vee})(t)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F)).

Up to translation and a finite linear combination, there is no harm to assume that
\Phi
is supported in a maximal open compact subgroup of
T(F)
with
\mathrm{supp}(\Phi)\subset\{t\in T(F)\mid|\nu(t)|=1\}
. By definition,

(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}*\Phi^{\vee})(t)=\int_{T(F)}\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(yt)\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}(yt)\Phi(y)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}y

By the support condition of
\Phi
,
\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}(yt)=\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}(t)
. Therefore

(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}*\Phi^{\vee})(t)=\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}(t)\int_{T(F)}\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(yt)\Phi(y)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}y=\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}(t)(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}*\Phi^{\vee})(t).

By (
2.2.14
), it is equal to

\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}(t)|\nu(t)|{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}(\Phi)(t)=\mathbbm{1}_{T_{n}}(t)|\nu(t)|(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi))(t)

which is equal to

(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\rho}_{n}}|\det|{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi))(t)

where
\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\rho}_{n}}=\{x\in D^{\rho}(F)\mid|\det x|=q^{-n}\}
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi)\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))
,

\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\rho}_{n}}|\det|{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F))

and hence

(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}(\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\rho}_{n}}|\det|{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}(\phi))\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T(F)).

It follows that
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}
lies in the Bernstein center of
T(F)
.
∎

It follows that there exists a regular function
{\gamma}_{n}
on the admissible dual of
T(F)
, such that for any character
\chi
of
T(F)
,

\chi(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n})={\gamma}_{n}(\chi,\rho,\psi).

It remains to show that

s\in{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\sum_{n}{\gamma}_{n}(\chi\otimes|\nu|^{s},\rho,\psi)

is convergent for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
large, with a meromorphic continuation to
{\mathbb{C}}
, and is equal to
{\gamma}(-s,\chi^{-1},\rho,\psi)
. But it follows directly from the functional equation (
2.2.5
) and the same calculation as
[
JL22
, Thm. 3.6]
. To make it precise, choose a family of open compact subgroups
K_{T,k}
of
T(F)
tending to identity and let
{\mathfrak{c}}_{T,k}
be the normalized characteristic function of
K_{T,k}
. By the functional equation (
2.2.5
),

{\gamma}(s,\chi\circ\rho_{T},\psi){\mathcal{Z}}(s,{\mathfrak{c}}_{T,k},\chi)={\mathcal{Z}}(1-s,{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{T,k}),\chi^{-1}).

Notice that since
{\mathfrak{c}}_{T,n}
is smooth and compactly supported, and
\chi
is fixed,

{\mathcal{Z}}(s,{\mathfrak{c}}_{T,k},\chi)=1

for any
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
as long as
k
is bigger than the level/depth of
\chi
. Therefore for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
sufficiently small, the zeta integral defining
{\mathcal{Z}}(1-s,{\mathcal{F}}^{\rho}_{\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{T,k}),\chi^{-1})
is absolutely convergent and is equal to
{\gamma}(s,\chi\circ\rho_{T},\psi)
. Equivalently, for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
sufficiently large, the zeta integral defining
{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}*{\mathfrak{c}}^{\vee}_{T,k},\chi)
is absolutely convergent and

{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}*{\mathfrak{c}}^{\vee}_{T,k},\chi)={\gamma}(-s,\chi^{-1},\rho,\psi).

Therefore

{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}*{\mathfrak{c}}^{\vee}_{T,k},\chi)=\sum_{n}{\mathcal{Z}}(s,\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,n}*{\mathfrak{c}}^{\vee}_{T,k},\chi)=\sum_{n}{\gamma}_{n}(\chi\otimes|\nu|^{s},\rho,\psi)

is convergent for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)
large and is equal to
{\gamma}(-s,\chi^{-1},\rho,\psi)
.

It follows that we have established all the desired properties listed in Conjecture
2.1.1
for
T
a torus over a non-archimedean local field.

\rho
-Fourier kernel: Archimedean case

Now let us assume that
F
is archimedean. Following the same idea as the non-archimedean case, let us fix a delta sequence
\{{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
tending to the delta mass supported at the identity element of
D^{\rho}(F)
. Consider the limit

\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})\big)(t),\quad t\in T(F).

Following the argument of
[
JL22
, Prop. 4.3]
, we are going to show that the limit (and all of its derivatives in
t
) is uniformly convergent for
t
lying in a compact subset, from which we deduce that the limit converges to a smooth function on
T(F)
.

Take the Casimir element
{\Delta}_{\mathrm{cas}}
of
D^{\rho}(F)
. By
[
BK14
, Lem. 3.7]
, for any integer
m
with
2m>\dim D^{\rho}(F)
, there exists a function
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{2m-\dim D^{\rho}(F)-1}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
and
f_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
, such that

(2.2.15)

{\Delta}_{\mathrm{cas}}^{m}*f_{1}+f_{2}={\delta}_{{\mathrm{Id}}_{D^{\rho}(F)}}.

Let
L
be the translation action of
T(F)
on a space of functions on
T(F)
. Then

L({\Delta}_{\mathrm{cas}}^{m}*f_{1}+f_{2})\text{ is equal to the identity operator}.

Therefore

(2.2.16)

(\rho_{M_{T}})_{!}({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n}))(t)=\int_{x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)}{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})(x)L({\Delta}_{\mathrm{cas}}^{m}*f_{1}+f_{2})(\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\rho}})(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x

where
\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\rho}}
is the identity function (which can also be viewed as the trivial character) on
D^{\rho}(F)
. Now the Carsimir element act on
\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\rho}}
via a scalar. Hence up to constant, (
2.2.16
) is equal to the sum of the following two integrals

\int_{x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)}{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})(x)f_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x,\quad i=1,2.

Now by absolutely convergence and the compactly supportedness of
f_{i}
,

\displaystyle\int_{x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)}{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho},\psi}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})(x)f_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x=

\displaystyle\int_{x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)}f_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x\int_{y\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)}\psi(xy){\mathfrak{c}}_{n}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(2.2.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{y\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rho}(F)}{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)}f_{i}(x)\psi(xy)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x

which, as
n\mapsto\infty
, tends to the absolutely convergent integral

\int_{x\in(\rho_{T})^{-1}(t)}f_{i}(x)\psi(xy)\,\mathrm{d}_{t}x.

As
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{2m-\dim D^{\rho}(F)-1}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
and
f_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(D^{\rho}(F))
, and
m
can be arbitrary large, we deduce that the limit (
2.2.17
) is uniformly convergent on compact subset and converges to a smooth function on
T(F)
, which is still denoted as
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
. Finally for Part (2)(b) of Conjecture
2.1.1
, it follows immediately from the functional equation after plugging one side a family of delta sequence. The proof is the same as
[
JL22
, Thm. 4.6]
and we omit.

It follows that we have established all the desired properties listed in Conjecture
2.1.1
for
T
a torus over an archimedean local field.

Theorem 2.2.7
.

Conjecture
2.1.1
holds for
G=T
a torus over any local fields.

2.3.
Low symmetric power examples

In subsection
2.2
, the Conjecture
2.1.1
is fully established when
G
is a torus. The next question is to construct
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
for
G
a general reductive group over a local field
F
with a fixed
L
-homomorphism
\rho:{}^{L}G\to{\mathrm{GL}}(V_{\rho})
.

Let
\mathbf{c}_{G}:G\to G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}{\mathrm{Ad}}(G)
be the Chevalley quotient map. For convenience let us assume that
G
is split, semisimple and simply connected. We fix a maximal split torus
T
and let
W=W(G,T)
be the Weyl group attached to
T
. Following
[
Ste74
]
,
{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}=G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}{\mathrm{Ad}}(G)\simeq T\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W
is an affine space. By restricting
{}^{L}G
to the diagonal torus
{}^{L}T
, we obtain a homomorphism
\rho_{T}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\dim V_{\rho}}\to T
. The idea of Braverman and Kazhdan (
[
BK00
]
) is that one should be able to define a (possibly birational)
W
-action on
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\dim V_{\rho}}
such that the
W
-action on
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\dim V_{\rho}}
preserves the trace function
{\mathrm{tr}}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\dim V_{\rho}}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
, acts via sign character
{\mathrm{sgn}}:W\to\{\pm 1\}
on the standard volume form on
{\mathbb{A}}_{n}^{\dim V_{\rho}}
, and makes
\rho_{T}
a
W
-equivariant morphism. If such a
W
-action is ensured, there is an induced morphism
\rho_{T\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\dim V_{\rho}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W\to T\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W
. Through fiber product there is a morphism
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\dim V_{\rho}}\times_{T\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W}G\to G
and one can push-forward the datum on
({\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{\dim V_{\rho}}\times_{T\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W}G)(F)
to
G(F)
to get the desired distribution. We are not able to find the
W
-action that Braverman and Kazhdan stipulated in general but only in some particular cases.

Instead, we will pursue an idea speculated in
[
Ngô20
, §6]
. It is expected that the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
should be glued from the family of smooth functions
\{\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,T}\}_{T}
, where
T\subset G
runs over the stable conjugacy classes of maximal tori of
G
and
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,T}
is the Fourier kernel constructed in subsection
2.2
for the pair
(T,\rho)
. The first naive idea is to patch the functions
\{\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,T}\}_{T}
directly to obtain the desired function
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
, as this works in the Godement-Jacquet case for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{n}
and
\rho
the standard representation of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{n}
(
[
GJ72
]
).

In the following, for convenience, let us consider
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
over a non-archimedean local field of odd residual characteristic. Actually based on Part (2) of Remark
2.1.2
we shall take
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, but for introductory purpose let us stick to
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
. There are four stable conjugacy classes of one-dimensional tori
T_{\alpha}
with
T_{\alpha}(F)=E_{\alpha}^{1}
for
{\alpha}\in\{0,1,\pm 1/2\}
:
E_{\alpha}
are the étale quadratic
F
-algebras with
E_{0}=F\times F
the split extension,
E_{1}
the unramified extension,
E_{\pm 1/2}
the two nonisomorphic ramified quadratic extensions of
F
, and
E^{1}_{\alpha}
is the subgroup of elements of norm one. When
{\alpha}\neq 0
,
T_{\alpha}(F)
are compact commutative groups. For each
{\alpha}
, we denote by
\pi_{\alpha}:T_{\alpha}(F)\to{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
given by the trace map, and
{\mathrm{tr}}(E_{\alpha}^{1})
the image of
\pi_{\alpha}
. We have an almost partition of
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)=F
as

{\mathfrak{c}}(F)=F=\bigcup_{{\alpha}\in\{0,1,\pm 1/2\}}{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})

with
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})\cap{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1}_{\beta})=\{\pm 2\}
if
{\alpha}\neq{\beta}
. We denote by
\tau_{\alpha}
the involution of
T_{\alpha}
corresponding to the Galois involution of
E_{\alpha}/F
. Every
\tau_{\alpha}
-invariant smooth function
J_{\alpha}
on
T_{\alpha}(F)
descends to a unique function
\nu_{\alpha}(J_{\alpha})
on
{\mathrm{tr}}(E_{\alpha}^{1})
:

(2.3.1)

J_{\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}^{*}(\nu_{\alpha}(J_{\alpha})).

The naive guest would be
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}=\sum_{\in\{0,1,\pm 1/2\}}\nu_{\alpha}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\alpha})
where
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,T_{\alpha}}
is constructed in subsection
2.2
. It turns out that
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
conincides with the Godement-Jacquet kernel based on the fact that
\mathrm{J}^{\rho=\rm std}_{\psi}=\psi({\mathrm{tr}}(\cdot))
and
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}=\psi({\mathrm{tr}}_{E_{\alpha}/F}(\cdot))
.

But this naive approach does not work in other cases, as was shown by Lafforgue in
[
Laf14
]
. He showed that in general, the naive patching construction fails to yield the correct gamma factor, even for principal series representations. He further gave an integral transform that, when applied to the naive patching function
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
, gives rise to the correct Langlands local gamma factors for principal series representations. His transform

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\mathrm{Laf}}:=\mathrm{Laf}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\mathrm{Ngo}})

is given by the following identity

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\mathrm{Laf}}={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(|.|{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\mathrm{Ngo}}))

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}
is the Fourier transform in the trace variable, and
|.|
is the absolute value of the dual variable. In
[
Laf14
]
, Lafforgue claimed without proof that, disregarding potential convergence issue in its definition,
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\mathrm{Laf}}
would act on principal series representations with the correct Langlands local gamma factors. The next question is whether the Lafforgue recipe applied to the naive patching function gives rise to the correct
\gamma
factor for supercuspidal representations. The answer is still negative in general, but as we will see, the Lafforgue integral transform is still an essential device for formulating the correct construction.

In the following, we discuss three explicit examples related to the conjectural construction of the second-named author and the correction provided by Lafforgue.

{\mathrm{I}}

The first example we discuss is
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
and
\rho
the standard representation. We have seen from the above discussion that
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}=\psi({\mathrm{tr}}(\cdot))
. On the other hand, since
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\psi(\cdot))={\delta}_{1}
, the delta mass supported at
x=1
. It follows that in this case,
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Laf}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
.

{\mathrm{I}}{\mathrm{I}}

The second example we discuss is
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
\rho={\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}
, i.e.
{}^{L}G={\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\times{\mathrm{SO}}_{3}
and
\rho
is the standard embedding of
{\mathrm{SO}}_{3}
into
{\mathrm{GL}}_{3}
together with the central scaling action of
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. This is the case treated in
[
JLZ22
]
(based on
[
GPSR87
]
).

Based on
[
JLZ22
, (1.9)]
, up to unramified twist on the determinant factor,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(a,{\mathrm{tr}}(g))=\eta_{\psi}(a(2+{\mathrm{tr}}(g)))

where
\eta_{\psi}
is a smooth function on
F^{\times}
with the property that for any character
\chi
of
F^{\times}
, the following regularized Mellin transform holds as meromorphic function of
s\in{\mathbb{C}}

\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\chi^{-1}(x)|x|^{-s}\eta_{\psi}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x={\gamma}(s+1,\chi,\psi){\gamma}(s+2,\chi^{2},\psi)

where
{\gamma}(s,\chi,\psi)
is the local Tate gamma factor (
[
Tat50
]
). For the definition of the regularization, see
[
JLZ22
, §4.3]
for details. Equivalently,
\eta_{\psi}
is the (multiplicative) convolution of the following two distributions on
F^{\times}
:

(2.3.2)

\eta_{\psi}=\Phi_{1,\psi}*\Phi_{2,\psi}

where
\Phi_{1,\psi}=\psi(\cdot)
and
\Phi_{2,\psi}={\lambda}_{!}(\psi(\cdot)|\cdot|^{-1})(\cdot)
where
{\lambda}:F^{\times}\to F^{\times}
is the map
x\mapsto x^{2}
and
{\lambda}_{!}
is the push-forward of
\psi(\cdot)|\cdot|^{-1}
along
{\lambda}
. Formally, we can also write
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
by the following integral formula

(2.3.3)

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(a,{\mathrm{tr}}(g))=\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\psi\bigg(z+\frac{a(2+{\mathrm{tr}}(g))}{z^{2}}\bigg)|z|^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}z.

In the following, let us compute
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
,
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Laf}
and compare them with
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
.

We first compute
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
. We follow the presentation in subsection
2.2
. The restriction of
\rho
to the diagonal torus of
{}^{L}G
is given by

\rho|_{T^{\vee}}:(a,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}t&&\\
&1&\\
&&t^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right))\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}at&&\\
&a&\\
&&at^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Passing to the dual side, we obtain the following homomorphisms

(2.3.4)

For any étale quadratic
F
-algebra
E
, let
{\mathbb{G}}_{m,E}={\mathrm{Res}}_{E/F}{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
and
{\mathbb{G}}_{m,E}^{1}={\mathrm{Ker}}(\mathrm{Nr}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m,E}\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
. Let
T_{E}={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{G}}_{m,E}^{1}
be the maximal torus in
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
determined by
E
, then the homomorphism dual to
\rho|_{T^{\vee}}
attached to
E
is given by

\displaystyle\rho_{E}:{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{G}}_{m,E}

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathbb{G}}_{m,E}^{1}

\displaystyle(d,e)

\displaystyle\mapsto(d\mathrm{Nr}(e),e/\tau_{E}(e)).

It is worth pointing out that in this situation,
\ker\rho_{E}
is always isomorphic to
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
.

Passing to
F
-points, for
(a,g)\in F\times E^{1}=T_{E}(F)
, we are going to push-forward the standard Fourier kernel
\psi(d+{\mathrm{tr}}_{E/F}(e))
on
F\times E
along the fiber
\rho^{-1}_{E}(a,g)
:

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,E}(a,g)=\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\mathrm{Nr}(e)=a\\
e/\tau_{E}(e)=g\\
d\in F^{\times},e\in E^{\times}\end{subarray}}\psi(d+{\mathrm{tr}}_{E/F}(e))(\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}d\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}e)_{(a,g)}.

The integral need regularization. Before discussing the regularization let us simplify the algebraic equations for the fiber
\rho^{-1}_{E}(a,g)
given by the equations

(2.3.5)

d\mathrm{Nr}(e)=a,e/\tau_{E}(e)=g,\quad d\in F^{\times},e\in E^{\times}.

Choose
e_{g}\in E^{\times}
such that
e_{x}/\tau_{E}(e_{g})=g
. Then all other solutions to the equation
e/\tau_{E}(e)=x
are given by

e=ze_{g}
for
z\in F^{\times}
. It follows that (
2.3.5
) can be reformulated as

dz^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(e_{g})=a,\quad z\in F^{\times},d\in F^{\times}

and we are pushing forward
\psi(d+{\mathrm{tr}}_{E/F}(e))=\psi(\frac{a}{z^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(e_{g})}+z{\mathrm{tr}}_{E/F}(e_{g}))
along
z\in F^{\times}
. The measure on
z\in F^{\times}
used is the multiplicative Haar measure. Changing variable
z\mapsto z/{\mathrm{tr}}(e_{g})
, and using the identity
{\mathrm{tr}}(e_{g})^{2}/\mathrm{Nr}(e_{g})=2+{\mathrm{tr}}(g)
, we deduce the following fact:

(2.3.6)

The following identity holds for any étale quadratic
F
-algebra
E
and
(a,g)\in F^{\times}\times E^{1}
,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,E}(a,g)=\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{z\in F^{\times}}\psi\bigg(z+\frac{a(2+{\mathrm{tr}}(g))}{z^{2}}\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}z.

The regularization is understood as follows:

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,E}(a,g)=\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}(a(2+{\mathrm{tr}}(g)))

where
\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}
is the smooth function on
F^{\times}
whose regularized Mellin transform enjoys the following identity in the sense of
[
JLZ22
, §4.3]

\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\chi^{-1}(x)|x|^{-s}\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x={\gamma}(s+1,\chi,\psi){\gamma}(s+1,\chi^{2},\psi).

Equivalently,
\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{1,\psi}*\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}
where
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1,\psi}=\psi(\cdot)
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}={\lambda}_{!}(\psi(\cdot))
where
{\lambda}:F^{\times}\to F^{\times}
is given by
x\mapsto x^{2}
. As a result

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}(a,g)=\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\psi\bigg(z+\frac{a(2+{\mathrm{tr}}(g))}{z^{2}}\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}z,\quad(a,g)\in G(F).

Comparing with (
2.3.3
), we see that
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\neq\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
.

Next, we are going to compute
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Laf}
based on the above explicit formula for
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
. Following Part (2) of Remark
2.1.2
, instead of performing the Lafforgue transform on the trace variable of
G
, we would like to embed
G
into the reductive monoid
M^{{\lambda}_{2}}
and perform the Lafforgue transform on the trace variable of
M^{{\lambda}_{2}}
, which is suggested by Lafforgue in
[
Laf14
]
. Precisely,
M^{{\lambda}_{2}}=\{(a,x)\in{\mathbb{A}}\times{\mathrm{M}}_{2\times 2}\mid a^{2}=\det x\}
and
G
embeds into
M^{{\lambda}_{2}}
via

(a,g)\in G\mapsto(a,ag).

The analogue of the Chevalley quotient map is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}:M^{{\lambda}_{2}}

\displaystyle\to{\mathbb{A}}^{2}

\displaystyle(a,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto(a,{\mathrm{tr}}(x))

As a result, we have the following fact:

(2.3.7)

With the above notation, for
(a,x)\in M^{{\lambda}_{2}}(F)
,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(a,x)=\eta_{\psi}(2a+{\mathrm{tr}}(x)),\quad\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}(a,x)=\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}(2a+{\mathrm{tr}}(x)).

After written the above kernel functions in
(a,t={\mathrm{tr}}(x))
-variable,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(a,t)=\eta_{\psi}(2a+t),\quad\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}(a,t)=\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}(2a+t).

Now we calculate the Fourier inversion of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
and
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
in trace variable, as distributions on the affine line. By definition,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo})(a,\xi)=\psi(2a\xi){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi})(\xi),\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})(\xi)(a,\xi)=\psi(2a\xi){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\eta_{\psi})(\xi).

Hence to show that
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Laf}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
, it suffices to establish the following identity:

(2.3.8)

|\xi|{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\eta_{\psi})(\xi)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi})(\xi).

We first calculate
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi})
. Following (
2.3.6
), write
\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}
as the multiplicative convolution
\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{1,\psi}*\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}
with
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1,\psi}=\psi(\cdot)
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}={\lambda}_{!}(\psi(\cdot))
.
In general, for a Schwartz-Bruhat function
f\in{\mathcal{S}}(F)
, its Fourier transform can be written as the following multiplicative convolution

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(f)(t)=\int_{y\in F}\psi(ty)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\psi*(|\cdot|f)^{\vee}(t).

It follows that as distributions,

\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{1,\psi}*\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(|\cdot|^{-1}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}^{\vee})
. Hence

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi})(f)=\int_{t\in F}\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi}(t){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(f)(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle\int_{t\in F}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(|\cdot|^{-1}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}^{\vee})(t){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(f)(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t\in F}\big(|\cdot|^{-1}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}^{\vee}\big)(t)f(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

In other words,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\widetilde{\eta}_{\psi})=|\cdot|^{-1}\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}_{2,\psi}
. Similarly,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\eta_{\psi})=|\cdot|^{-1}\Phi^{\vee}_{2,\psi}
. Hence, to derive (
2.3.8
), it is equivalent to show the following identity

|\xi|\Phi^{\vee}_{2,\psi}(\xi)=\widetilde{\Phi}^{\vee}_{2,\psi}(\xi)

which follows directly from the definition of
\Phi_{2,\psi}
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2,\psi}
.

Remark 2.3.1
.

Unfortunately the remedy of Lafforgue still fails in general, which will be shown in the next example. Before preceding, let us explain why the remedy of Lafforgue works for
(G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2},\rho=\rm std)
and
(G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2},\rho={\mathrm{Sym}}^{2})
intuitively.

Precisely, for the above two situations, the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
enjoys the following two characterizations:

(2.3.9)

The distributions
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo})
are always supported on
(F^{\times})^{2}\subset F
;

(2.3.10)

From
[
JL70
, Thm. 4.7]
, for any étale quadratic
F
-algebra
E
, the following identity for the standard local Langlands gamma factors hold

{\gamma}(s,{\mathrm{Ind}}^{W_{F}}_{W_{E}}\chi,\psi)={\lambda}_{E}\cdot{\gamma}(s,\chi,\psi_{E})

for any character
\chi
of
T_{E}(F)
. Here
{\mathrm{Ind}}^{W_{E}}_{W_{F}}\chi
is the automorphic induction from
F
to
E
of the character
\chi
, and
{\lambda}_{E}=\varepsilon(\frac{1}{2},\eta_{E},\psi)
is the Weil constant attached to
E/F
.

It turns out that based on Theorem
6.1.1
that we are going to establish later, for any character
\chi
of
T_{E}(F)
with automorphic induction
\pi_{\chi}
as a representation of
G(F)
, up to unramified twist and potential convergence issue the following identity holds

{\gamma}(\pi_{\chi},\rho,\psi)={\lambda}_{E}\cdot\int_{T_{E}(F)}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})\frac{\eta_{E}(\cdot)}{|\cdot|}\bigg)\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}e

When
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})
is supported on
(F^{\times})^{2}\subset F
,
\eta_{E}
is equal to
1
identically. Hence the right hand side above can be reduced to

{\lambda}_{E}\cdot\int_{T_{E}(F)}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})}{|\cdot|}\bigg)\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}e.

Furthermore, by the difference between the Langlands local gamma factors attached to
\pi_{\chi}
and
\chi
, the above identity reduces to

{\gamma}(\chi,\rho,\psi)=\int_{T_{E}(F)}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})}{|\cdot|}\bigg)\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}e.

which indicates that

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})}{|\cdot|}\bigg)=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}\Longleftrightarrow\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(|\cdot|{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo})).

In conclusion, the fact that the remedy of Lafforgue works for the above two cases crucially relies on (
2.3.9
) and (
2.3.10
). However, in general as shown in the next example, there do exist nonabelian Fourier kernels whose Fourier inversions in trace variable are not supported on
(F^{\times})^{2}\subset F
, and the discrepancy between the Langlands local gamma factors on
T_{E}(F)
and their automorphic induction on
G(F)
differs more than just the constant
{\lambda}_{E}
. Hence a more sophisticated inversion formula is needed.

{\mathrm{I}}{\mathrm{I}}{\mathrm{I}}

The third example we are going to discuss is
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}\times_{\det}{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\mid g_{1},g_{2}\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2},\det g_{1}=\det g_{2}\}
and
\rho=\otimes
is the tensor lifting. The integral representation for the corresponding
L
-functions can be obtained via a slight variant of the doubling method (
[
GPSR87
]
). Following the same argument as
[
JLZ22
]
, one can show that the non-abelian Fourier kernel in this situation is given by

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(g_{1},g_{2})=\overline{\eta}_{\psi}({\mathrm{tr}}(g_{1})+{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{2}))

with
\overline{\eta}_{\psi}
the smooth function on
F^{\times}
whose regularized Mellin transform is given by

\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\chi^{-1}(x)|x|^{-s}\overline{\eta}_{\psi}(x)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}x={\gamma}(s+1,\chi,\psi){\gamma}(s+3,\chi,\psi).

Formally, we can also write
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
as follows

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}(g_{1},g_{2})=\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\psi\bigg(z+\frac{{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{1})+{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{2})}{z}\bigg)|z|^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}z.

The analytical discussions for this example is the same as
\rho={\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}
case and hence we only briefly explain the ideas below without diving into the details. Following the same argument for
\rho={\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}
, the Fourier inversion of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
in
{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{1})
and
{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{2})
two variables is given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi})(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\int_{\xi_{1}z=\xi_{2}z=1}\psi(z)|z|^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}z

which is
not
supported on the square class. As a result,
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\neq\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Laf}
and hence Lafforgue’s modification breaks down for this example. Precisely, suppose that Lafforgue’s modification works for this case, then we would have

\mathrm{Laf}^{\prime}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo})=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}

and hence

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}(g_{1},g_{2})=\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\psi\bigg(z+\frac{{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{1})+{\mathrm{tr}}(g_{2})}{z}\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}z.

However, by Theorem
6.1.1
and (
2.3.10
), for
E_{i}

(i=1,2)
two étale quadratic
F
-algebras,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,E_{1}\times E_{2}}(e_{1},e_{2})=\int^{\mathrm{pv}}_{F^{\times}}\psi\bigg(z+\frac{{\mathrm{tr}}(e_{1})+{\mathrm{tr}}(e_{2})}{z}\bigg)\eta_{1}(z)\eta_{2}(z)\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}z

where
\eta_{i}=\eta_{E_{i}}
, which is clearly not the same as the above formula.

Remark 2.3.2
.

Based on the above example, we see that neither the naive construction
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Ngo}
nor its Lafforgue transform
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\rm Laf}
provides the correct nonabelian Fourier kernel in general. In this paper, we are going to construct the correct nonabelian Fourier kernel for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
from the abelian pieces from tori
\nu_{\alpha}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,\alpha})
using the Langlands’ stable transfer factor and the Lafforgue transform.

2.4.
An explicit formula

Let
F
be a local field of residual characteristic not equal to two. In this subsection, following Part (2) of Remark
2.1.2
, we are going to take
(G,\rho)
to be the following cases

•

G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, and
\rho=\rho_{2n}
the tensor product of the scaling action of
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
and the unique irreducible representation of
{\mathrm{SO}}_{3}({\mathbb{C}})
of dimension
2n+1
;

•

G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, and
\rho=\rho_{2n+1}=(\det)^{-n}\otimes{\mathrm{Sym}}^{2n+1}
.

Our goal is to provide an explicit integral formula for
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
such that

(2.4.1)

\pi(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\circ\mathbf{c})={\gamma}(1/2,\pi^{\vee},\rho,\psi){\mathrm{Id}}_{\pi},\quad\pi\in\mathrm{Irr}(G(F)).

In particular we take
n_{\rho}=0
in Conjecture
2.1.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}
be the following set parametrizing stable conjugacy classes of maximal tori
\{T_{\alpha}\}_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}
in
G(F)

(2.4.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}\{0\}&F={\mathbb{C}}\\
\{0,1\}&F={\mathbb{R}}\\
\{0,1,\pm 1/2\}&F\text{ non. archi.}\end{matrix}

Here
T_{0}
corresponds to the split quadratic
F
-algebra
E_{0}=F\times F
,
T_{1}
corresponds to the unramified quadratic extension (resp. complex extension) when
F
is non-archimedean (resp. real), and
T_{\pm 1/2}
correspond to the two ramified extensions of
F
.

Now we are ready to state our explicit formula for
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
.

Theorem 2.4.1
.

With the above notation, the following identities hold.

(1)

When
F={\mathbb{C}}
,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\mathrm{Laf}\big(\nu_{0}(\widetilde{\mathrm{J}}^{\rho}_{\psi,0})\big);

(2)

When
F={\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\mathrm{Laf}\bigg(\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(\nu_{\alpha}(\widetilde{\mathrm{J}}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}})\big)\bigg);

(3)

When
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic,

\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}=\mathrm{Laf}\bigg({\mathcal{E}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv}}_{0}(\nu_{0}(\widetilde{\mathrm{J}}^{\rho}_{\psi,0}))+\sum_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\big(\nu_{\alpha}(\widetilde{\mathrm{J}}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}})\big)\bigg).

We are going to explain the theorem below.

(1) The transform
\mathrm{Laf}
, as discussed in subsection
2.3
, is defined as follows: For a function
z
on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
,

\mathrm{Laf}(z)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(|\cdot|{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(z)\big)

where the Fourier transform and absolute value are taken in trace variable. From the discussion in subsection
5.1
,
\mathrm{Laf}
realizes the natural map from (a subspace of) the stable cocenter of
G(F)
to its stable Bernstein center;

(2)

\widetilde{\mathrm{J}}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}\cdot\kappa^{\rho}_{\alpha},,\quad\kappa^{\rho}_{\alpha}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}{\lambda}_{{\alpha}}^{n}&G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2},\rho=\rho_{2n}\\
{\lambda}_{\alpha}^{n+1}&G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2},\rho=\rho_{2n+1}\end{matrix}

where
{\lambda}_{\alpha}={\lambda}_{E_{\alpha}/F}
is the Weil constant attached to
E_{\alpha}/F
(
[
JL70
]
), and
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}
is the smooth function on
T_{\alpha}(F)
studied in subsection
2.2
with the property that for any character
\chi_{\alpha}
of
T_{\alpha}(F)
,

\chi_{\alpha}(\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}})={\gamma}(1/2,\chi^{-1}_{\alpha},\rho|_{{}^{L}T_{\alpha}},\psi).

(3) In particular,

\chi_{\alpha}(\widetilde{\mathrm{J}}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}})={\gamma}(1/2,{\mathcal{W}}(\chi^{-1}_{\alpha}),\rho,\psi)

where
{\mathcal{W}}(\chi_{\alpha})
is the dihedral lifting of
\chi_{\alpha}
that is going to be reviewed in subsection
4.2
.

To be precise, since the residual characteristic of
F
is not equal to two, every irreducible admissible representation of
G(F)
is a subquotient of some dihedral representation of
G(F)
(
[
JL70
]
[
Cas72
]
). For any character
\chi
of a maximal torus
T(F)
attached to a quadratic
F
-algebra
E
, let
{\mathcal{W}}(\chi)
be the dihedral lifting of
\chi
to
G(F)
that is going to be reviewed in subsection
4.2
. From the local Langlands correspondence for
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
(
[
BH06
]
)
{\mathcal{W}}(\chi)
has local
L
-parameter given by
{\mathrm{Ind}}^{W_{F}}_{W_{E}}\chi
. Moreover,
({\mathrm{Ind}}^{W_{F}}_{W_{E}}\chi)^{\vee}\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}^{W_{F}}_{W_{E}}\chi^{-1}
.

Without loss of generality, let us for convenience assume that
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
and
\rho=(\det)^{-n}\otimes{\mathrm{Sym}}^{2n+1}
. Since
{}^{L}G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
, up to direct sum and twist by determinant, by
[
JL23
, Thm. 1.0.2]
,
\rho\circ{\mathrm{Ind}}^{W_{F}}_{W_{E}}\chi^{-1}
is a direct sum of one dimensional character or two dimensional dihedral representations attached to
E
and the induced character depends only on
\chi
. The description is uniform over local fields of residual characteristic not equal to two. In particular, from
[
JL70
, Thm. 4.7]
,

{\gamma}(s,{\mathrm{Ind}}^{W_{F}}_{W_{E}}\chi,\psi_{F})={\gamma}(s,\chi,\psi_{E})\cdot{\lambda}_{E/F}

where
{\lambda}_{E/F}=\varepsilon(1/2,\eta_{E},\psi)
is the Weil constant. It follows from the decomposition of local
L
-parameters as in
[
JL23
, Thm. 1.0.2]
that there exists a constant
\kappa^{\rho}_{E/F}={\lambda}_{E/F}^{n}
depending only on the quadratic extension
E/F
,
\rho
and
\psi
only, such that

(2.4.3)

{\gamma}(1/2,{\mathcal{W}}(\chi^{-1}),\rho,\psi)={\gamma}(1/2,\chi^{-1},\rho,\psi_{E})\cdot\kappa^{\rho}_{E/F}.

(4)
The map
\nu_{\alpha}
is introduced in (
2.3.1
), which is the descent of the
\tau_{\alpha}
-invariant function
\widetilde{\mathrm{J}}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}
to
\mathbf{c}(T_{\alpha}(F))\subset{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
. Indeed,
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}\circ\tau_{\alpha}=\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}
which follows from the fact that
{\mathrm{tr}}_{{\alpha}}\circ\tau_{\alpha}={\mathrm{tr}}_{\alpha}
, and the fact that
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}
is the push-forward of the standard Fourier kernel along
\rho^{\vee}_{\alpha}
:

•

If
G={\mathbb{G}}_{m}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\rho^{\vee}_{\alpha}:(E^{\times}_{\alpha})^{n}\times F^{\times}

\displaystyle\to F^{\times}\times E^{1}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\big((e_{i})_{i=1}^{n},a\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigg(a\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Nr}_{\alpha}(e_{i}),\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{e_{i}}{\tau_{\alpha}(e_{i})}\bigg)

•

If
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\rho^{\vee}_{\alpha}:(E_{\alpha}^{\times})^{n+1}

\displaystyle\to E_{\alpha}^{\times}

\displaystyle\big((e_{i})_{i=1}^{n+1}\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}e_{i}\bigg(\frac{e_{i}}{\tau_{\alpha}(e_{i})}\bigg)^{i-1}

(5) For
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv}}
are sections from functions on
\mathbf{c}(T_{\alpha}(F))
to the stable cocenter of
G(F)
that are realized as the (elliptic part of the) Langlands stable transfer map between
G(F)
and its maximal tori that is reviewed in subsection
3.7
. In particular they can be realized as Gelfand-Graev transforms on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
as shown in Theorem
7.5.3
and Theorem
7.7.4
, which are Fourier type transforms on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
that is going to be reviewed in subsection
4.3
.

(6) The convergence issue is addressed as follows: Up to multiplying a smooth and compactly supported function along the determinant, we may assume that the distribution
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\circ\mathbf{c}
that we want to construct actually lies in the stable Bernstein center and its archimedean variant, which is going to be reviewed in subsection
3.5
. On the other hand, for the purpose of local
L
-factors, it suffices to examine the action of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}\circ\mathbf{c}
on
K
-finite vectors for smooth and compactly supported (or Schwartz if
F
is archimedean) functions on
G(F)
. In particular, we may reduce the construction of
\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi}
which lie in the stable Bernstein center of
G(F)
, to distributions in the stable Bernstein center that are actually supported only on finitely many Bernstein components. Hence we are reduced to assume that
\{\mathrm{J}^{\rho}_{\psi,{\alpha}}\}_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}
are all supported on finitely many components of the admissible dual of
\{T_{\alpha}(F)\}_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}
where they now become test (Schwartz if
F
is archimedean) functions on
T_{\alpha}(F)
. Now for this situation the convergence are properly addressed in section
6
, Section
7
and Section
8
.

3.
Bernsten center, cocenter and orbital integrals

We recall the normalization of
[
FLN10
]
of stable orbital integrals as a function over the Steinberg-Hitchin base. We will connect this construction to Kazhdan’s theory of the cocenter and its stable version.

3.1.
Normalization of measures

In this subsection, over a local field
F
, we define a measure on the space of conjugacy classes of
G(F)
of
F
-points of a semisimple simply connected group
G
, essentially following
[
FLN10
]
. We define first a measure on the space of stable conjugacy classes using a canonical volume form of the Steinberg-Hitchin base then derive a measure on the space of conjugacy classes. The discussion can be generalized to general reductive groups following the treatment of
[
FLN10
, §3.3]
.

Following Steinberg (
[
Ste74
]
), if
G
is a split semisimple simply connected group, the invariant theoretic quotient of
G
acting on itself by conjugation is the
r
-dimensional affine space

(3.1.1)

{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathfrak{c}_{G}=G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}G={\mathrm{Spec}}(F[G]^{G})=\mathbb{A}^{r}

with coordinates
c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}
given by
c_{i}(g)={\mathrm{tr}}(\rho_{\omega_{i}}(g))
where
\rho_{\omega_{1}},\ldots,\rho_{\omega_{r}}
are the fundamental representations of
G
. Following
[
FLN10
]
, we call the invariant theoretic quotient
{\mathfrak{c}}={\mathfrak{c}}_{G}
the Steinberg-Hitchin base because in
loc. cit.
the normalization of stable orbital integrals was inspired by the Hitchin fibration. For
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, the invariant theoretic quotient
\mathfrak{c}=\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the affine line with the coordinate function
\mathbf{c}(g)={\mathrm{tr}}(g)
, the trace of the
2\times 2
-matrix
g
with determinant one.

By the Chevalley restriction theorem, we have an isomorphism

{\mathfrak{c}}=T\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W={\mathrm{Spec}}(F[T]^{W})

where
T
is a maximal split torus and
W
is the Weyl group. We define the Weyl discriminant
D\in F[{\mathfrak{c}}]
as the
W
-invariant function on
T
given by

(3.1.2)

D(t)=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi}(1-\alpha(t))

where
\Phi=\Phi(G,T)
is the set of roots. In the case
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
,
T
is torus of diagonal matrices of entries
(t,t^{-1})
with
t\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
. The Weyl discriminant is then

D(t)=(1-t^{2})(1-t^{-2}).

The absolute value
|D(t)|
of the Weyl discriminant
D(t)
admits a square root constructed as follows. Choose a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-basis
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{r}
of the group
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)
of rational characters of
T
. We then have an invariant
r
-form on
T
given by

(3.1.3)

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\chi_{1}}{\chi_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\chi_{r}}{\chi_{r}}

which, up to a sign, is independent of the choice of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-basis
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{r}
of
\mathbb{X}^{*}(T)
. By pulling back the
r
-form
\,\mathrm{d}c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\,\mathrm{d}c_{r}
along the morphism
\pi_{T}:T\to\mathfrak{c}=T\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}W
, we have the formula

(3.1.4)

\pi_{T}^{*}\,\mathrm{d}c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\,\mathrm{d}c_{r}=\Delta_{T}(t)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\chi_{1}}{\chi_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\chi_{r}}{\chi_{r}}

where
\Delta_{T}(t)
is an algebraic function on
T
well defined up to a sign. Its absolute value

\Delta(t)=|\Delta_{T}(t)|\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

is then a real-valued function invariant with respect to the action of the Weyl group
W
.
By
[
FLN10
, Prop. 3.29]
we have the formula

(3.1.5)

|D(t)|=\Delta(t)^{2}.

For
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, we have
c=t+t^{-1}
, and hence

\,\mathrm{d}c=(t-t^{-1})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

We have
\Delta(t)=|t-t^{-1}|
and
\Delta(t)^{2}=|D(t)|
.

The
r
-form
\,\mathrm{d}c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\,\mathrm{d}c_{r}
on the
r
-dimensional affine space
\mathfrak{c}
determines a measure

(3.1.6)

\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}=|\,\mathrm{d}c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\,\mathrm{d}c_{r}|

on
\mathfrak{c}(F)=F^{r}
. It follows from the formula (
3.1.4
) that
\Delta^{-1}\pi_{T}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}
is an invariant measure on
T(F)
which coincides with the measure associated with the invariant
r
-form (
3.1.3
). If
T
is now a maximal torus of
G
but not necessarily split, we still have the finite map
\pi_{T}:T\to\mathfrak{c}
and the formula
\Delta^{-1}\pi_{T}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}
still defines an invariant measure on
T(F)
. We will denote the corresponding measure by

(3.1.7)

\omega_{T}=\Delta^{-1}\pi_{T}^{*}\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}.

Following (
2.4.2
), let

(3.1.8)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}\{0\}&F={\mathbb{C}}\\
\{0,1\}&F={\mathbb{R}}\\
\{0,1,\pm 1/2\}&F\text{ non-archi. of odd residual characteristic}\end{matrix}

parametrizing quadratic
F
-algebras and the stable conjugacy of maximal tori in
G(F)={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
. We have

T_{\alpha}(F)=E_{\alpha}^{1}=\ker(\mathrm{Nr}_{\alpha}:E_{\alpha}^{\times}\to F^{\times})

which is compact for
{\alpha}\neq 0
. For
{\alpha}\neq 0
indexing non-split torus of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
we have the constants

(3.1.9)

\mathbf{v}_{\alpha}={\mathrm{vol}}(E_{\alpha}^{1},\omega_{T_{\alpha}})^{-1}

where
\,\mathrm{d}t_{\alpha}=\omega_{T_{\alpha}}
is the invariant measure on
T_{\alpha}(F)
defined by the formula (
3.1.7
). These constants will play an important role in our discussion of the Weyl integration formula.

We denote by
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}
the complement of the divisor defined by the Weyl discriminant function
D(t)
. Its inverse image
G^{\prime}
in
G
consists of regular semisimple elements of
G
. Following Kottwitz (
[
Kot82
]
), the map

(3.1.10)

\mathbf{c}:G^{\prime}(F)\to{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}(F)

is surjective, and its fibers are stable conjugacy classes so that one can identify
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}(F)
with the set of stable regular semisimple conjugacy classes. As a result, the set of stable regular semisimple conjugacy classes can thus be equipped with a canonical structure of
F
-analytic variety induced from
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
, and the measure induced from
\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}
.

Let
C^{\prime}(F)
denote the set of regular semisimple conjugacy classes in
G^{\prime}(F)
. The fibers of the quotient map

(3.1.11)

\mathbf{d}:G^{\prime}(F)\to C^{\prime}(F)

are regular semisimple conjugacy classes. We consider the map

\mathbf{e}:C^{\prime}(F)\to{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}(F)

from the set of regular semisimple conjugacy classes of
G^{\prime}(F)
into the set of stable regular semisimple conjugacy classes. This is a finite-to-one map since in every stable regular semisimple conjugacy class, there are only finitely many conjugacy classes. This is also a covering in the sense that for every
x\in C^{\prime}(F)
, there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
x
, and an open neighborhood
V
of
\mathbf{e}(x)
such that
\mathbf{e}
induces a homeomorphism
U\to V
. The canonical measure on
\omega_{{\mathfrak{c}}}
on
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}(F)
gives rise to a canonical measure
\omega_{C}
on
C^{\prime}(F)
. We also note that the map (
3.1.11
) is an
F
-analytic submersion. Since
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}(F)
is equipped with a structure of
F
-analytic manifold, its covering
C^{\prime}(F)
is also an
F
-analytic manifold. The measure
\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}
on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
pulls back to a measure on
C^{\prime}(F)
. This extends to a measure on the set
C(F)
of conjugacy classes of
G(F)
by declaring that the complement of
C^{\prime}(F)
in
C(F)
has measure zero.

Finally the above results can be generalized to general reductive groups over
F
without hard based on the discussion in
[
FLN10
, §3.3]
. We omit the details and refer the readers to
loc. cit.
for a detailed discussion.

3.2.
Normalization of stable orbital integrals and orbital integrals

Let
\mathcal{H}
denote the algebra of smooth and compactly supported functions on
G(F)
, where
G
is a reductive group over
F
. When
F
is archimedean, it is also convenient to work with the Schwartz algebra of rapidly decreasing functions
{\mathcal{S}}
on
G(F)
(
[
Wal88b
, p.230]
). In the following, we will focus on functions in
{\mathcal{H}}
, and the discussion applies to functions in
{\mathcal{S}}
when
F
is archimedean.

For every
h\in\mathcal{H}
, we consider the pushforward measure
\mathbf{c}_{!}(h\,\mathrm{d}g)
defined so that for every continuous function
f:{\mathfrak{c}}(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}
we have the following identity, here
\,\mathrm{d}g
is a Haar measure on
G(F)

\langle\mathbf{c}_{!}(h\,\mathrm{d}g),f\rangle=\langle h\,\mathrm{d}g,\mathbf{c}^{*}f\rangle.

Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the standard pairing between compactly supported measures and continuous functions and
\mathbf{c}^{*}f=f\circ\mathbf{c}
is the pull-back of
f
along
\mathbf{c}
. Based on the work of Harish-Chandra, we know that there exists a function
{\rm SO}(h)
locally integrable with respect to
\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}
such that

\mathbf{c}_{!}(h\,\mathrm{d}g)={\rm SO}(h)\omega_{\mathfrak{c}}.

When restricted to the (strongly) regular semisimple locus,

{\rm SO}(h):{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}

is a smooth function that we call the stable orbital integral of
h
. This is the normalization of stable orbital integrals adopted in
[
FLN10
, (3.31)]
.

We can apply the same construction for the map
G^{\prime}(F)\to C^{\prime}(F)
and thus define a function

{\rm O}(h):C^{\prime}(F)\to{\mathbb{C}},

such that

{\mathbf{d}}_{!}(h\,\mathrm{d}g)={\rm O}(h)\omega_{C},

and we call it the orbital integral of
h
. This is equivalent to requiring that for every continuous function
f:C(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}
we have

\langle\mathbf{d}_{!}(h\,\mathrm{d}g),f\rangle=\langle h\,\mathrm{d}g,\mathbf{d}^{*}f\rangle.

We now compare the above normalization of orbital and stable orbital integrals with the more usual definition. In the customary setting, we define the orbital integral for an element
\gamma\in G^{\prime}(F)
with the formula

(3.2.1)

{\rm O}_{\gamma}(h)=\int_{G(F)/G_{\gamma}(F)}h(g\gamma g^{-1})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}g}{\,\mathrm{d}t}

where
\,\mathrm{d}t
is a Haar measure of the of the group of
F
-points
T(F)
of the torus
T=G_{\gamma}
. In general, this definition requires the assumption that the centralizer of
\gamma
is a maximal torus
T=G_{\gamma}
, that is
\gamma
is strongly regular, but under the running assumption that
G^{\rm der}
is simply connected, all regular semisimple elements are strongly regular. We will denote
G^{\rm sr}
the Zariski open subset of
G
consisting of strongly regular elements and
T^{\rm rs}=G^{\rm rs}\cap T
.

This definition is more suitable if we assume that
\gamma
varies in a fixed maximal torus
T(F)
. Fix a maximal torus
T
and an invariant measure
\,\mathrm{d}t
on
T(F)
, we then have a function

(3.2.2)

{\rm O}_{T}(h):T^{\rm sr}(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}

such that for every
\gamma\in T^{\rm sr}(F)
, the value
{\rm O}_{T}(h)(\gamma)={\rm O}_{\gamma}(h)
is supplied by the formula (
3.2.1
).

As the maximal torus
T
of
G
varies, we must consistently choose the Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}t
on
T(F)
. One way to choose the Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}t
on
T(F)
is to use the formula (
3.1.7
). With the Haar measures on maximal tori being chosen as in (
3.1.7
), we can compare the FLN-normalization of orbital integrals with the traditional normalization with the aid of the Weyl integration formula.

For every maximal
F
-torus
T
of
G
, we consider the
F
-analytic map

G(F)/T(F)\times T(F)\to G(F)

given by
(gT(F),t)\mapsto gtg^{-1}
. Its image is the subset of
G(F)
consisting of elements conjugate to an element of
T(F)
. If
T^{\prime}(F)
is the intersection
T(F)\cap G^{\rm sr}(F)
then the image of

q_{T}:G(F)/T(F)\times T^{\rm sr}(F)\to G^{\rm sr}(F)

is an open subset of
G^{\prime}(F)
. The latter map is locally an isomorphism on the source. It induces a cover of its image with
|{N}_{T}(F)/T(F)|
sheets where
{N}_{T}
is the normalizer of
T
in
G
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
denote the set of
G(F)
-conjugacy classes of maximal tori of
G(F)
.
We then have a map

(3.2.3)

q=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}}q_{\alpha}:\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}}G(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)\times T_{\alpha}(F)\to G(F)

whose image is the subset of semisimple elements in
G(F)
. If two maximal tori
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
which are not
G(F)
-conjugate, the images of
G(F)/T_{1}(F)\times T_{1}^{\prime}(F)
and
G(F)/T_{2}(F)\times T_{2}^{\prime}(F)
in
G^{\prime}(F)
are disjoint open subsets. It follows that the map

q:\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}}G(F)/T(F)\times T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F)\to G^{\rm sr}(F).

defines a partition into open and closed subsets

(3.2.4)

G^{\rm sr}(F)=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}}G_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F)

and the map

G(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)\times T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F)\to G^{\rm sr}_{\alpha}(F)

is a covering with
N_{T_{\alpha}}(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)
sheets.

Theorem 3.2.1
.

For every
h\in\mathcal{H}
the following identity holds

(3.2.5)

\int_{G(F)}h(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{|N_{\alpha}(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)|}\int_{t\in T_{\alpha}(F)}|D(t)|{\rm O}_{T_{\alpha}}(h)(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

When
F
is archimedean the theorem also holds for
h\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

Following (
3.2.3
) and (
3.2.4
), it is enough to prove that the Jacobian of the morphism
q_{\alpha}:G(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)\times T_{\alpha}(F)\to G(F)
at a point
(g,t)\in G(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)\times T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F)
is equal to
D(t)
. Because
q_{\alpha}
is
G
-equivariant, we can assume that
g
is the identity element of
G
. The induced map on the tangent space at
(1,t)\in G/T\times T
is now

\,\mathrm{d}q_{\alpha}:\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{t}\times\mathfrak{t}\to\mathfrak{g}

given by

(x,y)\mapsto({\rm ad}(t)-1)(x)+y.

The Jacobian determinant can now be identified with the Weyl discriminant
|D(t)|
. ∎

For every
\alpha\in\mathcal{T}
, the map
p_{\alpha}:T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F)\to C^{\rm sr}(F)
is a covering on its image
p_{\alpha}(T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F))
with the deck group
N_{T_{\alpha}}(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)
. We also remark that the function
{\rm O}_{T_{\alpha}}(h):T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}
descends to a function
{\rm O}^{\alpha}(h):p_{\alpha}(T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F))\to{\mathbb{C}}
so that for every
t\in T_{\alpha}(F)
we have

{\rm O}_{T_{\alpha}}(h)(t)={\rm O}^{\alpha}(h)(p_{\alpha}(t)).

Because the sets
p_{\alpha}(T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F))
form a partition of
C^{\rm sr}(F)
, we have a unique function

{\rm O}^{\mathcal{T}}(h):C^{\rm sr}(F)\to\mathbb{C}

by gluing the functions
{\rm O}^{\alpha}(h)
so that we have the identity

{\rm O}_{T_{\alpha}}(h)(t)={\rm O}^{\mathcal{T}}(h)(p_{\alpha}(t))

for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{T}
and
t\in T_{\alpha}^{\rm sr}(F)
.
Because of the Jacobian relation
\Delta(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\,\mathrm{d}a
with
|D(t)|=\Delta(t)^{2}
, we have

(3.2.6)

\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{|N_{T_{\alpha}}(F)/T_{\alpha}(F)|}\int_{t\in T_{\alpha}(F)}|D(t)|{\rm O}_{T_{\alpha}}(h)(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{C^{\rm sr}(F)}\Delta(a){\rm O}^{\mathcal{T}}(h)(a)\,\mathrm{d}a.

Corollary 3.2.2
.

We have the identities
{\rm O}(h)(a)=\Delta(a){\rm O}^{\mathcal{T}}(h)(a)
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that for every continuous function
f:C(F)\to\mathbb{C}
we have the equality

\int_{C(F)}{\rm O}(h)(a)f(a)\,\mathrm{d}a=\int_{C(F)}|\Delta(a)|{\rm O}^{\mathcal{T}}(h)(a)f(a)\,\mathrm{d}a.

But this equality can be derived from the Weyl integration formula (
3.2.5
) and (
3.2.6
).
∎

3.3.
Cocenter and orbital integrals

Following Harish-Chandra (
[
HC70
]
[
Var77
]
), the function

{\rm O}(h):C(F)^{\rm sr}\to{\mathbb{C}}

is bounded for every
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
. We thus have a map
{\mathcal{H}}\to L^{\infty}(C(F))
given by
h\mapsto{\rm O}(h)
from the algebra of smooth and compactly supported functions
{\mathcal{H}}
to the space of bounded functions on
C(F)
defined almost everywhere. When
F
is non-archimedean, by Kazhdan (
[
Kaz86c
]
), the kernel of this map is the commutator
[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
of
{\mathcal{H}}
. This induces an isomorphism between the cocenter
{\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
with the image
{\rm O}({\mathcal{H}})
of
{\mathcal{H}}
in
L^{\infty}(C(F))
. In general we let
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})
be the image of
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Similarly, the map
h\mapsto{\rm SO}(h)
defines a map
{\mathcal{H}}\to L^{\infty}({\mathfrak{c}}(F))
from the Hecke algebra to the space of defined almost everywhere bounded functions on the Steinberg-Hitchin base whose image will be denoted
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
. We will call
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
the stable cocenter of
{\mathcal{H}}
. The above discussion applies to
F
archimedean and functions
h\in{\mathcal{S}}
. In particular, for
F
archimedean, one can also introduce
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})
and
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})
for functions in
{\mathcal{S}}
.

For
\pi
a smooth irreducible representation of
G(F)
, by definition, its distribution character
\Theta_{\pi}
is the generalized function on
G(F)
such that

{\mathrm{tr}}(\pi(h))=\int_{G(F)}h(g)\Theta_{\pi}(g),\quad h\in{\mathcal{H}}.

The trace distribution extends to
{\mathcal{S}}
when
F
is archimedean (
[
Wal88b
, §8.1]
).

From the work of Harish-Chandra, it is known that
\Theta_{\pi}
is an invariant locally integrable function, which is smooth on the regular semisimple locus. We abuse the notation and write
\Theta_{\pi}
the corresponding invariant function on
G(F)
.

We denote
\Theta_{\pi}^{C}:C^{\rm sr}(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}
the induced function on the space
C^{\rm sr}(F)
of strongly regular conjugacy classes of
G(F)
. The trace of
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
can now written as an integral over
C(F)
:

(3.3.1)

{\mathrm{tr}}_{\pi}(h)=\int_{G(F)}h(g)\Theta_{\pi}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\int_{C(F)}{\rm O}(h)(a)\Theta_{\pi}^{C}(a){\omega}_{C}=\langle{\rm O}(h),\Theta_{\pi}^{C}\rangle.

where
{\omega}_{C}
is the measure on
C(F)
introduced in last subsection, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the standard pairing between functions on
C(F)
with respect to the measure
{\omega}_{C}
.

Similarly, for every
L
-packet of representations
[\pi]
of
G(F)
with attached stable distribution character
\Theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{[\pi]}
(
[
Lan79
]
[
Art13
]
),
\Theta^{{\mathrm{st}}}_{[\pi]}
descends to a function
\Theta^{{\mathfrak{c}}}_{[\pi]}
on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
and we have

(3.3.2)

{\mathrm{tr}}^{\rm st}_{[\pi]}(h)=\int_{G(F)}h(g)\Theta_{[\pi]}^{\rm st}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g=\int_{{\mathfrak{c}}(F)}{\rm SO}(h)(c)\Theta_{[\pi]}^{{\mathfrak{c}}}(c){\omega}_{\mathfrak{c}}=\langle{\rm SO}(h),\Theta_{[\pi]}^{{\mathfrak{c}}}\rangle.

3.4.
Bernstein center and Kazhdan cocenter

Let
G
be a reductive group over a non-archimedean local field
F
. Following
[
Ber84
]
, the Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}={\mathcal{Z}}_{G}={\mathcal{Z}}(G(F))
has four equivalent definitions:

-

It is the ring of endomorphism of the identity functor of the category of smooth representations of
G(F)
;

-

It is the projective limit of the centers of Hecke algebras of finite levels
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}=e_{K}{\mathcal{H}}e_{K}
where
e_{K}
is the characteristic measures of the compact open subgroups
K
, as
K
get smaller and smaller;

-

It is the ring of invariant distributions
J
on
G(F)
which are essentially of compact support, that is for every compact open subgroup
K
, we have
e_{K}J=Je_{K}\in{\mathcal{H}}
;

-

Finally, it is the ring of regular functions on the algebraic variety
\Omega={\Omega}_{G}=\Omega(G(F))
, the Bernstein variety whose points are irreducible representations of
G(F)
up to inertial equivalence.

The Bernstein variety has infinitely many connected components. We denote
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm fin}
the nonunital
subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Z}}
whose elements are regular functions on the Bernstein variety which are supported on the union of finitely many connected components. For every finite subset
I
of the set
\pi_{0}(\Omega)
of connected components of
\Omega
, we will denote
\epsilon_{I}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
the regular function on
\Omega
supported on
\bigcup_{i\in I}\Omega_{i}
and taking the value one at those components. We abuse the notation and still write
{\epsilon}_{I}
for the corresponding distribution on
G(F)
. For every
I\subset\pi_{0}({\mathcal{Z}})
,
\epsilon_{I}
is a finite idempotent of
{\mathcal{Z}}
, i.e. supported on a finite number of components of
\Omega
. We denote
\epsilon^{I}=1-\epsilon_{I}
, which is a cofinite idempotent. If
I\subset J
are two finite subsets of
\pi_{0}(\Omega)
, we have
\epsilon_{I}=\epsilon_{I}\epsilon_{J}
and therefore a homomorphism of unital algebras
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{J}\to{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{I}
given by
f\mapsto f\epsilon_{I}
and a homomorphism of nonunital algebras
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{I}\to{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{J}
. In other words,
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{I}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{J}
are both unital algebras but only the surjection
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{J}\to{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{I}
which preserves units, while the injection
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{I}\to{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{J}
does not preserve units. The Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}
can be presented as the projective limit of
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{I}
, which is then an unital commutative algebra, and its finite part
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm fin}
can be presented as the injective limit of
{\mathcal{Z}}\epsilon_{I}
, which is then a nonunital algebra. For every compact open subgroup
K
of
G(F)
, the quotient
{\mathcal{Z}}_{K}
can be decomposed as a product

{\mathcal{Z}}_{K}={\mathcal{Z}}_{K}\epsilon_{I}\times{\mathcal{Z}}_{K}\epsilon^{I}.

For a fixed compact open subgroup
K
we have
{\mathcal{Z}}_{K}={\mathcal{Z}}_{K}\epsilon_{I}
for all finite subsets
I
of
\pi_{0}(\Omega)
large enough. In this case, we say that
I
dominates
K
and we have
e_{K}\epsilon_{I}=e_{K}
. On the other hand, for every finite subset
I
of
\pi_{0}(\Omega)
, for all compact open subgroup
K
small enough, we have
e_{K}\epsilon_{I}=\epsilon_{I}
, and as a result
{\mathcal{Z}}_{K}\epsilon_{I}={\mathcal{Z}}_{I}
. In this case, we say that the open compact subgroup
K
dominates the index set
I
.

By construction, the Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}
acts on the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
. Because the action of
{\mathcal{Z}}
preserves the commutator
[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
, it acts on the cocenter
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})={\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
. For every compact open subgroup
K
,
{\mathcal{Z}}
acts on
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}=e_{K}{\mathcal{H}}e_{K}
through the quotient
{\mathcal{Z}}e_{K}
and the commutator
[{\mathcal{H}}_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K}]
is a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{K}
-submodule of
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
. As a result, the quotient
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K}]
is a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{K}
-module. If
K^{\prime}
is a compact open subgroup contained in
K
, then we have inclusions
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}
and
[{\mathcal{H}}_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K}]\subset[{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}]
but the inclusion

[{\mathcal{H}}_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K}]\subset[{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}]\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{K}

may be strict. In other words, the induced maps on the quotients

{\mathcal{H}}_{K}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K}]\to{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}]

may or may not be injective. Nevertheless, the inductive limit of
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K}]
as the compact open subgroup
K
gets smaller is
{\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
and it is equipped with a canonical structure of
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module.

We note that we don’t have a natural map
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{K}
let alone a natural map between the quotients
{\mathcal{H}}_{K}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{K},{\mathcal{H}}_{K}]

and
{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{K^{\prime}}]
. However, instead of taking a projective limit on
K
, we can take a projective limit on the set of finite subsets
I
of
\pi_{0}(\Omega)
. For every such a finite subset
I
, we denote
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}={\mathcal{H}}\epsilon_{I}=\epsilon_{I}{\mathcal{H}}
, which is a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{I}
-module. The commutator
[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]
is a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{I}
-submodule, and as a result, the quotient
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]
is a
{\mathcal{Z}}_{I}
-module. If
I^{\prime}
is a finite subset of
\pi_{0}(\Omega)
containing
I
then
\epsilon_{I^{\prime}}\epsilon_{I}=\epsilon_{I}
, and as a result, we have the inclusion
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}
as well as a surjective
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear map
{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{I}
given by
f\mapsto{\epsilon}_{I}f
. With respect to the inclusion, we have
[{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}]\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{I}=[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]
(
[
Kaz86c
, Lem. 3.2]
), and the surjection
f\mapsto{\epsilon}_{I}f
sends
[{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}]
onto
[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]
. We derive an injective map
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]\to{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}]
from the inclusion, and the surjective map
{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}_{I^{\prime}}]\to{\mathcal{H}}_{I}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]
from the surjection. The inductive limit of
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]
as the finite
I
gets bigger is again the cocenter
{\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
. The projective limit of
{\mathcal{H}}_{I}/[{\mathcal{H}}_{I},{\mathcal{H}}_{I}]
as the finite
I
gets bigger is what we will call the
Kazhdan cocenter
and denote by
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{G}={\mathcal{C}}(G(F))
, which is a completion of the cocenter
{\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
. Indeed, we have

{\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]={\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin}

where
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin}
are the
{\mathcal{Z}}
-submodule consisting of elements
f\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\epsilon_{I}f=f
for some finite set
I
of
\pi_{0}(\Omega)
.

It is often enlightening to think of the cocenter
{\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]={\mathcal{C}}^{\rm fin}
and its completion
{\mathcal{C}}
in terms of the Lafforgue variety, constructed in
[
Psa23
]
. In
[
Psa23
]
, the set
{\rm Irr}(G(F))
of irreducible representations of
G(F)
is given a canonical structure of algebraic variety which has a finite-to-one morphism to the Bernstein variety
{\Omega}
. Following the trace Paley-Wiener theorem of Bernstein-Deligne-Kazhdan
[
BDK86
]
, a linear form
\ell
on the vector space
R(G(F))
with basis
{\rm Irr}(G(F))
is of the form

\ell(\pi)=\langle{\rm O}(h),\Theta_{\pi}^{C}\rangle

for some
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
if and only if the function
\pi\mapsto\ell(\pi)
is an algebraic function on the Lafforgue variety and it is supported on finitely many components of the Lafforgue variety. From this angle, we can reinterpret the Kazhdan cocenter
{\mathcal{C}}
as the space of linear form
\ell:R(G(F))\to\mathbb{C}
such that the function
\pi\mapsto\ell(\pi)
defines an algebraic function on the Lafforgue variety, which may or may not be supported on finitely many components of the Lafforgue variety.

Archimedean variant

Next we briefly discuss the archimedean analogue of the Bernstein center. Let
F
be archimedean. We will work with the category of Casselman-Wallach representations of
G(F)
, which are admissible smooth Fréchet representations of moderate growth. By
[
BK14
, Prop. 2.20]
, the category of smooth Fréchet representations of moderate growth is equivalent to the category of continuous non-degenerate
{\mathcal{S}}
-modules, where we recall that
{\mathcal{S}}
is the Schwartz algebra of rapidly decreasing functions on
G(F)
.

A natural candidate for the archimedean analogue of the Bernstein center is the algebra of multipliers of
{\mathcal{S}}
, i.e. the ring of
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}
-equivariant continuous endomorphisms of
{\mathcal{S}}
which is defined as
{\mathrm{End}}_{{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}}({\mathcal{S}})
. Similarly, the Kazhdan cocenter can also be defined as the projective limit over finite subsets of the set of
K
-types of
G(F)
. Precisely, let
\hat{K}
be the set of equivalence classes of irreducible finite dimensional representations of a fixed maximal compact subgroup of
G(F)
. For any
{\sigma}\in\hat{K}
, let
e_{\sigma}
be the idempotent attached to
{\sigma}
. For any finite subset
I
of
\hat{K}
. Let
{\epsilon}_{I}=\sum_{{\sigma}\in I}e_{\sigma}
. Then following the same idea as the non-archimedean case, we may define the Kazhdan cocenter to be the projective limit over the collection of finite subsets of
\hat{K}
of the level
I
cocenter
{\mathcal{S}}_{I}/[{\mathcal{S}}_{I},{\mathcal{S}}_{I}]
, where
{\mathcal{S}}_{I}={\epsilon}_{I}{\mathcal{S}}{\epsilon}_{I}
and both the quotient
{\mathcal{S}}_{I}/[{\mathcal{S}}_{I},{\mathcal{S}}_{I}]
and the projective limit are taken in the category of Fréchet spaces. We will treat the details of the above more algebraic construction in a separate paper. For the purpose of this paper, we would like to follow the spectral characterization in the non-archimedean case and introduce the following more restricted version of the center and cocenter, which are sufficient for the purpose of this paper.

A spectral characterization of this ring relies on the matrix Paley-Wiener theorem for
{\mathcal{S}}
, which seems not known to us for general reductive groups. However, for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, we do have the matrix Paley-Wiener theorem for
{\mathcal{S}}
. When
F={\mathbb{R}}
it follows from
[
Bar88
, p.100]
, and when
F={\mathbb{C}}
, it follows from
[
GGV66
, Chap. IV,§ 5.6]
. The results can be generalized to
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
without difficulties.

Precisely, let
B=TN
be the standard upper triangular Borel of
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
with Cartan decomposition
G(F)=B(F)K
. For any character
\chi:T(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
, the unitary dual
\widehat{T}
of
T(F)
has a real manifold structure given by

\widehat{T}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}\{\pm 1\}\times i{\mathbb{R}},&F={\mathbb{R}}\\
{\mathbb{Z}}\times i{\mathbb{R}},&F={\mathbb{C}}\end{matrix}

When
F={\mathbb{R}}
, for
(\pm,r)\in\widehat{T}
, the associated unitary character is given by
\chi_{(\pm,r)}(z)={\mathrm{sgn}}(z)|z|^{r}
; When
F={\mathbb{C}}
, for
(n,r)\in\widehat{T}
, the associated unitary character is given by
\chi_{n,r}(z)=(\frac{z}{|z|})^{n}|z|^{r}
. Consider the normalized induced representation
\pi_{\chi}={\mathrm{Ind}}_{B}^{G}\chi\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}^{K}_{K\cap B}\chi
.

Notice that
\pi_{\chi_{(\pm,r)}}\simeq\pi_{\chi_{(\pm,-r)}}
when
F={\mathbb{R}}
, and
\pi_{\chi_{(n,r)}}\simeq\pi_{\chi_{(-n,-r)}}
when
F={\mathbb{C}}
. The matrix Paley-Wiener theorem from
[
Bar88
, p.100]
and
[
GGV66
, Chap. IV,§ 5.6]
implies that the functions
h\in{\mathcal{S}}
are characterized by the family of operators
\chi\in\widehat{T}\mapsto\pi_{\chi}(h):{\mathrm{Ind}}^{K}_{K\cap B}\chi\to{\mathrm{Ind}}^{K}_{K\cap B}\chi
extending to entire functions in
\widehat{T}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{C}}
that are of rapidly decay in bounded vertical region and invariant under the Weyl group permutation, together with the compatibility with the intertwining operators between principal series. For details, see the statement of theorems in
loc. cit.

Remark 3.4.1
.

For
F={\mathbb{C}}
, the result from
[
GGV66
, Chap. IV,§ 5.6]
reads as follows. Following the notation from
loc. cit.
the operators
\pi_{\chi}
are represented by the kernels
K(z_{1},z_{2};n_{1},n_{2})
, with
n_{1},n_{2}
characters of
F^{\times}
satisfying
n_{1}-n_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular, comparing with our notation,
n_{1}-n_{2}=n
and
n_{1}+n_{2}=r
(
[
GGV66
, Chap. III,§ 2.2]
). Hence the analytical properties of the operators
\chi\in\widehat{T}\mapsto\pi_{\chi}(h)
are captured by
(n_{1},n_{2})\mapsto K(z_{1},z_{2};n_{1},n_{2})
. Following the proof of the main theorem in
[
GGV66
, Chap. IV,§ 5.6]
,
K(z_{1},z_{2};n_{1},n_{2})
is the Mellin transform of
\varphi(z_{1},z_{2};{\lambda})
via

K(z_{1},z_{2};n_{1},n_{2})=\frac{i}{2}\int_{{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{C}}}\varphi(z_{1},z_{2};{\lambda}){\lambda}^{n_{1}-1}\bar{{\lambda}}^{n_{2}-1}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}\bar{{\lambda}}

and
\varphi(z_{1},z_{2};{\lambda})=|{\lambda}|^{2}\Phi(z_{1},1;{\lambda}z_{2},{\lambda})
, which, by the main theorem in
[
GGV66
, Chap. IV,§ 5.6]
, is of rapidly decay in
{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
with extra symmetries capturing its compatibility with the intertwining operators. Essentially, the theorem says that besides the compatibility with intertwining operators, the analytical behavior of the kernel functions for the operators
\chi\mapsto\pi_{\chi}
are exactly given by the Mellin transform of Schwartz functions on
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
whose image are invariant under the Weyl group action. For a precise characterization of the Mellin transform of Schwartz functions on
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
(i.e. smooth functions on
{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
that are both rapidly decay near infinity and zero), see
[
Igu78
, Thm. 4.3]
or a quick sumary in
[
JL21
, Defin. 2.2]
.

For the purpose of this paper, we introduce the following algebra of multipliers
{\mathcal{Z}}={\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
on
{\mathcal{S}}
:

Definition 3.4.2
.

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
.

•

Let
F={\mathbb{R}}
. Let
{\mathcal{Z}}
be the space of holomorphic functions
{\gamma}
on
{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
that are even on
{\mathbb{C}}
-factor, of rapidly decay in bounded vertical strips, and are of polynomial growth on the subset
{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
;

•

Let
F={\mathbb{C}}
. Let
{\mathcal{Z}}
be the space of functions
{\gamma}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{C}}
that are holomorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}
-factor with symmetry
{\gamma}(n,z)={\gamma}(-n,-z)
, and are of rapidly decay on bounded vertical strips that is uniform in
{\mathbb{Z}}
-factor.

Let
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
.

•

Let
F={\mathbb{R}}
. Let
{\mathcal{Z}}
be the space of holomorphic functions on
({\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})^{2}
that are invariant under switching the two factors, of rapidly decay in bounded vertical strips, and are of moderate growth on the subset
{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
;

•

Let
F={\mathbb{C}}
. Let
{\mathcal{Z}}
be the space of functions on
({\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{C}})^{2}
that are holomorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}
-factor, invariant under switching the two factors,and are of rapidly decay on bounded vertical strips that is uniform in
{\mathbb{Z}}
-factor.

Let
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\mathrm{fin}}
be the subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
that are supported on finitely many
K
-types.

Notice that the archimedean Langlands local gamma factors are of rapidly decay with neighborhoods of possible poles removed via Stirling’s formula (see
[
Luo19
, Lem. 3.4.6]
for instance). Hence following Part (5) of Remark
2.1.2
, after multiplying a smooth and compactly supported function on the determinant factor for the
\rho
-Fourier kernels (which, after regularization, are the Plancherel inversion of archimedean Langlands gamma factors), the associated holomorphic functions on the admissible dual belong to
{\mathcal{Z}}
. In particular
{\mathcal{Z}}
is enough for our purpose. When
F={\mathbb{R}}
the extra moderate growth condition on
{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
is needed due to the existence of discrete series representations of
G({\mathbb{R}})
. It is clear that
{\mathcal{Z}}
is an algebra under multiplication, and any
{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
gives rise to a multiplier from
{\mathcal{S}}
to itself. Moreover, the matrix Paley-Wiener theorem for smooth and compactly supported functions (
[
Del05
]
) ensures that
{\gamma}
is represented by an invariant distribution
J=J_{\gamma}
on
G(F)
.

For the purpose of next subsection, let
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
be the subset of
{\mathcal{Z}}
given by stable distributions in the sense of
[
Lan79
]
, and let
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{fin}}
.

3.5.
Stable Bernstein center

Let
G
be a reductive group over a non-archimedean local field
F
. Let
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
be the Bernstein center of
G(F)
. Following Bezrukavnikov, Kazhdan and Varshavsky (
[
BKV13
]
), let
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}
be the stable Bernstein center of
G(F)
which is the subset of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
consisting of stable distributions on
G(F)
in the sense of
[
Lan79
]
. We recall that an invariant distribution
J
on
G(F)
is called stable if for any test function
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
, we have
J(h)=0
whenever the stable orbital integral of
h
vanishes identically. Let
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}_{G}={\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}\cap{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{fin}}_{G}
. The stable center conjecture of
[
BKV13
]
stipulates that
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}
is a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
.

Let
{\Omega}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}
be the variety of infinitesimal characters of
G
(
[
Hai14
, §5]
[
SS13
, §6]
). For
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, since the local Langlands conjecture is known (
[
LL79
]
), there is a finite to one map
p:{\Omega}_{G}\to{\Omega}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}
sending the cuspidal datum in
{\Omega}_{G}
to the corresponding
\widehat{G}
-conjugacy classes of semi-simple local
L
-parameters. For
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}
and a connected component
{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}}\subset{\Omega}_{G}^{\mathrm{st}}
, we call the projection of
J
to
{\Omega}^{\mathrm{st}}_{0}
to be the distribution in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
that is the projection of
J
to
p^{-1}({\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}})
. When
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, there is no stability issue and the stable Bernstein center is the same as the Bernstein center.

In this section, we establish the following theorem. Notice that when
F
is archimedean, we have introduced
{\mathcal{Z}},{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
in subsection
3.4
when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
.

Theorem 3.5.1
.

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and the residual characteristic of
F
is other than two. Then the following statements hold.

(1)

The stable tempered characters are dense in the space of stable distributions;

(2)

{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\mathrm{st}}
is a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
;

(3)

For any
J_{G}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}
, its projection to any connected components of
{\Omega}^{\mathrm{st}}_{G}
lands in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}_{G}
;

The above theorem also holds for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
over any local field
F
. Part (2) and Part (3) are manifest, while Part (1) is based on the density theorem of Kazhdan (
[
Kaz86a
, Thm. 0]
) when
F
is non-archimedean, and Shelstad when
F
is archimedean (
[
She79a
, Lem. 5.3]
).

Proof.

(1) When
F
is archimedean, the density of stable tempered characters follows from
[
She79a
, Lem. 5.3]
. It remains to focus on the case when
F
is non-archimedean.

To show that the stable tempered characters are dense in the space of stable distributions for
G
, we only need to show that for a fixed test function
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
, if the stable tempered traces of
h
vanish, then all the stable orbital integrals of
h
vanish.

Notice that the stable tempered characters for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
are given by the restriction of tempered characters of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
(
[
LL79
]
). For
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, there is no stability issue, and the density theorem of Kazhdan (
[
Kaz86a
]
) shows that for a test function
\widetilde{h}
on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, its tempered trace vanishes if and only if its orbital integral vanishes. Now for the fixed test function
h\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G(F))
, since
G(F)
is closed in
\widetilde{G}(F)=\{g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)\mid\det g\in F^{\times 2}\}
, and
\widetilde{G}(F)
is open in
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
, we can lift
h
to a test function
\widetilde{h}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F))
such that
\widetilde{h}|_{G(F)}=h
. Applying the density theorem to
\widetilde{h}
, we get

(3.5.1)

\Theta_{\Pi}(\widetilde{h})=0
for any tempered representation
\Pi
of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
if and only if
{\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})=0
. Here
\Theta_{\Pi}
