is the trace distribution of
\Pi
, and
{\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})
is the orbital integral of
\widetilde{h}
.

By Weyl integration formula and the support condition of
\widetilde{h}
, we can rewrite
\Theta_{\Pi}(\widetilde{h})
in terms of (trace, determinant) variables

\Theta_{\Pi}(\widetilde{h})=\int_{(t,d^{2})\in F\times F^{\times}}\Theta_{\Pi}(t,d^{2}){\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(t,d^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}d

Twisting
\Pi
by a unitary character
\chi
of
F^{\times}
, and using the Mellin inversion in
\chi
, we get

(3.5.2)

\Theta_{\Pi}(\widetilde{h})=0
for any tempered representation
\Pi
of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
if and only if the following function vanishes identically (on regular semi-simple locus)

(t,d)\in F\times F^{\times 2}\mapsto\int_{t\in F}\Theta_{\Pi}(t/d,1){\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(t,d^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t=0=\int_{t\in F}\Theta_{\Pi}(t,1){\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(td,d^{2})\,\mathrm{d}t,

if and only if the orbital integral of
\widetilde{h}
vanishes.

But
{\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(td,d^{2})
is the orbital integral of
\widetilde{h}(d\cdot)
evaluated at any regular semi-simple element with trace
t
. Since
\widetilde{h}
is a test function,
d\in F^{\times}\mapsto\widetilde{h}(dg)
is uniformly locally constant in
d
for any
g
. Hence we may multiply
\widetilde{h}
by a characteristic function along the center that is supported in a small neighborhood
{\mathcal{N}}_{\widetilde{h}}
of identity, such that
{\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(td,d^{2})
is non-vanishing only for
d\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\widetilde{h}}
, and is identically equal to
{\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(t,1)
whenever
d\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\widetilde{h}}
. It follows that we deduce the following fact for the above choice of
\widetilde{h}
:

(3.5.3)

\int_{t\in F}\Theta_{\Pi}(t,1){\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(t,1)\,\mathrm{d}t=0

for any tempered representation
\Pi
of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
if and only if
{\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})
vanishes.

But
{\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(t,1)
is exactly equal to the stable orbital integral of
h
evaluated at any regular semi-simple element of trace
t
, hence

\int_{t\in F}\Theta_{\Pi}(t,1){\mathrm{O}}(\widetilde{h})(t,1)\,\mathrm{d}t={\mathrm{tr}}(\Pi|_{G})(h)=0

if and only if
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(h)=0
. This completes the proof of the density of stable tempered characters in the space of stable distributions.

(2) To show that
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\mathrm{st}}
is a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}
, we only need to show that for any
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\mathrm{st}}
, whenever the stable orbital integral of
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
vanishes, the stable orbital integral of
J*h
also vanishes. By Part (1) above, we only need to show that the stable tempered trace of
J*h
vanishes. Using the Plancherel inversion formula for distributions in the Bernstein center, and the fact that the Plancherel measure for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
is constant on tempered local
L
-packets (
[
GGPS69
, Chap.2,§6]
), we only need to show that the function on the Bernstein variety determined by
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\mathrm{st}}
is constant on tempered local
L
-packets. This follows from the following statement:

(3.5.4)

For a fixed tempered local
L
-packet of
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
, the only stable distributions that are given by the linear combination of distribution characters inside the
L
-packet are given by the constant multiples of the stable tempered characters.

The proof for archimedean situation is the same as non-archimedean case. In the following we outline the proof for non-archimedean situation. We only need to treat the supercuspidal local
L
-packets. When the supercuspidal local
L
-packet has two elements, the above fact is manifest since a single supercuspidal character is not stable, which can be checked by hand using the character table from
[
SS68
]
. For the unique supercuspidal local
L
-packet with four elements, following
[
Cas72
, Thm. 1.9]
, there are two (reducible) representations
\Pi^{\pm}
with
\Pi^{\pm}=\pi_{1}^{\pm}\oplus\pi_{2}^{\pm}
, such that
\{\pi_{i}^{\pm}\}_{i=1}^{2}
forms the packet, and conjugation by
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
permutes
\Pi^{+}
and
\Pi^{-}
. Hence the result follows from the linear independence of the distribution characters.

(3) Following the discussion in Part (1) and Part (2), it suffices to show that
J_{{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}}}
is a stable distribution, where
{\Omega}^{\mathrm{st}}_{0}\subset{\Omega}_{G}^{\mathrm{st}}
is a fixed connected component, and
J_{{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}}}\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\mathrm{fin}}
is the distribution corresponding to the characteristic function of
p^{-1}({\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}})\subset{\Omega}_{G}
. But this follows from the explicit calculation in subsection
5.5
, which shows that
J_{{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}}}
is locally integrable and hence is given by a stable function in the sense that
J_{{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}}}(g)=J_{{\Omega}_{0}^{\mathrm{st}}}(g^{\prime})
whenever
g
and
g^{\prime}
are regular semi-simple and stably conjugate to each other.
∎

Remark 3.5.2
.

Let
G
be a reductive group over a non-archimedean local field
F
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be its Hecke algebra. Consider the map
p_{\mathrm{st}}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})
sending a test function to its stable orbital integral. Let

{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}=\{f\in{\mathcal{H}}\mid{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(f)=0\}=p^{-1}_{\mathrm{st}}(\{0\}),

which is the subset of
{\mathcal{H}}
whose stable orbital integral vanishes identically. Following
[
SS13
]
, one define
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}
to be the subalgebra of the usual Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}
consisting of distributions that stabilizes
{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}
, i.e.

{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}=\{z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\mid z*{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}\subset{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}\}.

Comparing with the definition for
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
, the definition for
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}
has the benefit that it is immediately a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{Z}}
by definition. However, in general it is not clear about the relation between
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
. In the following, we establish the following fact:

Proposition 3.5.3
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

In general,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}\subset{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
;

(2)

When
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}\supset{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
, and hence
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}={\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}
.

Proof.

(1) For any
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}
and
f\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}
, by definition,
z*f\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}
. Equivalently,
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(z*f)=0
. It is clear that
{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}
is invariant under the involution
g\mapsto g^{-1}
. Hence
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(z*f^{\vee})=0
. Now to show that
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
, we need to show that
z
is a stable distribution, which, by definition, is equivalent to show that
z(f)=0
as long as
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(f)=0
. But by definition,
z(f)={\delta}_{e}(z*f^{\vee})
. Since
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(z*f^{\vee})=0
and
{\delta}_{e}
is a stable distribution, we deduce that
{\delta}_{e}(z*f^{\vee})=z(f)=0
. It follows that
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}\subset{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
.

(2) For any
f\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}
and
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
, we only need to show that
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}(z*f)=0
. By the density of stable tempered characters for
G(F)={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
that is proved in Theorem
3.5.1
, we only need to show that for any tempered local
L
-packet
[\pi]
of
G(F)
,
{\mathrm{tr}}^{{\mathrm{st}}}_{[\pi]}(z*f)=0
. Following the proof of Theorem
3.5.1
, the regular function
{\gamma}(z,\cdot)
on the Bernstein variety attached to
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}}}
is constant on the tempered local
L
-packets of
G(F)
. Hence we are reduced to show that
{\mathrm{tr}}^{\mathrm{st}}_{[\pi]}(f)=0
. But it follows from the fact that
f\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{\mathrm{st}}
.

∎

As a corollary, we have the following fact.

Corollary 3.5.4
.

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
over a non-archimedean local field
F
of odd residual characteristic. Then
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
acts on
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})
.

Similar statement is true for
F
archimedean, based on the trace Paley-Wiener theorem for
{\mathcal{S}}
(
[
Del86
]
). Since our definition of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
is pretty limited in the archimedean case, we will not discuss the details here but leave it to a future work.

3.6.
Integral transforms on the stable cocenter

By
[
MT02
, Thm. 2.5]
, for any reductive group
G
over a non-archimedean local field
F
, any distribution
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\rm fin}
can be represented by an invariant locally integrable function on
G(F)
which is smooth on
G^{\prime}(F)
. It follows that
J
is the pullback of a smooth function
J_{C}
on the set
C^{\prime}(F)
regular semisimple conjugacy classes in
G(F)
. Similarly, any stably invariant distribution
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
is represented by a locally integrable stable function on the Steinberg-Hitchin base, and hence can be represented as
J=({\bf c}^{*}J_{\mathfrak{c}})\,\mathrm{d}g
where
J_{\mathfrak{c}}
is a function on the Steinberg-Hitchin base
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
whose necessary analytic properties will be established in subsection
5.5
when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
.

In general, assuming the stable center conjecture,
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
has a natural algebra structure under convolution. Moreover, assuming the analogue of Corollary
3.5.4
for general reductive groups, the stable cocenter
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})={\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st,fin}
carries a natural structure of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st}
-module. By construction, elements of
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
are of the form
f={\rm SO}(h)
with
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
f
is a locally integrable function on the Steinberg-Hitchin base
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
, well-defined as a smooth function on
{\mathfrak{c}}^{\prime}(F)
. At least in the case
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
F
is non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic, we know that
f
is represented by continuous functions on
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
(
[
Lan13
, (2.2.10)]
). In our framework, a crucial problem is to represent the action of an element
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
on an element
f\in{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
via integral transform over the Steinberg-Hitchin base. This problem will be solved in section
10
.

3.7.
Langlands stable transfer and descent

In
[
Lan13
]
, Langlands introduced the notion of stable transfer that we now reformulate with the aid of the Kazhdan cocenter. Let
H
be another reductive group over
F
such that we have a homomorphism of
L
-groups
\rho:\,\!\!^{L}H\to\,\!\!^{L}G
. Following the functoriality conjecture of Langlands,
\rho
induces a map
[\pi_{H}]\mapsto[\pi]
from the set of
L
-packets of
H
to the set of
L
-packets of
G
. It follows that by adjunction there exists a map

{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}:{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}}_{G})\to{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}}_{H})

that Langlands calls the stable transfer such that for every
L
-packet
[\pi_{H}]
of
H(F)
mapping to the
L
-packet
[\pi]
of
G(F)
, we have

\langle f,\Theta^{{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}}_{[\pi]}\rangle=\langle{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}(f),\Theta_{[\pi_{H}]}^{{\mathfrak{c}}_{H}}\rangle

for every
f\in{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}}_{G})
. It is expected that Langlands’ stable transfer extends to a linear map between the Kazhdan cocenters

{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}(G(F))\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}(H(F)).

Since
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}}_{G})
can be realized as a suitable space of function on the
F
-analytic manifold
{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}(F)
, it is desirable that
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}
is an integral transform whose kernel is of algebraic nature.

The map
\rho_{*}:[\pi_{H}]\mapsto[\pi]
is expected to induce an algebraic map
\rho_{*}:\Omega^{\rm st}_{H}\to\Omega^{\rm st}_{G}
which is equivalent to a homomorphism of algebras

(3.7.1)

\rho^{*}:{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\rm st}\to{\mathcal{Z}}_{H}^{\rm st}

between the stable Bernstein centers, assuming the stable center conjecture à la
[
BKV13
]
. We call the map
descent
. Langlands’ stable transfer map
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}_{G}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}_{H}
is then
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\rm st}
-linear.

In the case of
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
H
is a one-dimensional torus, the stable transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rho}
was discussed in great detail in Langlands’ paper (
[
Lan13
]
), which is a source of inspiration for our work. It is rather surprising that Langlands did not seem to be aware that his stable transfer has an integral representation discovered fifty years earlier by Gelfand and Graev (
[
GG63
]
). The second-named author learned about the Gelfand-Graev formula from R. Kottwitz and cannot thank him enough for sparkling his curiosity about this formula.

4.
Gelfand-Graev stable transfer

In this section, we review the construction of dihedral representations of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
via Weil representation following
[
Wei64
]
and
[
JL70
, §1]
. We will recall the Gelfand-Graev formula for the character of dihedral representations of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
following
[
GG63
]
. For
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, this gives rise to the stable character, which induces an explicit formula for Langlands’ stable transfer
[
Lan13
]
. We will call this formula the Gelfand-Graev transform and explore its properties.

4.1.
The Weil representation

Let
E
be an étale quadratic
F
-algebra where
F
is a local field of residual characteristic not two. We have the additive character
\psi_{E}:E\to{\mathbb{C}}^{1}
given by
\psi_{E}(x)=\psi({\mathrm{tr}}_{E/F}(x))
where
\psi:F\to{\mathbb{C}}^{1}
is the additive character of
F
chosen in
1.4
. The Weil representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}
of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
on the space
{\mathcal{S}}(E)
of Schwartz-Bruhat functions on
E
, given in
[
JL70
, Prop.1.3]
, is defined on generators of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
by the following formulae: for any test function
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(E)
and
z\in E
,

(1)

\big({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\begin{pmatrix}{\alpha}&\\
&{\alpha}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\phi\big)(z)=\eta_{E}({\alpha})|{\alpha}|\,\phi({\alpha}z),\quad{\alpha}\in F^{\times};

(2)

\big({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\phi\big)(z)=\psi\big(u\mathrm{Nr}(z)\big)\,\phi(z),\quad u\in F^{\times};

(3)

\big({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{pmatrix}\phi\big)(z)={\lambda}_{E/F}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}(\phi)(\iota(z))

Here we have

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}(\phi)(z)=\int_{x\in E}\psi_{E}(xz)\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x,

and

(4.1.1)

{\lambda}_{E/F}={\lambda}_{E/F,\psi}={\varepsilon}(1/2,\eta_{E},\psi)

is the Weil constant
[
JL70
, Lem. 1.1]
.
We will need an explicit formula for the Weil representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}
as in Lemma
4.1.2
, which will be derived from the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

For every Schwartz-Bruhat function
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(E)
, we have

\big({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\phi\big)(z)={\lambda}_{E/F}\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|}\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x.

Proof.

Write

\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&\\
&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-v\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{pmatrix},

then

\big({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\phi\big)(z)=\eta(-1){\lambda}_{E/F}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\begin{pmatrix}1&-v\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{pmatrix}\phi\bigg)\big(-\iota(z)\big).

Expanding the definition of
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}
on the right hand side, the above equation can be rewritten as

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \eta(-1){\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{x\in E}\psi_{E}\big(-x\iota(z)\big){\mathcal{W}}_{E}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}1&-v\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{pmatrix}\phi\bigg)(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \eta(-1){\lambda}^{2}_{E/F}\int_{x\in E}\psi_{E}\big(-x\iota(z)\big)\psi\big(-v\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}(\phi)\big(\iota(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x.

By
[
BH06
, Prop. 29.4 (3)]
,

{\lambda}^{2}_{E/F}={\varepsilon}(\frac{1}{2},\eta,\psi)^{2}=\eta(-1)
. Hence the above identity becomes

(4.1.2)

=\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(-v\mathrm{Nr}(x)-\mathrm{Tr}\big(x\iota(z)\big)\bigg)\,{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}(\phi)\big(\iota(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x.

Since

-v\mathrm{Nr}(x)-\mathrm{Tr}\big(x\iota(z)\big)=-v\mathrm{Nr}\bigg(x+\frac{z}{v}\bigg)+\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}

equation (
4.1.2
) becomes

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\bigg)\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(-v\mathrm{Nr}\bigg(x+\frac{z}{v}\bigg)\bigg){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}(\phi)\big(\iota(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\bigg)\int_{x\in E}\psi\big(-v\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi_{E}}(\phi)\left(\iota(x)-\frac{\iota(z)}{v}\right)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x

Following Weil’s formula for the Fourier transform of
\psi\circ\mathrm{Nr}
(
[
JL70
, Lem. 1.1]
), the above equation is equal to

\psi\left(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\right)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|}{\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{x\in E}\phi(x)\psi_{E}\left(-\frac{\iota(z)x}{v}\right)\psi\left(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\right)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x.

The final formula follows from the following identity

\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\frac{\iota(z)x}{v}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}=\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}.

∎

Lemma 4.1.2
.

For any
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(E)
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\phi\bigg)(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta(-v){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|v|}\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)|t|\psi\big(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)\phi(tx)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1.1
,

\displaystyle\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\phi\bigg)(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta(-v){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|v|}\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\phi\bigg)(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x

By definition, we have the formula

\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\phi\bigg)(x)=\eta(t)|t|\psi\big(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)\phi(tx),

which proves the lemma.
∎

4.2.
Dihedral representations

Fix a unitary character
\chi
of
E^{\times}
and let
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)={\mathcal{S}}(E,\chi)
be the
(\chi,E^{1})
-coinvariant space of
{\mathcal{S}}(E)
consisting of functions
\Phi
on
E
given by

\Phi(z)={\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}(\phi)(z)=\int_{E^{1}}\phi(ze)\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e,\quad z\in E^{\times},\quad\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(E).

When
E/F
is a nonsplit quadratic extension and hence
E^{1}
is compact,
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
can also be viewed as the space of functions
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(E)
such that
\phi(ze)=\chi(e)\phi(z)
for any
e\in E^{1},z\in E
, which is then a direct factor of
{\mathcal{S}}(E)
. Whether
E/F
is split or not, the action of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
on
{\mathcal{S}}(E)
commuting with the action of
E^{1}
and induces a representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi)
of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
on
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
, see
[
JL70
, Prop. 1.5]
.

The Galois conjugation
\iota\in{\mathrm{Gal}}(E/F)
, induces an involution of
{\mathcal{S}}(E)
commuting with the action of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
while normalizing the action of
E^{1}
mapping by the unitary character
\chi:E^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{1}
to its inverse
\chi^{-1}
. It induces an involutive intertwiner

\iota:{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi)\to{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi^{-1}).

We recall the well-known fact
[
Cas72
, Thm. 1.7]
:

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Let
E/F
be a quadratic extension then
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi)
is irreducible if
\chi^{2}\neq 1
. It is also irreducible for
\chi=1
in which case it is a principal series representation. There is a unique nontrivial character
\chi_{E}
of
E^{1}
with
\chi_{E}^{2}=1
. In this case, the involution
\iota:{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi_{E})\to{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi_{E})
decomposes
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi_{E})
into a direct sum of of two irreducible representations

{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi_{E})={\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi_{E})_{+}\oplus{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi_{E})_{-}.

Let
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
be the subgroup of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
consisting of
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
such that

\det g\in{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathrm{Nr}:E^{\times}\to F^{\times}).

Then the representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}^{+}(\chi)
of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
can be extended to a representation of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
, also denoted as
{\mathcal{W}}^{+}_{E}(\chi)
, via

\bigg({\mathcal{W}}^{+}_{E}(\chi)\begin{pmatrix}a&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\phi\bigg)(z)=|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\phi(ze_{a}),\quad z\in E

where
a=\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a})
. Notice that
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
and hence the right-hand side is independent of the choice of the representative
e_{a}
.
We denote

(4.2.1)

{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)={\mathrm{Ind}}^{{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)}_{{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}}{\mathcal{W}}^{+}_{E}(\chi),

and call it the
dihedral representation
of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
attached to the unitary character
\chi
of
E^{\times}
.

Theorem 4.2.2
.

If
\chi\neq 1
, the restriction of the dihedral representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
to
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
decomposes into a direct sum or two irreducible supercuspidal representations if
\chi\neq\chi_{E}
, and four irreducible supercuspidal representations if
\chi=\chi_{E}
. In both cases, those irreducible summands form an
L
-packet of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
.

Following
[
JL70
, §4]
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
is an irreducible admissible representation of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
, which is independent of the choice of
\psi
. Moreover, from
[
JL70
, Thm. 4.7, Thm. 5.15, Thm. 6.4]
,

(4.2.2)

L(s,{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))=L(s,\chi),\quad{\varepsilon}(s,{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi),\psi)={\varepsilon}(s,\chi,\psi_{E}){\lambda}_{E/F}

where the local factors on the left hand side are defined via Hecke integrals as in
[
JL70
, Thm. 2.18, Thm. 5.15, Thm. 6.4]
, and the right hand side are defined via Tate integrals (
[
Tat50
]
).

Up to a finite linear combination, we can assume that
\phi\in{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
is either supported on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{-}={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)\backslash{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
. When
\phi
is supported on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
, we can assume that
\phi
is actually represented by a vector in
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
. The following lemma is similar to Lemma
4.1.2
.

Lemma 4.2.3
.

With the above notation, for
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)

\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}a&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\phi\bigg)(z)

is nonzero only when
a\in\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})
, and is equal to

\displaystyle=\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-v){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|v|}\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)|t|\psi(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x))\phi(tx)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x

when
a=\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a})
for some
e_{a}\in E^{\times}
.

4.3.
Gelfand-Graev stable character formula

In this subsection, we review the stable character formula of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
, following Gelfand and Graev
[
GG63
]
and
[
GGPS69
, Chap. 2,§5]
. We also slightly generalize their work to
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
.

Let
E
be an étale quadratic field extension of
F
and
\chi:E^{\times}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
a character. We will not discuss the stable transfer when
E
is split since the distribution character for the principal series is easier and will not be used in subsequent. Let
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
be the dihedral representation (
4.2.1
) constructed in the previous subsection. We will denote
\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}
its distribution character, which is a generalized function determined by a locally integrable function on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
smooth over the regular semisimple locus.

As we noted in theorem
4.2.2
for
\chi\neq 1
, the restriction of the dihedral representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
to
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
decomposes into a direct sum of two irreducible supercuspidal representations if
\chi\neq\chi_{E}
, and four irreducible supercuspidal representations if
\chi=\chi_{E}
. Those irreducible summands form an
L
-packet of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
in both cases. The sum of characters in the
L
-packet is the restriction of
\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}
to
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
, which is the stable character of the
L
-packet. We have a beautiful formula for those stable characters, following Gelfand and Graev
[
GG63
]
(see also
[
GGPS69
, Chap. 2,§5, p.204]
).

Theorem 4.3.1

(
[
GG63
]
)
.

For every regular semi-simple element
g\in{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
, we have

(4.3.1)

\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)=\frac{2}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e)}\int^{*}_{E^{1}}\frac{\eta_{E}\big({\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)\big)}{|{\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)|}\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

As we notice immediately that the above expression is independent of the choice of the Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e
on
E^{1}
because it appears in both the numerator and the denominator. We will nonetheless use the Haar measure fixed in subsection
3.1
.

Remark 4.3.2
.

We also notice that the integral over
E^{1}
is not absolutely convergent, and some regularization is needed. Following
[
GGPS69
, Chap. 2,§5]
we can make sense of the integral as the holomorphic continuation at
s=1
of the following
s
-family of integrals

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle\int_{E^{1}}\frac{\eta_{E}\big({\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)\big)}{|{\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)|^{s}}\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e

which are absolutely convergent for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1
.

Remark 4.3.3
.

Alternatively, the regularized integral (
4.3.1
) can be understood as follows. Let
\chi^{\iota}=\chi\circ\iota
where
\iota
is the nontrivial Galois involution attached to
E/F
. Then

\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)=\int^{*}_{E^{1}}\frac{\eta_{E}\big({\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)\big)}{|{\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)|}\frac{(\chi+\chi^{\iota})(e)}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

From
[
FLN10
, Prop. 3.29]
, under the trace map
{\mathrm{tr}}:{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)\supset E^{1}\to F
which is generically a
2
-fold cover, the additive Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}x
and
\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e
for
x={\mathrm{tr}}(e)
are related as in subsection
3.1
by the following identity

(4.3.3)

\,\mathrm{d}x={\Delta}(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e

where
{\Delta}(e)
is the square root of the norm of the Weyl discriminant. Let
\phi_{\chi}
be the function supported on
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1})\subset F={\mathrm{tr}}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))
defined via

(4.3.4)

\phi_{\chi}({\mathrm{tr}}(e))=\frac{\chi(e)+\chi^{\iota}(e)}{{\Delta}(e)}

then

\displaystyle\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}\int^{*}_{\xi\in F}\frac{\eta_{E}\big({\mathrm{tr}}(g)-\xi\big)}{|{\mathrm{tr}}(g)-\xi|}\phi_{\chi}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi

(4.3.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}\bigg(\frac{\eta_{E}}{|\cdot|}*_{+}\phi_{\chi}\bigg)({\mathrm{tr}}(g)).

Here
*_{+}
is the additive convolution.
Using Tate’s thesis we can provide an alternative expression for (
4.3.5
). Precisely, the functional equation of Tate integrals can be reinterpreted as the following identity for meromorphic families of tempered distributions on
F
,

{\gamma}(s,\eta_{E},\psi){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\eta_{E}|\cdot|^{s-1})=\eta_{E}|\cdot|^{-s}.

Therefore (
4.3.5
) is equal to

\displaystyle\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{2{\gamma}(1,\eta_{E},\psi)}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\eta_{E})*_{+}\phi_{\chi}\bigg)({\mathrm{tr}}(g))

\displaystyle=\frac{2{\gamma}(1,\eta_{E},\psi)}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}\big(\eta_{E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{\chi})\big)\bigg)({\mathrm{tr}}(g)).

Here
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{\chi})
is understood as the Fourier transform of a compactly supported distribution, which is represented by a globally bounded smooth function since
\phi_{\chi}
is absolutely integrable. After multiplying by
\eta_{E}
, it is still a tempered distribution. Then the Fourier transform of the tempered distribution
\eta_{E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{\chi})
is represented by a function which up to constant is exactly the desired stable distribution character.

Finally, from
[
GGPS69
, p.204]
and the functional equation of local gamma factors,

a_{E}c_{E}=\frac{2}{{\gamma}(0,\eta_{E},\psi)}=2\eta_{E}(-1){\gamma}(1,\eta_{E},\psi)

with

a_{E}={\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}),\quad c_{E}=\frac{{\gamma}(1,\mathbbm{1}_{E},\psi_{E})}{{\gamma}(1,\mathbbm{1}_{F},\psi_{F})\cdot{\gamma}(1,\eta_{E},\psi_{F})}={\lambda}_{E/F}^{-1}

where the formula for
a_{E}
comes from
[
GGPS69
, p.136]
and
c_{E}
from
[
GGPS69
, p.155 (3)]
together with
[
JL70
, p.76]
. Moreover from
[
GGPS69
, p.151]
,
\lambda_{E/F}
is the Weil constant.
It follows that

\frac{2{\gamma}(1,\eta_{E},\psi)}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}={\lambda}_{E/F}

and therefore

(4.3.6)

\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)={\lambda}_{E/F}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}\big(\eta_{E}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{\chi})\big)\bigg)({\mathrm{tr}}(g)).

Notice that the above formula is also valid for
E
split over
F
. Precisely, when
E
is split over
F
,
{\lambda}_{E/F}=1=\eta_{E}
, and hence
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}=\phi_{\chi}
which recovers the distribution character for principal series representations.

In the following, we will adapt Theorem
4.3.1
to
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
.

Theorem 4.3.4
.

The distribution character
\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}
of the dihedral representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
is supported on
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}=\{g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)\mid\det g\in\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})\}
. For any
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
with
\det g=a
,

\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)=\frac{2|\det(g)|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}\int^{*}_{E^{a}}\frac{\eta({\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e))}{|{\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)|}\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E,a}e.

Here
E^{a}=\{e\in E^{\times}\mid\mathrm{Nr}(e)=a\}
and
\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{a}}e
is the measure on
E^{a}
induced from the
E^{1}
-torsor structure. The regularized integral is understood as (
4.3.2
) and Remark
4.3.3
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to
[
GGPS69
]
, so we only sketch it below.

Since the stable distribution character
\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}
is locally integrable, it suffices to determine its value on the open dense Bruhat cell. Following
[
GGPS69
]
, we are going to write the operator
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)
as an integral operator on the underlying space of
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)={\mathrm{Ind}}^{{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)}_{{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}}{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
, which decomposes
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)\oplus{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)^{-}
as a dense subspace. Here
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
(resp.
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)^{-}
) consists of vectors in
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
supported on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
, resp.
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{-}
.

For
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{-}
, the operator
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)
permutes
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
and
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)^{-}
, hence
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}
vanishes for
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{-}
. It remains to determine
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}(g)
for
g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{+}
.

In the following, let us calculate the contribution to the trace of the operator
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)
on
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
, with

(4.3.7)

g=\begin{pmatrix}a&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}.

For
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
, by Lemma
4.2.3
,

\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}a&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\phi\bigg)(z)

is nonzero only when
a\in\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})
, and is equal to

\displaystyle=\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-v){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|v|}\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)|t|\psi(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x))\phi(tx)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x

when
a=\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a})
for some
e_{a}\in E^{\times}
. Changing variable
x\mapsto\frac{x}{t}
, the above identity is equal to

\displaystyle=\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-vt){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|vt|}\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}z-\frac{x}{t})}{v}\bigg)\psi(u\mathrm{Nr}(x))\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x.

Using the fact that
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
, the integral
x\in E
can be separated according to the short exact sequence
1\to E^{1}\to E^{\times}\to\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})\to 1
and is equal to

\displaystyle=\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-vt){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|vt|}\int_{y\in\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})}\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}z-\frac{e_{y}e}{t})}{v}\bigg)\psi(uy)\chi^{-1}(e)\phi(e_{y})\,\mathrm{d}e

where
y=\mathrm{Nr}(e_{y})
for some
e_{y}\in E^{\times}
and
\,\mathrm{d}y
is the additive Haar measure on the affine line. In particular, identifying
\phi
as a function on
\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})
, the above integral transformation admits an integral kernel

K_{{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)}(z,e_{y}e)=\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-vt){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|vt|}\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}z-\frac{e_{y}e}{t})}{v}\bigg)\psi(uy)\chi^{-1}(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

Hence the trace of the operator
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)
on
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)
can be calculated through integrating the kernel along the diagonal, which provides

(4.3.8)

=\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-vt){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|vt|}\int_{y\in\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})}^{*}\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}e_{y}-\frac{e_{y}e}{t})}{v}\bigg)\psi(uy)\chi^{-1}(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

Notice that the integral in
y\in\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})
is not absolutely convergent and regularization is needed, which are addressed carefully in
[
GGPS69
, Chap. 2, §5]
.

Similarly, for vectors supported on
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)^{-}
and hence lying in
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)^{-}
, the operator
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)
with
g
in the open dense Bruhat cell as in (
4.3.7
) has trace

(4.3.9)

=-\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-vt){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|vt|}\int_{y\in E^{\times}\backslash\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})}^{*}\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}e_{y}-\frac{e_{y}e}{t})}{v}\bigg)\psi(uy)\chi^{-1}(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e

on the space
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)^{-}
.
Combining (
4.3.8
) and (
4.3.9
) together, the trace of the operator
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)
is given by

\displaystyle\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)=

\displaystyle\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-vt){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|vt|}\int_{y\in F}^{*}\eta(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(\frac{y\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}-\frac{e}{t})}{v}\bigg)\psi(uy)\chi^{-1}(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a})\eta(-vt){\lambda}_{E/F}}{|vt|}\int_{y\in F}^{*}\eta(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(y\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}-\frac{e}{t})}{v}+u\bigg)\bigg)\chi^{-1}(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e

where we use the fact that
\eta|_{\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})}=1
and
\eta_{F^{\times}\backslash\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})}=-1
.

Changing variable
y\mapsto(-vt)y
, the above integral becomes

\displaystyle=|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a}){\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{y\in F}^{*}\eta(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(-y\big(t\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}-\frac{e}{t})+uvt\big)\bigg)\chi^{-1}(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

Since

\displaystyle t\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a}-\frac{e}{t})+uvt=ta+\frac{1}{t}+tuv-{\mathrm{tr}}(e_{a}\iota(e))={\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e_{a}\iota(e))

and
\iota(e)=e^{-1}
for
\mathrm{Nr}(e)=1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)=

\displaystyle|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}\chi(e_{a}){\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{y\in F}^{*}\eta(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{1}}\psi\bigg(-y\big({\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e_{a}e)\big)\bigg)\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}{\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{y\in F}^{*}\eta(y)\psi\big(-y{\mathrm{tr}}(g)\big)\,\mathrm{d}y\int_{e\in E^{a}}\psi\big(y{\mathrm{tr}}(e)\big)\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E,a}e.

Now following the same idea as Remark
4.3.3
, after descending down to the trace variable, the integration in
e\in E^{a}
can be viewed as the Fourier transform of a compactly supported absolutely integrable function, which in particular is smooth and globally bounded in
y
. Hence the regularized integral
\int^{*}_{y\in F}
can be understood as the Fourier inversion of a tempered distribution. Following the same argument as Remark
4.3.3
, by Tate’s thesis,

\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)=\frac{|a|^{\frac{1}{2}}{\lambda}_{E/F}}{{\gamma}(1,\eta,\psi)}\int_{E^{a}}^{*}\frac{\eta({\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e))}{|{\mathrm{tr}}(g)-{\mathrm{tr}}(e)|}\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E,a}e.

For details on the convergence issue we refer to
[
GGPS69
, Chap. 2, §5, p.207]
. The final equality follows from

\frac{2{\gamma}(1,\eta,\psi)}{{\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1},\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}})}={\lambda}_{E/F},

which can be found from the equation above (
4.3.6
).
∎

Remark 4.3.5
.

Returning to the formula (
4.3.5
), we note that the formula expressing the stable character of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
is naturally in the form of an additive convolution in the trace variable. Because after the Fourier transform, the additive convolution becomes a multiplication operator, we can rewrite the Gelfand-Graev stable character for
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
in a convenient form

(4.3.10)

\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)={\lambda}_{E/F}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}\big(\eta_{E}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{\chi})\big)\bigg)\big({\mathrm{tr}}(g)\big)

where
\phi_{\chi}=\frac{\chi+\chi^{\iota}}{{\Delta}}
is the function supported in the subset
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1})
of
F
defined in (
4.3.4
). The formula for the character of the dihedral representations of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
now becomes

(4.3.11)

\theta_{\chi}^{\rm st}(g)={\lambda}_{E/F}|\det g|^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}\big(\eta_{E}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{\chi})\big)\bigg)\big({\mathrm{tr}}(g),\det(g)\big)

where the Fourier transform is taken over the trace variable. It is also worth noticing that the above stable character formula holds automatically when
E
is split over
F
since the transforms on the right hand side of (
4.3.10
) becomes the identity map.

Remark 4.3.6

(Gelfand-Graev formula for the stable transfer)
.

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
(resp.
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
). For any étale quadratic
F
-algebra
E
, let
T_{E}\simeq E^{1}
(resp.
E^{\times}
) be a fixed maximal torus determined by
E
. The adjoint of the Gelfand-Graev transfer
\chi\mapsto\theta_{\chi}
is then a linear map

(4.3.12)

{\mathcal{T}}_{E}:\mathrm{SO}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{H}}_{T_{E}}

given by the same kernel but read in the opposite direction. When
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, it maps every function
f\in{\rm SO}({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F))
to the function

(4.3.13)

{\mathcal{T}}_{E}(f)(e)=\lambda_{E/F}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(\eta_{E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(f)\big)({\mathrm{tr}}_{E}(e)).

In other words, we have

(4.3.14)

{\mathcal{T}}_{E}(f)=\lambda_{E/F}{\mathrm{tr}}_{E}^{*}\left({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(\eta_{E}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(f)\big)\right).

We note that the latter formula also works for the split torus. In the split case
E=F\times F
and
\eta_{E}
is the trivial character of
F^{\times}
. As a result, we have the stable transfer is simply given by the restriction to the locus of hyperbolic trace
{\mathrm{tr}}(E_{0}^{1})
i.e. elements
c\in F
of the form
c=t+t^{-1}
with
t\in F^{\times}
.

5.
The Lafforgue transform

In this section, we construct natural maps between the Bernstein center and the Kazhdan cocenter based on representation theoretic considerations. We stipulate that when we restrict this construction to the stable part, it can be expressed as an integral transform that we call the Lafforgue transform. In the remaining subsections, we investigate the convergence property of the Lafforgue transform in the case of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
. We show that all distributions in the stable Bernstein center of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
supported on finitely many connected components of the Bernstein variety enjoy a vanishing property based on explicit calculations of Moy and Tadic, which implies the convergence of the Lafforgue transform. We also establish the archimedean variant.

5.1.
Representation theoretic definition

For a reductive group
G
over a non-archimedean local field
F
, the set
{\rm Irr}(G(F))
of irreducible representations of
G(F)
can be equipped with a canonical structure of algebraic variety over
\mathbb{C}
, called the Lafforgue variety. This construction has been realized in Psaromiglikos’ thesis
[
Psa23
]
. We have an algebraic map
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G(F))\to\Omega(G(F))
from the Lafforgue variety
{\mathrm{Irr}}(G(F))
to the Bernstein variety
\Omega(G(F))
whose
\mathbb{C}
-points are irreducible representations of
G(F)
up to inertial equivalence. The Bernstein center
\mathcal{Z}
is the ring of regular functions on
\Omega(G(F))
and a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear surjective map from the Kazhdan cocenter to the ring of regular functions on
{\rm Irr}(G(F))
.

By construction, there exists an open dense subvariety
\Omega^{\prime}(G)
of
\Omega(G)
consisting of supercuspidal representations of Levi subgroup (up to inertial equivalence) whose parabolic induction to
G(F)
is irreducible, its projection from the inverse image
{\rm Irr}^{\prime}(G(F))
in
{\rm Irr}(G(F))
is an isomorphism. For every element
f\in\mathcal{C}
viewed as a regular function on
{\rm Irr}(G(F))
, we obtain by restriction a function
f^{\prime}
on
{\rm Irr}^{\prime}(G(F))=\Omega^{\prime}(G(F))
. One can show that the function
f^{\prime}
extends uniquely to a regular function on
\Omega(G(F))
that we denote
\mathrm{Laf}(f)
. We thus have a map

(5.1.1)

\mathrm{Laf}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{Z}

which is
\mathcal{Z}
-linear that is an isomorphism after restricting to
{\Omega}^{\prime}(G(F))\simeq\mathrm{Irr}^{\prime}(G(F))
. Explicitly, for any cuspidal datum
(M,{\sigma})\in{\Omega}
with
M
a Levi subgroup of
G
and
{\sigma}
a supercuspidal representation of
M(F)
,

\mathrm{Laf}(f)(M,{\sigma})=f\big({\mathrm{Ind}}({\sigma})\big).

where
{\mathrm{Ind}}
is the parabolic induction from
M(F)
to
G(F)
. In general,
\mathrm{Laf}
is only surjective but not injective. For instance, when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
,
\mathrm{Laf}
has one dimensional kernel generated by the indicator function on the Steinberg representation of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
, which, as an element in
{\mathcal{C}}
, is given by the normalized elliptic character of the Steinberg representation (= orbital integral of the pseudo coefficient of the Steinberg representation). In particular for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, after localization,
\mathrm{Laf}
is an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module away from the Steinberg representation component.

After restricting to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{fin}}
,
\mathrm{Laf}
induces a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-linear morphism

\mathrm{Laf}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{fin}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{fin}}.

Moreover,
\mathrm{Laf}
sends
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
into
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
. To make it precise, elements in
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
are represented by the stable orbital integral of test functions. On the other hand, distributions in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{fin}}
are represented by invariant locally integrable functions on
G(F)
(
[
MT02
, Thm. 2.5]
). It follows that the elements in
\mathrm{Laf}({\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}})
are represented by invariant locally integrable functions on
G(F)
that are also stably invariant, which therefore are represented by stably invariant functions on
G(F)
.

In general, since
\mathrm{Laf}
is not injective, there are different choices of sections for
\mathrm{Laf}
. For the purpose of this paper, when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, we consider the following section
\mathrm{LafSec}:{\mathcal{Z}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
:

•

Let
{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv}}
be the subspace of
{\mathcal{C}}
that are orthogonal to one-dimensional characters of
G(F)
, i.e.

\langle f,\chi\rangle=0,\quad\text{ $\chi$ a $1$-dimensional character of $G(F)$}.

Then there exists a unique section
\mathrm{LafSec}:{\mathcal{Z}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\perp,\mathrm{triv}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}
such that

\mathrm{LafSec}(z)\big({\mathrm{Ind}}({\sigma})\big)=z(M,{\sigma})

for any cuspidal datum
(M,{\sigma})
. Indeed, the induced representations
{\mathrm{Ind}}({\sigma})
are either irreducible or admit a filtration given by twisted Steinberg representations and
1
-dimensional characters, which furnishes the proof.

On the other hand, when restricted to finite and stable locus, elements in
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
can be realized as functions on the Steinberg-Hitchin base
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
. One of our main results in this paper is to show that, for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, when restricted to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin},\perp,\mathrm{triv}}
, both
\mathrm{LafSec}
and
\mathrm{Laf}
can be realized as explicit integral transforms on the Steinberg-Hitchin base
{\mathfrak{c}}(F)
. The integral transforms appear first in an unpublished note of Lafforgue for a different purpose (
[
Laf14
]
). We use the name of Lafforgue for the linear map
\mathrm{Laf}
, which has deep representation theoretic significance, because in the case of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, its stable part is given by Lafforgue’s integral formula, and in fact, attempting to understand conceptually Lafforgue’s mysterious formula was a motivation for this work.

Theorem 5.1.1
.

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
over a local field
F
of residual characteristic not equal to two. For any distribution
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st,fin}
lying in the stable Bernstein center and supported on finitely many Bernstein components, there exists a stably invariant function
\mathrm{J}_{G}=\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c}
such that
z=\mathrm{J}_{G}\,\mathrm{d}g
. Then
\mathrm{LafSec}(z)
is given as a function
f
on the Steinberg-Hitchin base by the following formula, where the Fourier transform and
|\cdot|
are in trace variable,

(5.1.2)

\mathrm{LafSec}(z)={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\left(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|.|}\right).

It is not at all obvious that the expression on the right-hand side makes sense. This is what we will prove in the next subsection based on some explicit formulas in the case
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
due to Moy and Tadic. The proof of this theorem will be achieved in subsection
6.1
.

For general reductive group
G
, we expect that there always exists a distinguished section
\mathrm{LafSec}:{\mathcal{Z}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
of the Lafforgue transform
\mathrm{Laf}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
, such that when restricted to
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
, both transforms can be realized by explicit integral transforms on the Steinberg-Hitchin base of
G
. In a forthcoming work, we propose a conjectural formula for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{n}
generalizing Theorem
5.1.1
.

5.2.
Supercuspidal component of the Lafforgue transform

In this subsection, we study the image under
\mathrm{LafSec}
of the supercuspidal idempotent for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
. A supercuspidal representation of
G(F)
is represented by an isolated point in the Bernstein variety
\Omega
. The associated idempotent
\epsilon_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{Z}}
is defined as the function on
\Omega
taking the value one at
\pi
and
0
on other components. We can express
\epsilon_{\pi}
and its Lafforgue transform using the distributional character of the representation
\pi
.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

(1)

The idempotent
\epsilon_{\pi}
is given by the formula

\epsilon_{\pi}=\Theta_{\pi}d_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}g

where the distributional character
\Theta_{\pi}
of
\pi
is a generalized function,
d_{\pi}
is the formal degree which depends on the choice of the Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}g
but the product
d_{\pi}\,\mathrm{d}g
is independent of that choice.

(2)

As function on the set of regular semisimple conjugacy classes
C^{\prime}(F)
we have

\mathrm{LafSec}(\epsilon_{\pi})=\Delta{\bf v}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\rm ell}\Theta_{\pi}

where
\Delta
is the square root of the absolute value of the Weyl discriminant,
{\mathbbm{1}}_{\rm ell}
is the characteristic function of the elliptic regular semi-simple locus, and
\mathbf{v}
is the inverse of the volume function defined on the elliptic regular semi-simple locus determined by (
3.1.9
), i.e. following (
3.1.9
), for a quadratic field extension
E_{\alpha}
of
F
and
e\in E_{\alpha}^{1}
,
\mathbf{v}(e)={\mathrm{vol}}(E^{1}_{\alpha},{\omega}_{T_{\alpha}})^{-1}
. We will call the right-hand side the normalized elliptic character of
\pi
.

Proof.

(1) This is a result of Moy and Tadic
[
MT02
, 3.1]
.

(2) By definition,
\mathrm{LafSec}({\epsilon}_{\pi})
is the unique element in the cocenter of
G(F)
that vanishes on all subrepresentations of principal series representations and supercuspidal representations, except takes value one on the supercuspidal representation
\pi
. As a result,
\mathrm{Laf}({\epsilon}_{\pi})
is equal to the orbital integral of normalized matrix coefficient attached to
\pi
. The final identity follows from
[
Art93
, Thm. 5.1]
specialized to
M=G
. Notice that
{\Delta}
and
\mathbf{v}
show up due to the normalization of the measures introduced in subsection
3.1
.
∎

5.3.
Discrete series representations and Chebyshev polynomials

In this section, we would like to study the analogue of Theorem
5.2.1
over the real field. In particular we would like to highlight the connection between Theorem
6.1.1
for discrete series representations of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
and the Chebyshev polynomials. Let
F={\mathbb{R}}
and
E={\mathbb{C}}
. For any integer
\ell\geq 1
, let
\Theta_{\ell}=\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\ell}
be the stable discrete series character of weight
\ell
, and let
d_{\ell}
be its formal degree which is equal to
\ell
.

By the orthogonality of elliptic discrete series characters, it is immediate to see that Corollary
6.1.2
is equivalent to the following fact:

Theorem 5.3.1
.

The Lafforgue transform of
d_{\ell}\Theta_{\ell}
equals
\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\Theta_{\ell}^{\mathrm{ell}}
. Equivalently,

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(d_{\ell}\Theta_{\ell})}{|\cdot|}\bigg)=\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\Theta_{\ell}^{\mathrm{ell}}.

Here
\mathbf{v}=2\pi
, and
{\Delta}
is the square-root of the norm of Weyl discriminant.

Proof.

Let
\phi_{\ell}
be the function on the Steinberg-Hitchin base supported on the trace of elliptic maximal torus of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
such that

\phi_{\ell}(x)=\frac{e^{i\ell\theta}+e^{-i\ell\theta}}{|e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}|},\quad x=e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta}.

The Gelfand-Graev character identity (
4.3.6
) shows that (switching
\psi
to
\psi^{-1}
)

(5.3.1)

\Theta_{\ell}=\overline{{\lambda}}_{{\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(\eta_{\mathbb{C}}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\ell})\big)\bigg).

Plugging (
5.3.1
) into the statement of Theorem
5.3.1
, and using the fact that
\eta_{\mathbb{C}}(x)|x|={\mathrm{sgn}}(x)|x|=x
, Theorem
5.3.1
can be reformulated as follows:

{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\ell})}{(\cdot)}\bigg)={\lambda}_{{\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}}}d_{\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\Theta^{\mathrm{ell}}_{\ell}.

Equivalently,

\phi_{\ell}={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}\bigg((\cdot){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big({\lambda}_{{\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}}}d_{\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\Theta_{\ell}^{\mathrm{ell}}\big)\bigg).

Since the Fourier transform sends multiplication by
2\pi ix
to derivative, the above identity is equivalent to the following

(2\pi i)d_{\ell}\cdot\phi_{\ell}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\big({\lambda}_{{\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}}}\mathbf{v}{\Delta}\Theta_{\ell}^{\mathrm{ell}}\big)}{\,\mathrm{d}x}

From
[
JL70
, Lem. 1.2]
,
{\lambda}_{{\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}}}=i
, therefore we are reduced to show

d_{\ell}\cdot\phi_{\ell}=\ell\cdot\phi_{\ell}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\big({\Delta}\Theta_{\ell}^{\mathrm{ell}}\big)}{\,\mathrm{d}x}.

Write
x=e^{i\theta}+e^{-i\theta}=2\cos\theta
, and
{\Delta}(x)=|e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}|=\sqrt{4-x^{2}}
, then

\phi_{\ell}(x)=\frac{e^{i\ell\theta}+e^{-i\ell\theta}}{{\Delta}(x)}=\frac{2\cos\ell\theta}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}.

It turns out that
\phi_{\ell}(x)
is related to the well-known Chebyshev polynomials. We recall their definitions and basic properties below.

(1)

Chebyshev polynomials of the first kind
{\mathrm{T}}_{n}
are defined by

{\mathrm{T}}_{n}(\cos\theta)=\cos(n\theta);

Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind
{\mathrm{U}}_{n}
are defined by

{\mathrm{U}}_{n}(\cos\theta)=\frac{\sin(n+1)\theta}{\sin\theta};

(2)

Moreover, the derivative of
{\mathrm{U}}_{n}(x)
enjoys the following identity

\frac{\,\mathrm{d}{\mathrm{U}}_{n}(x)}{\,\mathrm{d}x}=\frac{(n+1){\mathrm{T}}_{n+1}(x)-x{\mathrm{U}}_{n}(x)}{x^{2}-1}.

These facts can be deduced by straightforward computation.

Based on the above facts we have

d_{\ell}\phi_{\ell}(x)=\ell\cdot\frac{2{\mathrm{T}}_{\ell}(x/2)}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}},\quad|x|<2.

Similarly, from
[
Lan13
, (1.3.1)]
,

\Theta_{\ell}^{\mathrm{ell}}(x)=-\frac{e^{i\ell\theta}-e^{-i\ell\theta}}{e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}}=-\frac{\sin\ell\theta}{\sin\theta}=-{\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2),\quad|x|<2,

hence we are reduced to show the following identity

\displaystyle 2\ell\cdot\frac{{\mathrm{T}}_{\ell}(x/2)}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}=\frac{-\,\mathrm{d}\bigg(\sqrt{4-x^{2}}{\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2)\bigg)}{\,\mathrm{d}x},\quad|x|<2.

By direct calculation, the right hand side is equal to

\frac{x}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2)-\frac{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{2}{\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}^{\prime}(x/2).

Applying the derivative for Chebyshev polynomials above, we have

{\mathrm{U}}^{\prime}_{\ell-1}(x/2)=\frac{\ell{\mathrm{T}}_{\ell}(x/2)-(x/2){\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2)}{(x/2)^{2}-1}

Therefore we are reduced to show

2\ell\cdot\frac{{\mathrm{T}}_{\ell}(x/2)}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}=\frac{x}{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2)-\frac{\sqrt{4-x^{2}}}{2}\bigg(\frac{\ell{\mathrm{T}}_{\ell}(x/2)-(x/2){\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2)}{(x/2)^{2}-1}\bigg).

After multiplying
\sqrt{4-x^{2}}
on both sides, it is equivalent to the following identity

2\ell{\mathrm{T}}_{\ell}(x/2)=x{\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2)-\bigg(x{\mathrm{U}}_{\ell-1}(x/2)-2\ell{\mathrm{T}}_{\ell}(x/2)\bigg)

which holds automatically. We thus complete the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.4.
The image of the stable Lafforgue transform

In this subsection, we describe the image of
\mathrm{LafSec}
for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
over a non-archimedean local field of odd residual characteristic.

Theorem 5.4.1
.

The image of
\mathrm{LafSec}:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}
is the subspace
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st,\perp triv}
of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st}
orthogonal to the character of the trivial representation.

Proof.

We only need to establish the theorem for the non-unital subalgebra of finite support
\mathrm{LafSec}:{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
.

First, by Part (2) of Theorem
5.5.1
, for any
z=(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(0)=0
. From here we deduce that
\mathrm{Laf}(z)
is orthogonal to the trivial character, i.e. the Fourier transform of
\mathrm{Laf}(z)
, as a function on the Steinberg-Hitchin base, vanishes at zero. It follows that the image of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
lands in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm st,fin,\perp triv}
. It remains to show the surjectivity. In order to show the surjectivity, not surprisingly we will use the trace Paley-Wiener theorem (
[
BDK86
]
). By the trace Paley-Wiener theorem, any element in the usual cocenter
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{fin}}={\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
is uniquely characterized by linear functionals
\ell
on
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))
such that for any character
\chi_{0}
of
T(F)
,
\chi\in{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\ell({\mathrm{Ind}}\chi\otimes\chi_{0})
is a regular function. Here
T
is the diagonal split torus of
G
,
{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T)
is the rational character group of
T
, and
{\mathrm{Ind}}
is the induced representation functor attached to the characters of
T(F)
. Passing to the stable cocenter, by Theorem
3.5.1
, since stable tempered characters are dense in the space of stable distributions, the stable cocenter can be characterized by linear functionals
\ell
on
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))
that are in the usual cocenter and are constant on tempered local
L
-packets.

Write
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))=\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{scusp}}\bigsqcup\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{ind}}
, where
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{scusp}}
are supercuspidal representations of
G(F)
, and
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{ind}}
are irreducible representations of
G(F)
whose cuspidal support are not supercuspidal. By linearity, we can divide the discussion to linear functionals on
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))
that are supported on
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{scusp}}
and
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{ind}}
separately. We first treat the supercuspidal situation.

By the trace Paley-Wiener theorem and linearity, for regular functions on
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))
supported on supercuspidal component only (actually they are indicator functions since
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
), they are given by the linear combination of the orbital integral of matrix coefficients of supercuspial representations. Passing to the stable cocenter, by Theorem
5.2.1
and its stable variant, they are given by the image of the stable Lafforgue transform of stable supercuspidal distribution characters. Hence the image of the stable Lafforgue transform contains all the elements in the stable cocenter supported on supercuspidal component only.

It remains to treat
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{ind}}
. The tempered local
L
-packet for irreducible representations are given by (the restriction of) fully induced tempered principal series of
G(F)
, except the Steinberg representation. Hence when restricted to
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{ind}}
, there are no difference between the stable cocenter and the usual cocenter. In particular, up to linear combination, after fixing a ramified (or trivial) character
\chi_{0}
of
T(F)
, any linear functional
\ell
on
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{ind}}
in the usual (stable) cocenter are determined uniquely by a regular function on
{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T)
, which in turn is uniquely determined by the same function on the Bernstein component attached to the ramified (or trivial) character
\chi_{0}
. It follows that the image of the stable Lafforgue transform also contains the (stable) cocenter supported on
\mathrm{Irr}(G(F))_{\mathrm{ind}}
.

It follows that we complete the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 5.4.2
.

Similar results hold for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, except one need to add the twist by central characters for trivial representations and Steinberg representations.

5.5.
Analytic properties of the function
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}

In this subsection, we establish some analytic properties of the function
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
on the Steinberg-Hitchin base, which will be useful in proving the descent formula. These analytic properties are established by explicit calculations. When
F
is non-archimedean, the calculation is based on explicit formulae of Moy and Tadic.

Theorem 5.5.1
.

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}

(
resp.
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2})
and the residual characteristic of
F
is other than two, then the following statements hold.

(1)

For any
\mathrm{J}_{G}\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
, there is an absolutely integrable function (resp. absolutely integrable function that is rapidly decreasing on the determinant factor)
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
on
{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}(F)\simeq F
(resp.
\simeq F\times F^{\times}
), such that
\mathrm{J}_{G}=(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c})\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}g
, where
\mathbf{c}:G\to(G\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}{\mathrm{Ad}}(G))={\mathfrak{c}}_{G}
is the Chevalley quotient map given by the trace function (resp. given by the (trace, determinant) function);

(2)

The Fourier inversion of
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
in trace variable, denoted as
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})
, is represented by a globally bounded smooth function vanishing at zero;

(3)

\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|}
is absolutely integrable on
F
. As a result, for any étale quadratic
F
-algebra
K
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\big(\frac{\eta_{K}}{|\cdot|}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})\big)
is globally bounded on
F
.

Remark 5.5.2
.

Since the orbital integrals
(
normalized by the square-root of the norm of Weyl discriminant
)
are locally bounded
(
[
BP15
, §1.8]
)
, Part
(1)
ensures that the distribution
\mathrm{J}_{G}
is locally integrable on
G(F)
.

Following the computation in
[
MT02
, §3]
, when
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
and
F
is non-archimedean, the Mellin transform of a regular function along the center of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
will cut out a compactly supported locus on the determinant factor. Similar statement is true for
F
archimedean since the entire functions on the admissible dual are still rapidly decay along the vertical strip when restricted to the determinant factor. Hence we only prove the case for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and leave the details for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
to the reader.

We first prove the theorem for
F
non-archimedean.

Proof.

By
[
MT02
, Thm. 2.5]
, any distributions supported on finitely many Bernstein components of
G(F)
are locally integrable. Moreover,
[
MT02
, §3]
provides explicit bases for
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{\mathrm{fin}}
which are presented as follows:

•

Cuspidal component (
[
MT02
, §3.1]
): For any supercuspidal representation
\pi
of
G(F)
, a basis is given by
e_{\pi}=\check{\Theta}_{\pi}
where
\check{\Theta}_{\pi}
is the contragredient of the supercuspidal character of
\pi
;

•

Regular non-cuspidal component (
[
MT02
, §3.4]
): For a character
{\lambda}_{0}:{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
with
{\lambda}_{0}^{2}\neq 1
, and any integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, up to constant, the basis distribution
e_{n}
is supported on split elements with

\displaystyle e_{n}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}0&k\neq\pm n\\
\frac{{\lambda}_{0}(o^{-1})}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}&k=n\\
\frac{{\lambda}_{0}(o)}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}&k=-n\end{matrix}\quad\text{if $n\neq 0$}

\displaystyle e_{0}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}0&k\neq 0\\
\frac{{\lambda}_{0}(o)+{\lambda}_{0}(o^{-1})}{|o^{2}-1|}&k=0\end{matrix}

where
o\in{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}
;

•

Irregular ramified principal series component (
[
MT02
, §3.5]
): Let
{\lambda}_{0}
be a nontrivial character of
{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}
of order two. For any positive integer
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, up to constant, the basis distribution
e_{n}
is supported on split elements with

e_{n}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}0&k\neq\pm n\\
\frac{{\lambda}_{0}(o)}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}&k=\pm n\end{matrix}

•

Unramified principal series component (
[
MT02
, §3.6]
and corrections in
[
MT05b
]
): For any non-negative integer
n\geq 0
, up to constant, the basis distribution
e_{n}=e^{\prime}_{n}+e^{\prime\prime}_{n}
is given by

\displaystyle e^{\prime\prime}_{n}(g)

\displaystyle=(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})\Theta_{\mathrm{St}_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}}(g)

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})\big(\frac{q^{k}+q^{-k}}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}-1\big),&g\text{ conjugate to }{\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1})\\
-(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n}),&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

and
e^{\prime}_{n}
supported on split elements given by

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{n}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))=\frac{q(q+1)}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}(I_{n+k}+I_{n-k}),\quad I_{l}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}-q^{-|l|-1}\frac{(q-1)}{(q+1)}&l\neq 0\\
\frac{2}{q(q+1)}&l=0\end{matrix}

Hence any distributions in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}_{G}
are actually represented by stable functions, and we derive the following explicit formulas for a basis of distributions in
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}_{G}
.

Theorem 5.5.3

(
[
MT02
]
[
MT05b
]
)
.

We have the following basis family of locally integrable stable distributions in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
:

•

Cuspidal component: For any supercuspidal representation
\pi
of
G(F)
,
b_{\pi}=
stable distribution character of
\pi
.

•

Ramified principal series component: Fix a nontrivial character
{\lambda}_{0}:{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. If
{\lambda}_{0}^{2}
is nontrivial, then for any nonzero integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
b_{{\lambda}_{0},n}
is supported on split elements with

b_{{\lambda}_{0},n}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}0&k\neq\pm n\\
\frac{{\lambda}_{0}(o^{-1})}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}&k=n\\
\frac{{\lambda}_{0}(o)}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}&k=-n\end{matrix}

If
{\lambda}_{0}^{2}
is nontrivial and
n=0
, or
{\lambda}_{0}^{2}
is trivial,

b_{{\lambda}_{0},n}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}0&k\neq\pm n\\
\frac{{\lambda}_{0}(o^{-1})+{\lambda}_{0}(o)}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}&k=\pm n\\
\end{matrix}

•

Unramified principal series component: For any non-negative integer
n\geq 0
,
b_{n}=b^{\prime}_{n}+b^{\prime\prime}_{n}
with

\displaystyle b^{\prime\prime}_{n}(g)

\displaystyle=(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})\Theta_{\mathrm{St}_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}}(g)

\displaystyle=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})\big(\frac{q^{k}+q^{-k}}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}-1\big),&g\text{ conjugate to }{\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1})\\
-(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n}),&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

and
b^{\prime}_{n}
supported on split elements given by

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{n}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))=\frac{q(q+1)}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}(I_{n+k}+I_{n-k}),\quad I_{l}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}-q^{-|l|-1}\frac{(q-1)}{(q+1)}&l\neq 0\\
\frac{2}{q(q+1)}&l=0\end{matrix}

Notice that
|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|={\Delta}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))
where
{\Delta}
is the square-root of the norm of the Weyl discrimiant.

Based on Theorem
5.5.3
, we only need to establish Theorem
5.5.1
for each case individually.

For Part (1) of Theorem
5.5.1
, it follows from the above explicit formula. Precisely, based on the above explicit basis, for any
\mathrm{J}_{G}=\big(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c}\big)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g
,
{\Delta}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is both compactly supported and globally bounded. Notice that for cuspidal component, one can find the explicit supercuspidal character table for
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
from
[
ADSS11
, p.6, p.7]
, which shows that they are supported on compact locus and are globally bounded after multiplying by
{\Delta}
. Hence the absolute integrability of
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
follows from the local integrability of
\frac{1}{{\Delta}}
. As a result
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})
is continuous and globally bounded.

It remains to establish Part (2) and Part (3) of Theorem
5.5.1
.

(I) We first treat the cuspidal component. Since we assume that the residual characteristic of
F
is not two, following subsection
4.3
, up to constant, we may assume that
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}={\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{E}(\phi_{\chi})
where
E
is an étale quadratic field extension of
F
and
\chi:E^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
is a nontrivial character. From the work of Harish-Chandra, we know that
{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{E}(\phi_{\chi})
is locally integrable and smooth over generic locus.

From Remark
4.3.3
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})=\eta_{E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\chi})
. Since
\frac{1}{{\Delta}}=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Tr}^{2}-4|^{1/2}}
is locally integrable on
F
,
\phi_{\chi}
is a compactly supported and locally integrable function. Therefore
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\chi})
is represented by a smooth function on
F
. Moreover, by definition,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\chi})(0)=\int_{F}\phi_{\chi}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{E_{1}}\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E,1}e=0
. Hence
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\chi})
is a smooth function on
F
vanishing at zero. Since we are working with non-archimedean situation, it indicates that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\chi})
vanishes in a neighborhood of zero. In particular,
\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|}
is still a smooth function on
F
that vanishes at zero. This justifies the second part of the theorem.

Write
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})
and
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}}=\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}}{|\cdot|}
. By the above argument, for any étale quadratic
F
-algebra
K
,
\eta_{K}\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is locally integrable in a neighborhood of zero and globally bounded, hence
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\eta_{K}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}})
is a well-defined distribution. It remains to show that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\eta_{K}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}})
is locally integrable. To see this, since
\frac{1}{{\Delta}}=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Tr}^{2}-4|^{1/2}}
,
\phi_{\chi}
lies in
L^{p}(F,\,\mathrm{d}x)
for any
1\leq p<2
. By Hausdorff-Young inequality (
[
Fol95
, Thm. 4.27]
),
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}=\eta_{E}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\phi_{\chi})
lies
L^{q}(F,\,\mathrm{d}x)
with
1/p+1/q=1
. It follows that by Hölder’s inequality, for sufficiently small positive constant
{\varepsilon}>0
,

\int_{|\xi|\geq{\varepsilon}}\bigg|\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\xi)}{|\xi|}\eta_{K}(\xi)\bigg|\,\mathrm{d}\xi\leq\bigg(\int_{|\xi|\geq{\varepsilon}}|\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{1/q}\cdot\bigg(\int_{|\xi|\geq{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{|x|^{p}}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg)^{1/p}<\infty

for any
1<p<2
i.e. the Fourier transform of
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}}\cdot\eta_{K}\cdot\mathrm{ch}_{|\xi|\geq{\varepsilon}}
is uniformly bounded, where
\mathrm{ch}_{|\xi|\geq{\varepsilon}}
is the (unnormalized) characteristic function of
\{\xi\in F\mid|\xi|\geq{\varepsilon}\}
. On the other hand, due to the integrability of
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}}\cdot\eta_{K}\cdot\mathrm{ch}_{|\xi|\leq{\varepsilon}}
, the Fourier transform of
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}}\cdot\eta_{K}\cdot\mathrm{ch}_{|\xi|\leq{\varepsilon}}
is a globally bounded smooth function. Therefore
\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}}{|\cdot|}
is the summation of two integrable function which is therefore still integrable. Hence
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\eta_{K}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{J}}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{c}})
is a globally bounded function which is clearly locally integrable.

Notice that the above proof also works for the stable distribution characters of the discrete series representations of
G({\mathbb{R}})
.

(II) Next we treat the ramified principal series component. Write
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}=b_{{\lambda}_{0},n}
. The local integrability of
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
follows from the local integrability of
\frac{1}{{\Delta}}
. Moreover, in this situation,
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is actually compactly supported on
F
, and the integration of
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
on
F
can be pulled back to the integration of the character
{\lambda}_{0}
on the split torus using (
4.3.3
). The vanishing of the integral
\int_{F}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi
follows from the fact that
{\lambda}_{0}
is nontrivial. The rest arguments are the same as the cuspidal situation and we omit.

(III) Finally we treat the unramified principal series component. After dividing
b_{n}
by
-(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})
, we may write

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathrm{tr}}(g))=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}\big(1-\frac{q^{k}+q^{-k}}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}\big)+\frac{\widetilde{I}_{n+k}+\widetilde{I}_{n-k}}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|},&g={\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1})\\
1&g\text{ elliptic}\end{matrix}

where

\widetilde{I}_{l}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}\frac{q^{-|l|}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}&l\neq 0\\
-\frac{2}{(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})}&l=0\end{matrix}

We first show that
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is actually compactly supported. Essentially we only need to show that
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is compactly supported on split locus. By symmetry let us assume that
k>n
(or
k<-n
), then
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathrm{tr}}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1})))
is equal to

=\big(1-\frac{q^{k}+q^{-k}}{q^{k}}\big)+q^{-k}\big(\frac{q^{-(n+k)}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}+\frac{q^{-(k-n)}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}\big)=-q^{-2k}+q^{-2k}=0.

It follows that
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is compactly supported on
F
. Moreover it is integrable on
F
since
{\Delta}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is globally bounded. Hence
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})
is represented by a smooth function.

Next we show that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(0)=\int_{\xi\in F}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=0
. The proof follows from tedious calculation. We first explain our idea for
n=0
. Using the above formula, when
n=0

\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathrm{tr}}(g))=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}1-\frac{2q}{(q-1)|o^{2}-1|}&g={\rm diag}(o,o^{-1}),o\in{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}\\
1&g\text{ elliptic}\end{matrix}

We can break
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
as
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}+\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}
with
\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}
is identically
=1
on compact elements, and hence is equal to the characteristic function of
{\mathfrak{o}}\subset F
, and
\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}
is supported on (compact) split elements given by

\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\mathrm{tr}}(g))=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}-\frac{2q}{(q-1)|o^{2}-1|}&g={\rm diag}(o,o^{-1}),o\in{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}\\
0&g\text{ elliptic}\end{matrix}

Therefore after descending down to the trace variable and with the help of (
4.3.3
),

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\in F}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=

\displaystyle\int_{|\xi|\leq 1}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi-\int_{\xi=o+o^{-1}}\frac{2q}{(q-1)|o^{2}-1|}\,\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}})-\frac{q}{(q-1)}\int_{o\in{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}}\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}o=1-\frac{q}{q-1}(1-q^{-1})=0.

It follows that we complete the vanishing statement for
n=0
.

Next let us assume that
n\geq 1
. As the case of
n=0
, we break
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}+\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}
where
\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}
is supported on compact elements given by

\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}({\mathrm{tr}}(g))=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}(1-\frac{2}{|o^{2}-1|})+\frac{2\widetilde{I}_{n}}{|o^{2}-1|},&g={\rm diag}(o,o^{-1})\\
1,&g\text{ elliptic}\end{matrix}

and
\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}
is supported on non-compact elements given by

\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\mathrm{tr}}(g))=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}\big(1-\frac{q^{k}+q^{-k}}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}\big)+\frac{\widetilde{I}_{n+k}+\widetilde{I}_{n-k}}{|\varpi^{k}o^{2}-\varpi^{-k}|}&g={\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}),-n\leq k\leq n,k\neq 0\\
0&g\text{ elliptic or }g={\rm diag}(o,o^{-1})\end{matrix}

Explicitly, for
1\leq k\leq n-1
and
g={\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1})
,

\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\mathrm{tr}}(g))=-q^{-2k}+\frac{q^{-k}\big(q^{-(n+k)}+q^{-(n-k)}\big)}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}=\frac{-q^{-2k+n}+q^{-n}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}

For
k=n
and
g={\rm diag}(\varpi^{n}o,\varpi^{-n}o)
,

\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\mathrm{tr}}(g))=-q^{-2n}+q^{-n}\bigg(\frac{q^{-2n}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}-\frac{2}{(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\bigg).

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\in F}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=

\displaystyle\int_{\xi=t+t^{-1}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=\frac{1}{2}\int_{t\in F^{\times}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\rm diag}(t,t^{-1})){\Delta}({\rm diag}(t,t^{-1}))\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=-n,k\neq 0}^{n}\int_{o\in{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1})){\Delta}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}o

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times})\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k},\varpi^{-k})){\Delta}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k},\varpi^{-k})).

Notice that for
k\geq 1
,
{\Delta}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k}o,\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}))=|\varpi^{k}o-\varpi^{-k}o^{-1}|=q^{k}
. Moreover

\displaystyle{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times})\cdot\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{n},\varpi^{-n})){\Delta}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{n},\varpi^{-n}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-q^{-1})\bigg(-q^{-2n}+q^{-n}\big(\frac{q^{-2n}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}-\frac{2}{(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\big)\bigg)q^{n},

and

\displaystyle{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times})\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k},\varpi^{-k})){\Delta}({\rm diag}(\varpi^{k},\varpi^{-k}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-q^{-1})\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigg(\frac{q^{-n}-q^{n-2k}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}\bigg)q^{k}=\frac{(1-q^{-1})}{(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigg(q^{-n+k}-q^{n-k}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-q^{-1})}{(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\cdot\bigg(\frac{q^{1-n}-q-1+q^{n}}{1-q}\bigg)

It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\in F}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=

\displaystyle(1-q^{-1})\bigg(-q^{-2n}+q^{-n}\big(\frac{q^{-2n}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}-\frac{2}{(q-1)(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\big)\bigg)q^{n}

\displaystyle+\frac{(1-q^{-1})}{(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\cdot\bigg(\frac{q^{1-n}-q-1+q^{n}}{1-q}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-q^{-1})}{(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\bigg(\frac{q^{-n+1}+q^{n}}{1-q}\bigg).

Similarly, following the same idea as the case for
n=0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\in F}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=

\displaystyle\int_{|\xi|\leq 1}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\xi\in{\mathfrak{o}}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{\xi=o+o^{-1}}\frac{2\widetilde{I}_{n}-2}{|o^{2}-1|}\,\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\int_{o\in{\mathfrak{o}}^{\times}}\big(\frac{q^{-n}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}-1\big)\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}o=1-\frac{q^{n}}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}{\mathrm{vol}}({\mathfrak{o}}^{\times})=1-\frac{q^{n}(1-q^{-1})}{q^{n}+q^{-n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-q^{-1})}{(q^{n}+q^{-n})}\bigg(\frac{q^{-n+1}+q^{n}}{q-1}\bigg).

It follows that

\int_{\xi\in F}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\xi\in F}\big(\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},1}(\xi)+\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}},2}(\xi)\big)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=0

and hence
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})=0
