. The rest argument is the same as cuspidal situation and we omit.
∎

Archimedean variant

Now we establish Theorem
5.5.1
for
F
archimedean and
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
. When
F={\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathrm{J}_{G}=\big(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c}\big)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g
being equal to the stable discrete series character of
G(F)
, using the explicit character formula from
[
Lan13
, 1.3.1]
, we see that
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is absolutely integrable. The rest argument are the same as the proof of the cuspidal component in non-archimedean case. Notice that unlike the non-archimedean case where the vanishing of
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}
at zero implies that
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}
vanishes in a neighborhood of zero, for archimedean situation we only know that
\widehat{\mathrm{J}}_{\mathfrak{c}}
vanishes at zero, while its derivatives might no longer vanish at zero.

It remains to treat
\mathrm{J}_{G}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}_{G}
supported on finitely many principal series component.

Proof.

We first treat the case when
F={\mathbb{C}}
. The tempered dual of
G({\mathbb{C}})
is parametrized by
{\mathbb{Z}}\times i{\mathbb{R}}
. For
(n,{\lambda})\in{\mathbb{Z}}\times i{\mathbb{R}}
, the corresponding tempered principal series representation
\pi_{n,{\lambda}}\simeq\pi_{-n,-{\lambda}}
has distribution character given by

\Theta_{{\lambda},n}({\rm diag}(e^{t}e^{i\theta},e^{-t}e^{-i\theta}))=\frac{e^{{\lambda}t}e^{in\theta}+e^{-{\lambda}t}e^{-in\theta}}{|e^{t}e^{i\theta}-e^{-t}e^{-i\theta}|},\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},\theta\in[0,2\pi).

Here
e^{t}e^{i\theta}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
. Any fixed pairs
(n,{\lambda})
and
(-n,-{\lambda})
uniquely determine a fixed connected component of the tempered dual of
G({\mathbb{C}})
. By Weyl integration formula, for a test function
f
on
G({\mathbb{C}})
,

(5.5.1)

\Theta_{{\lambda},n}(f)=\int_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{\theta\in[0,2\pi)}e^{in\theta}e^{{\lambda}t}\mathrm{F}_{f}(e^{t}e^{i\theta})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{d}t

where
\mathrm{F}_{f}(e^{t}e^{i\theta})
is the orbital integral function (normalized by the square-root of Weyl discriminant) given by
={\Delta}({\rm diag}(e^{t}e^{i\theta},e^{-t}e^{-i\theta}))\int_{g\in G({\mathbb{C}})/T({\mathbb{C}})}f(g^{-1}{\rm diag}(e^{t}e^{i\theta},e^{-t}e^{-i\theta})g)\,\mathrm{d}g
and
T\subset G
is the diagonal split torus.
By
[
GGPS69
, p.123]
, up to constant, the Plancherel formula for
G({\mathbb{C}})
reads as follows:

{\delta}_{e}(g)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\Theta_{ix,n}(g)(n^{2}+x^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x.

Following the same idea as
[
MT02
, §3.4-§3.5]
, for a fixed integer
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
corresponding to components of tempered dual
\{n,-n\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, we are going to calculate the invariant distribution given by an entire function
\xi({\lambda})
that is rapidly decreasing function in vertical strip. Notice that when
n=0
, we need to assume that
k
is even since the regular functions need to be invariant under the involution
(n,{\lambda})\mapsto(-n,-{\lambda})
.

For notational convenience, we switch
{\lambda}
to
i{\lambda}
with
{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. For any
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
k\geq 0
, consider the invariant distribution
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\xi}
on
G({\mathbb{C}})
that is given by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\xi}*f(1)=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\xi({\lambda})\Theta_{i{\lambda},n}(f)+\xi(-{\lambda})\Theta_{i{\lambda},-n}(f)\bigg)(n^{2}+{\lambda}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\begin{matrix}2\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\xi({\lambda})\Theta_{i{\lambda},n}(f)(n^{2}+{\lambda}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}&(n\neq 0)\text{ or }(n=0,k\text{ even})\\
0&n=0,k\text{ odd}\end{matrix}

with
\xi({\lambda})
entire on
{\mathbb{C}}
and rapidly decay in bounded horizional strip (since we switch
{\lambda}
to
i{\lambda}
).
Using the formula (
5.5.1
),

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\xi({\lambda})\Theta_{i{\lambda},n}(f)(n^{2}+{\lambda}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\xi({\lambda})(n^{2}+{\lambda}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{\theta\in[0,2\pi)}e^{in\theta}e^{i{\lambda}t}\mathrm{F}_{f}(e^{t}e^{in\theta})\frac{\,\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{d}t.

In other words,
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\xi}
represents the following tempered distribution on
{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}(F)=F
:

(5.5.2)

As a tempered distribution on the trace variable
\in F={\mathbb{C}}
,
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\xi}
represents the following tempered distribution: For any Schwartz function
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{C}})
,

{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\xi}(\phi)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\xi({\lambda})(n^{2}+{\lambda}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{\theta\in[0,2\pi)}e^{in\theta}e^{i{\lambda}t}\phi(e^{t}e^{in\theta}+e^{-t}e^{-in\theta})\frac{\theta}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{d}t

i.e. after pulling back
\phi
along the trace map to get a Schwartz function
\phi(e^{t}e^{in\theta}+e^{-t}e^{-in\theta})
on
T({\mathbb{C}})
,
{\mathfrak{D}}_{n,\xi}
acts on
\phi
via the above formula.

The integration in
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
is compact and won’t affect the analytical issue. We focus on
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. After changing variable, essentially we are looking at the distribution

\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\xi({\lambda})(n^{2}+{\lambda}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}|x|^{i{\lambda}}\phi(x+x^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}x.

Now by Mellin inversion (
[
JL21
, Defin. 2.2]
,
[
Igu78
, Thm. 4.3]
),

x\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|x|^{i{\lambda}}\xi({\lambda})(n^{2}+{\lambda}^{2})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

represents a Schwartz function
f_{\xi}(x)
on
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})
, which is rapidly decreasing at both
0
and
\infty
. Hence the distribution becomes

(5.5.3)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\xi}(x)\phi(x+x^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}^{\times}x=\int_{|y|>2}\frac{f_{\xi}(\frac{-y+\sqrt{y^{2}-4}}{2})}{\sqrt{y^{2}-4}}\phi(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

Notice that the function
\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{|y|>2}f_{\xi}(\frac{-y+\sqrt{y^{2}-4}}{2})}{|\sqrt{y^{2}-4}|}
is integrable near
y=2
, and smooth of rapidly decay near infinity. Hence its Fourier transform is represented by a smooth function that is globally bounded. To show that the Fourier transform vanishes at zero, Finally to show that the Fourier transform vanishes at zero, it suffices to see that in (
5.5.2
), if we plug
\phi
to be the constant function, then for
n\neq 0
, the following integral vanishes identically

\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{in\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\theta=0.

For
n=0
, we are reduced to show that

\int_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{i{\lambda}t}\,\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}=0.

But it follows from Fourier inversion directly. In conclusion we finished the proof for Part (2) of Theorem
5.5.1
for this situation. For Part (3), following the same idea as before, up to the compact integration in
e^{i\theta}
, we only need to observe that in the equation (
5.5.3
), after multiplying
\sqrt{|y^{2}-4|}
, which is the square-root of Weyl discriminant, we get

\mathbbm{1}_{|y|>2}f_{\xi}\big(\frac{-y+\sqrt{y^{2}-4}}{2}\big)

that is globally bounded. Hence Part (3) of Theorem
5.5.1
follows from the same argument as the Hausdorff-Young inequality in the non-archimedean case.

The case when
F={\mathbb{R}}
can be treated in a similar vein as
F={\mathbb{C}}
situation, and we give a brief sketch.
Up to constant, the Plancherel formula for
G({\mathbb{R}})
reads as follows (
[
GGPS69
, p.123]
)

{\delta}_{e}(g)=(\mathrm{Dis})+\int_{{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Theta_{+,i{\lambda}}(g){\lambda}\mathrm{tanh}\big(\frac{\pi{\lambda}}{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\Theta_{-,i{\lambda}}(g){\lambda}\mathrm{coth}\big(\frac{\pi{\lambda}}{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

where
(\mathrm{Dis})
is the discrete series part, and for
a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
, the distribution character is given as follows

\Theta_{\pm,i{\lambda}}({\rm diag}(a,a^{-1}))=\frac{|a|^{i{\lambda}}{\mathrm{sgn}}(a)^{n_{\pm}}+|a|^{-i{\lambda}}{\mathrm{sgn}}(a)^{n_{\pm}}}{|a-a^{-1}|}

with
n_{+}=0
and
n_{-}=1
. It suffices to treat
\mathrm{J}_{G}=\big(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c}\big)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
supported on each principal series component individually.

Fix an even entire function
\xi({\lambda})
on
{\mathbb{C}}
that is rapidly decay in bounded horizontal vertical strips. We are studying the following distributions on
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G({\mathbb{R}}))

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}_{+,\xi}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Theta_{+,i{\lambda}}(f)\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\tanh\big(\frac{\pi{\lambda}}{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda},

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}_{-,\xi}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Theta_{-,i{\lambda}}(f)\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\coth\big(\frac{\pi{\lambda}}{2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}

Following the complex case, let
\mathrm{F}_{f}(\pm e^{t})={\Delta}(\pm{\rm diag}(e^{t},e^{-t}))\int_{g\in G({\mathbb{R}})/T({\mathbb{R}})}f(\pm g^{-1}{\rm diag}(e^{t},e^{-t})g)\,\mathrm{d}g

be the normalized orbital integral where
T\subset G
is the diagonal split torus. Then

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}_{+,\xi}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\tanh({\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{i{\lambda}t}\big(\mathrm{F}_{f}(e^{t})+\mathrm{F}_{f}(-e^{t})\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}_{-,\xi}(f)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\coth({\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{i{\lambda}t}\big(\mathrm{F}_{f}(e^{t})-\mathrm{F}_{f}(-e^{t})\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

In other words,

•

As a tempered distribution on the trace variable
\in F={\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathfrak{D}}_{+,\xi}
represents the following tempered distribution: For any Schwartz function
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{D}}_{+,\xi}(\phi)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\tanh({\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}e^{i{\lambda}t}\big(\phi(e^{t}+e^{-t})+\phi(-e^{t}-e^{-t})\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\tanh({\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|x|^{i{\lambda}}\phi(x+x^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}x

•

As a tempered distribution on the trace variable
\in F={\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathfrak{D}}_{-,\xi}
represents the following tempered distribution: For any Schwartz function
\phi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
,

{\mathfrak{D}}_{-,\xi}(\phi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\coth({\lambda})\,\mathrm{d}{\lambda}\int_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|x|^{i{\lambda}}{\mathrm{sgn}}(x)\phi(x+x^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}x

where
{\mathrm{sgn}}(x)
is the sign character.

Following the same analytical estimations in the complex case, the results follow from the following two facts:

•

For the tempered distribution
{\mathfrak{D}}_{+,\xi}
, the Mellin transform of

{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\tanh({\lambda})

represents a Schwartz function on
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})
. Moreover,
\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\tanh({\lambda})
vanishes at zero which proves the vanishing statement in Part (2) of Theorem
5.5.1
. The rest analytical estimations are the same as complex situation and we omit;

•

For the tempered distribution
{\mathfrak{D}}_{-,\xi}
, the Mellin transform of

{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\xi({\lambda}){\lambda}\coth({\lambda})

represents a Schwartz function on
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{\times})
that is even (notice that
{\lambda}\coth({\lambda})
is still an entire function). Moreover, after multiplying by the sign function
{\mathrm{sgn}}(x)
, the resulting function is an odd function on
{\mathbb{R}}
whose integration on
{\mathbb{R}}
automatically vanishes. This proves the desired vanishing property in
Part (2) of Theorem
5.5.1
. The rest analytical estimations are the same as complex situation and we omit.

∎

6.
Calculation of the descent

In this section, we calculate the action of an element in the stable Bernstein center of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
on a dihedral representation over a local field
F
whose residual characteristic is not equal to two. Each element
\mathrm{J}_{G}\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st}
acts on the dihedral representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
associated with a quadratic extension
E/F
and a character
\chi
of
E^{1}
as a scalar
\gamma(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))
. From the integral formula expressing the scalar
\gamma(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))
, we derive Theorem
5.1.1
providing an integral formula for the stable Lafforgue transform. We also establish supplementary results for the image of the Lafforgue transform for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
.

6.1.
Descent formula

In this subsection, we will state and prove the following descent formula. We prove it for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
. The case for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
is similar and we leave it to the reader.

Theorem 6.1.1
.

(1)

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
. Let
\mathrm{J}_{G}=(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
. Then
\mathrm{J}_{G}
acts on the dihedral representation
{\mathcal{S}}(\chi)\subset{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
associated with an étale quadratic
F
-algebra
E
and a character
\chi
of
E^{1}
via the scalar
{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))
given by the formula

(6.1.1)

{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{E^{1}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})\frac{\eta}{|\cdot|}\bigg)({\mathrm{tr}}(e))\chi(e)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

(2)

Let
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
. Let
\mathrm{J}_{G}=(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}_{G}
. Then
\mathrm{J}_{G}
acts on the dihedral representation
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
associated with an étale quadratic
F
-algebra
E
and a character
\chi
of
E^{\times}
via the scalar
{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))
given by the formula

(6.1.2)

{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{E^{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})\frac{\eta}{|\cdot|}\bigg)({\mathrm{tr}}(e))\chi(e)|\mathrm{Nr}(e)|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{\times}}e.

Based on the Gelfand-Graev character formula (
4.3.6
) and the self-adjointness of the Fourier transform, the following corollary holds, which confirms Theorem
5.1.1
.

Corollary 6.1.2
.

With the above notation the following identity holds

{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))=\int_{\xi\in{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}(F)}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|}\bigg)(\xi)\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

Here
\,\mathrm{d}\xi
is the additive Haar measure introduced in subsection
3.1
when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, and is the product of additive Haar measure on trace variable with the multiplicative Haar measure on determinant variable when
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
.

We will use three different approaches to establish the theorem for different types of dihedral representations:

(1)

For
F
non-archimedean and
\chi
a nontrivial character of
E^{1}
, so that
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
is supercuspidal, we establish Theorem
6.1.1
using the so called
Bruhat regularization
;

(2)

For principal series representations, we establish Theorem
6.1.1
through studying the action of
\mathrm{J}_{G}
on a dense subspace of the
universal principal series model
;

(3)

Finally, for
F
the real field and
\chi
a nontrivial character of
E^{1}
, so that
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
is a discrete series representation, we establish Theorem
6.1.1
through connecting the stable discrete series character with the
Chebyshev polynomials
.

The discrete series representations are subrepresentations of principal series representations, hence a prior the third situation follows from the second. However we still find it fascinating to highlight the connection with the Chebyshev polynomials.

6.2.
Supercuspidal representations and Bruhat regularization

Let
F
be a non-archimedean local field,
E
a quadratic extension of
F
, and
\chi:E^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
a nontrivial character. In this case,
\pi={\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
can be identified as the subspace of functions in
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}
that are
\chi
-equivariant. Moreover, the matrix coefficients for
{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
are compactly supported. Therefore for
\mathrm{J}_{G}=\big(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c}\big)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{G}^{{\mathrm{st}},\mathrm{fin}}
, the following limit

(6.2.1)

\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}\int_{g\in G(F)}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K_{n}}\big)(g){\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)v\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g,\quad v\in{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)

is convergent to the following absolutely convergent integral

\int_{g\in G(F)}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c}(g){\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)(g)v\,\mathrm{d}^{*}g.

Here we use the fact that
\mathrm{J}_{G}
is locally integrable from Remark
5.5.2
. By the absolute convergence, we can restrict the integration to the open dense Bruhat cell based on (
1.4.3
) and the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

The following identity holds

\mathrm{J}_{G}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)=\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(t+t^{-1}+tuv)|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

Proof.

It follows from (
1.4.3
) and the following identity

{\mathrm{tr}}\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)=t+t^{-1}+tuv.

∎

It follows that for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
and
z\in E
,

\displaystyle{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))\phi(z)=\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(t+t^{-1}+tuv)

\displaystyle\bigg({\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)\phi\bigg)(z)|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v.

Furthermore, by Lemma
4.1.2
, the following corollary holds.

Corollary 6.2.2
.

With the above notation the following identity holds for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi)
,

\displaystyle{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))\phi(z)={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(t+t^{-1}+tuv)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|}\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)

\displaystyle\eta(t)|t|\psi\big(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)\phi(tx)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v.

In the following, in order to establish Theorem
6.1.1
, we introduce the so called
Bruhat regularization
for the above right hand side integral. We outline the procedure in the following steps:

(1)

Let
{\Lambda}={\mathbb{N}}^{3}
be the index set parameterizing triples of natural numbers. Fix a family of test functions
\{\mathbbm{1}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(F^{\times})
such that
\lim_{n\mapsto\infty}\mathbbm{1}_{n}
tends to the identity function on
F^{\times}
. For
{\lambda}=(i,j,k)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{3}
, let

\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda}(v,t,u)=\mathbbm{1}_{i}(v)\mathbbm{1}_{j}(t)\mathbbm{1}_{k}(u).

(2)

By absolute convergence, Corollary
6.2.2
can be rewritten as follows

\displaystyle{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))\phi(z)={\lambda}_{E/F}\lim_{i\mapsto\infty}\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda}(v,t,u)\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(t+t^{-1}+tuv)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)|t|\psi\big(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)\phi(tx)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v.

(3)

For
{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{1}),{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{2})\geq 0
and
z\in E^{\times}
, consider the following complex deformation of above integral

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda}(v,t,u)|v|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(t+t^{-1}+tuv)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)|t|\psi\big(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)\phi(tx)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

and set

{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(s_{1},s_{2})=\lim_{i\mapsto\infty}\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2}).

By absolute convergence,
{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(s_{1},s_{2})
is holomorphic for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{1}),{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{2})\geq 0
. In particular

{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},{\mathcal{W}}_{E}(\chi))\phi(z)={\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(0,0).

Now we are ready to introduce the Bruhat regularization.

Definition 6.2.3

(Bruhat regularization)
.

Let
F
be any local field and
E
an étale quadratic
F
-algebra. For any
\phi\in{\mathcal{W}}_{E}
,
z\in E^{\times}
and
{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{1}),{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{2})
to be sufficiently large, let

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda}(v,t,u)|v|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(t+t^{-1}+tuv)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)|t|\psi\big(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)\phi(tx)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

and set

{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(s_{1},s_{2})=\lim_{i\mapsto\infty}\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2}),\quad{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}={\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(0,0)

whenever the limit exists and
{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(s_{1},s_{2})
has a holomorphic continuation to
s_{1}=s_{2}=0
.

The main theorem in this subsection is the following.

Theorem 6.2.4
.

With the notation in Definition
6.2.3
,
{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(s_{1},s_{2})
is holomorphic when
s_{1}=s_{2}=s
satisfying
0<{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1
with a holomorphic continuation to
s=0
, and the following identity holds

{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{E^{1}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})\frac{\eta}{|\cdot|}\bigg)({\mathrm{tr}}(e))\phi(ez)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

It is clear that Theorem
6.2.4
implies Theorem
6.1.1
for supercuspidal representations. On the other hand, it is worth mentioning that Theorem
6.2.4
also works for
E
split over
F
. However to derive Theorem
6.1.1
for
E
split over
F
, or say the principal series representations, one need to show that the regularization (
6.2.1
) is equal to
{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}
, which is unknown to us when
E
splits over
F
. In the next subsection, we will use another strategy to resolve the split situation.

In the following, we are going to establish Theorem
6.2.4
.

Proof.

For each fixed
{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{3}
, by Part (1) of Theorem
5.5.1
, the integral defining
{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})
is absolutely convergent as a quadruple integral whenever
{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{1}),{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{2})\geq 0
. We first do a series of changing variables to simplify the integral:

(1)

Write

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\lambda}(v,t,u)|v|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(t+t^{-1}+tuv)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)|t|\psi\big(ut^{2}\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)\phi(tx)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v.

We change

\mu=t+t^{-1}+tuv\longleftrightarrow u=\frac{\mu-t-t^{-1}}{tv}

and get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}(v)\mathbbm{1}_{j}(t)\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(\frac{\mu-t-t^{-1}}{tv}\bigg)|v|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mu)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|^{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-x)}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)\psi\bigg(\frac{(\mu t-t^{2}-1)\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\bigg)\phi(tx)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}v.

(2)

Change variable
x\mapsto x/t
and get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}(v)\mathbbm{1}_{j}(t)\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(\frac{\mu-t-t^{-1}}{tv}\bigg)|v|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mu)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|^{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z-\frac{x}{t})}{v}\bigg)\eta(t)\psi\bigg(\frac{(\frac{\mu}{t}-1-\frac{1}{t^{2}})\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\bigg)\phi(x)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}v.

The term in the additive character can be calculated as follows

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)-{\mathrm{tr}}(\bar{z}x/t)+\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t^{2}}}{v}\bigg)\psi\bigg(\frac{(\frac{\mu\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}-\mathrm{Nr}(x)-\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t^{2}})}{v}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)-{\mathrm{tr}}(\bar{z}x/t)+\frac{\mu\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}-\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\bigg).

Therefore

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}(v)\mathbbm{1}_{j}(t)\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(\frac{\mu-t-t^{-1}}{tv}\bigg)|v|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mu)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|^{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\eta(t)\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)-{\mathrm{tr}}(\bar{z}x/t)+\frac{\mu\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}-\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\bigg)\phi(x)

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}v.

(3)

Change
x\mapsto xz
and get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}(v)\mathbbm{1}_{j}(t)\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(\frac{\mu-t-t^{-1}}{tv}\bigg)|v|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mu)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|^{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}

\displaystyle\eta(t)\psi\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)-\mathrm{Nr}(z){\mathrm{tr}}(x/t)+\frac{\mu\mathrm{Nr}(x)\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{t}-\mathrm{Nr}(x)\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\bigg)

\displaystyle\phi(xz)|\mathrm{Nr}(z)|\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}v.

(4)

Change
v\mapsto v\mathrm{Nr}(z)
and get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(v\mathrm{Nr}(z)\big)\mathbbm{1}_{j}(t)\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(\frac{\mu-t-t^{-1}}{tv\mathrm{Nr}(z)}\bigg)\big|v\mathrm{Nr}(z)\big|^{s_{1}}|t|^{s_{2}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mu)\frac{\eta(-v)}{|v|^{2}}\int_{x\in E}\eta(t)\psi\bigg(\frac{1-{\mathrm{tr}}(x/t)+\frac{\mu\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}-\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\bigg)

\displaystyle\phi(xz)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}v.

(5)

Change
t\mapsto-\frac{t\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}
and get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(v\mathrm{Nr}(z)\big)\mathbbm{1}_{j}\big(-\frac{t\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(-\frac{\mu+\frac{t\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}+\frac{v}{\mathrm{Nr}(x)t}}{t\mathrm{Nr}(x)\mathrm{Nr}(z)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\big|v\mathrm{Nr}(z)\big|^{s_{1}}\big|\frac{t\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}\big|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mu)\frac{\eta(t)}{|v|^{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\frac{1-\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{v}+{\mathrm{tr}}\big(\frac{1}{tx}\big)-\frac{\mu}{t}\bigg)\phi(xz)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}v.

(6)

Finally we change
t\mapsto t^{-1}
and
v\mapsto v^{-1}
, and get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{j}\big(-\frac{v\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(-\frac{\mu t+v\mathrm{Nr}(x)+\frac{t^{2}}{\mathrm{Nr}(x)v}}{\mathrm{Nr}(x)\mathrm{Nr}(z)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\big|\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big|^{s_{1}}\big|\frac{v\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}\big|^{s_{2}}\mathrm{J}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}(\mu)\eta(t)

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\big(1-\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)v+{\mathrm{tr}}\big(\frac{t}{x}\big)-\mu t\bigg)\phi(xz)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathrm{d}v.

After the tedious variable changes we are ready to take the iterated limit

{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}(s_{1},s_{2})=\lim_{i\mapsto\infty}\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2}).

We first evaluate the limit

\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2}).

Since
\{\mathbbm{1}_{n}\}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(F^{\times})
, for any
s_{1},s_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the integration in
v,t
and
x
are all absolutely convergent. By Part (1) of Theorem
6.1.1
,
\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\mu)
is absolutely integrable. Hence by the dominated convergence theorem we can switch the integration in
u,v,x
and
\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}
to get

\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}\int_{\mu\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{k}\bigg(-\frac{\mu t+v\mathrm{Nr}(x)+\frac{t^{2}}{\mathrm{Nr}(x)v}}{\mathrm{Nr}(x)\mathrm{Nr}(z)}\bigg)\psi(-\mu t)\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\mu)\,\mathrm{d}\mu={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(t).

Hence

\displaystyle\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{u,v\in F,t\in F^{\times}}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big)\mathbbm{1}_{j}\big(-\frac{v\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(t)\big|\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big|^{s_{1}}

\displaystyle\big|\frac{v\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}\big|^{s_{2}}\eta(t)\int_{x\in E}\psi\bigg(\big(1-\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)v+{\mathrm{tr}}\big(\frac{t}{x}\big)\bigg)

(6.2.2)

\displaystyle\phi(xz)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}v.

Now by Part (2) of Theorem
6.1.1
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(t)
is smooth and globally bounded. Moreover
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})
vanishes at zero, it implies the following fact:

•

When
F
is non-archimedean, the globally bounded smooth function
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})
vanishes in a neighborhood of zero. Therefore
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(t)|t|^{-s}
is absolutely integrable for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)>1
;

•

When
F
is archimedean, the globally bounded smooth function
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})
vanishes at zero. Therefore
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(t)|t|^{-s}
is

–

absolutely integrable near zero for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<2
,

–

and absolutely integrable near infinity for
{\mathrm{Re}}(s)>1
.

In conclusion
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(t)|t|^{-s}
is absolutely integrable for
1<{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<2
.

Therefore whenever
1<{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{2}+1)<2
(notice that we use the multiplicative Haar measure
\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t
), the integration in
u,v,x
are all absolutely convergent. Hence we can apply the dominated convergence theorem again to evaluate the limit
\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}
:

\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\int_{t\in F^{\times}}\mathbbm{1}_{j}\big(-\frac{v\mathrm{Nr}(x)}{t}\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(t)|t|^{-s_{2}-1}\eta(t)\psi\bigg({\mathrm{tr}}\big(\frac{t}{x}\big)\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}t={\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{\eta{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{-s_{2}-1}}\bigg)\big({\mathrm{tr}}(\frac{1}{x})\big).

In other words, for
0<{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{2})<1
and any
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})=

\displaystyle{\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{v\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big)\big|\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big|^{s_{1}}\big|v\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big|^{s_{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{x\in E}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{\eta{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{-s_{2}-1}}\bigg)\big({\mathrm{tr}}(\frac{1}{x})\big)\psi\bigg(\big(1-\mathrm{Nr}(x)\big)v\bigg)\phi(xz)\,\mathrm{d}_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}v.

Notice that the right hand side above is indeed absolutely convergent, since
\mathbbm{1}_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(F^{\times})
, and
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{\eta{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{-s_{2}-1}}\bigg)
is globally bounded for
0<{\mathrm{Re}}(s_{2})<1
.

Finally we evaluate the limit
\lim_{i\mapsto\infty}
. Since the right hand side integral is holomorphic for any
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{C}}
, we can let
s_{1}=s_{2}
, and break the integral in
x\in E
according to the short exact sequence
1\to E^{1}\to E^{\times}\to\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})\to 1
to get

\displaystyle\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{1},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}

\displaystyle\int_{v\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big)\psi\big((1-a)v\big)\,\mathrm{d}v\int_{a\in\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})}\big|\mathrm{Nr}(z)a\big|^{s_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}a

\displaystyle\int_{e\in E^{1}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{\eta{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{-s_{2}-1}}\bigg)\big({\mathrm{tr}}(\frac{1}{e_{a}e})\big)\phi(e_{a}ez)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e

where
e_{a}\in E^{\times}
such that
\mathrm{Nr}(e_{a})=a
.
For abbreviation, let
\mathrm{F}(a)
be the function supported on
\mathrm{Nr}(E^{\times})
given by

\mathrm{F}(a)=\psi(-av)\big|\mathrm{Nr}(z)a\big|^{s_{2}}\int_{e\in E^{1}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{\eta{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{-s_{2}-1}}\bigg)\big({\mathrm{tr}}(\frac{1}{e_{a}e})\big)\phi(e_{a}ez)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

Then the above integral can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\lambda}_{E/F}\int_{v\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big)\psi(v)\,\mathrm{d}v\int_{a\in F}\psi(-av)\mathrm{F}(a)\,\mathrm{d}a.

By Fourier inversion,

\lim_{i\mapsto\infty}\int_{v\in F}\mathbbm{1}_{i}\big(\frac{\mathrm{Nr}(z)}{v}\big)\psi(v)\,\mathrm{d}v\int_{a\in F}\psi(-av)\mathrm{F}(a)\,\mathrm{d}a=\mathrm{F}(1).

Therefore

\lim_{i\mapsto\infty}\lim_{j\mapsto\infty}\lim_{k\mapsto\infty}{\mathrm{I}}_{\phi,z}({\lambda},s_{2},s_{2})={\lambda}_{E/F}\big|\mathrm{Nr}(z)\big|^{s_{2}}\int_{e\in E^{1}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{\eta{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{-s_{2}-1}}\bigg)\big({\mathrm{tr}}(e)\big)\phi(ez)\,\mathrm{d}_{E^{1}}e.

Now
\phi|_{E^{1}}
is a test function on
E^{1}
, hence by Part (3) of Theorem
6.1.1
, the right hand side has a holomorphic continuation to
s_{2}=0
in the sense of tempered distributions. It follows that we complete the proof.

∎

6.3.
Principal series representations

In this subsection, we establish the theorem for principal series representations of
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
.

Let
F
be a local field. The principal series reprsentations of
G(F)={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
can be realized in the following induced representation model

i_{\chi}={\mathrm{Ind}}^{G}_{B}(\chi)=\{f:{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}(G(F))\to{\mathbb{C}}\mid f(bg)={\delta}^{1/2}_{B}(b)\chi(b)f(g)\}

where
B=TU
is the upper triangular Borel subgroup of
G
,
{\delta}_{B}
is the modular character of
B(F)
, and
\chi:B(F)\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
is a quasi-character factoring through the diagonal torus
T(F)
. As explained in
[
JL70
]
, the relation between
i_{\chi}
and those constructed via Weil representations are connected by the Whittaker models for
i_{\chi}
. In the following, instead of working with a particular principal series, we use the idea of Theorem
6.2.4
to work with the universal principal series model. Precisely, we consider the following right
G
-equivariant commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{J}_{G}}
\scriptstyle{r_{U}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{U}}
\textstyle{i_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{J}_{G/U}}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}
\textstyle{i_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}}
\textstyle{i_{\chi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi})}
\textstyle{i_{\chi}}

Here
{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))={\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G(F))
when
F
is non-archimedean, and is the Schwartz algebra of
G(F)
when
F
is archimedean. The space
{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
has a natural
G
-equivariant projection
r_{U}
:

i_{U}=\mathrm{ind}^{G}_{U}=\big\{r_{U}(f)(g)=g\in G(F)\mapsto\int_{u\in U(F)}f(ug)\,\mathrm{d}u\mid f\in{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))\big\}.

The projection
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
is defined via the following integral

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}:i_{U}

\displaystyle\to i_{\chi}

(6.3.7)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigg(g\mapsto\int_{t\in T(F)}\chi^{-1}(t){\delta}_{B}^{-1/2}(t)\phi(tg)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\bigg).

The integrals above are all absolutely convergent since the restriction of Schwartz functions on
G(F)
to closed subgroups are still Schwartz (
[
BZ76
]
[
AG08
]
). In particular the map
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
is entire in
\chi
.

The following lemma shows that the diagram (
6.3
) is well-defined.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

The operators
\mathrm{J}_{G/U}
is well-defined. Moreover, under the right
G
-equivariant projection
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
, it descends down to the multiplication by the function
\chi\in\widehat{T}(F)\mapsto{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi})
between
i_{\chi}
.

Proof.

To show that
\mathrm{J}_{G/U}
is well-defined, we only need to show that for
f\in{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
, if
r_{U}(f)=0
, then
r_{U}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)=0
. Since
G(F)
is semi-simple,
{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
is a subspace of
{\mathcal{C}}(G(F))
, the space of Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions on
G(F)
(
[
Del86
, Lem. 1.1]
for archimedean. The result is manifest for non-archimedean). In particular,
f
is a strongly cuspidal function in the sense of
[
BP15
, §5.1]
. By
[
BP15
, (5.3.1)]
,
f
is strongly cuspidal if and only if the operator
i_{\chi}(f)=0
for any (tempered) character of
T(F)
. Since
i_{\chi}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)={\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi})i_{\chi}(f)
, it follows that
i_{\chi}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)=0
and hence
\mathrm{J}_{G}*f
is also strongly cuspidal. Hence
r_{U}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)=0
identically, from which we deduce that the operator
\mathrm{J}_{G/U}
is well-defined.

Next we show that under the right
G
-equivariant projection
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}
,
\mathrm{J}_{G/U}
descends to the multiplication by the function
\chi\in\widehat{T}(F)\mapsto{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi})
.

First, we show that the map is well-defined. Essentially we need to show that for
\phi\in i_{U}
, if
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}(\phi)=0
identically, then
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G/U}(\phi)\big)=0
identically. By Mellin transform,
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}(\phi)=0
for any
\chi
implies that
\phi=0
. Therefore
\mathrm{J}_{G/U}(\phi)=0
which implies that
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G/U}(\phi)\big)=0
. Hence, the bottom map is well-defined.
It remains to show that the bottom map is the multiplication by the function
\chi\in\widehat{T}(F)\mapsto{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi})
. For generic
\chi
,
i_{\chi}
is irreducible. Hence by Schur’s lemma, the bottom map is given by multiplying a constant. It suffices to show that the constant is equal to
{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi})
.

For any
f\in{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
, fix a test function
e_{K}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G(F))
. Let us compute

{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\bigg(r_{U}\big((\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})*f\big)\bigg).

By definition, it is equal to the following iterated integral

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\bigg(r_{U}\big((\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})*f\big)\bigg)(g)=\int_{t\in T(F)}\chi^{-1}(t){\delta}^{-1/2}_{B}(t)r_{U}\big((\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})*f\big)(tg)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t\in T(F)}\chi^{-1}(t){\delta}^{-1/2}_{B}(t)\int_{u\in U(F)}\big((\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})*f\big)(utg)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t\in T(F)}\chi^{-1}(t){\delta}_{B}^{-1/2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\int_{u\in U(F)}\,\mathrm{d}u\int_{h\in G(F)}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})(h)f(h^{-1}utg)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}h.

Let
g
be the identity element, and put
f^{\vee}(g)=f(g^{-1})
, then the above identity becomes

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t\in T(F)}\chi^{-1}(t){\delta}_{B}^{-1/2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\int_{u\in U(F)}\,\mathrm{d}u\int_{h\in G(F)}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})(h)f^{\vee}(t^{-1}u^{-1}h)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t\in T(F)}\chi^{-1}(t){\delta}_{B}^{-1/2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\int_{u\in U(F)}\,\mathrm{d}u\int_{h\in G(F)}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})(h)f^{\vee}(t^{-1}uh)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}h.

The above integration is absolutely convergent since
\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K}\in{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
, hence we may change variable
u\mapsto tut^{-1}
and get

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t\in T(F)}\chi^{-1}(t){\delta}_{B}^{1/2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\int_{u\in U(F)}\,\mathrm{d}u\int_{h\in G(F)}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})(h)f^{\vee}(ut^{-1}h)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{h\in G(F)}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})(h){\mathcal{P}}_{\chi^{-1}}\big(r_{U}(f^{\vee})\big)(h)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}h.

In conclusion, we have established the following identity

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\bigg(r_{U}\big((\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})*f\big)\bigg)({\mathrm{Id}})=\int_{h\in G(F)}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K})(h){\mathcal{P}}_{\chi^{-1}}\big(r_{U}(f^{\vee})\big)(h)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}h.

Now we choose
\{e_{K_{n}}\}_{n\geq 1}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(G(F))
to be a delta sequence tending to the identity element at
G(F)
, then
(\mathrm{J}_{G}*e_{K_{n}})*f=e_{K_{n}}*(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)
is stably convergent to
\mathrm{J}_{G}*f
if
F
is non-archimedean, and is an approximation to identity if
F
is archimedean. Precisely, when
F
is archimedean,
e_{K_{n}}*(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)
converges to
\mathrm{J}_{G}*f
, at least under the
L^{1}
-norm on
G(F)
. As a result, by Fubini’s theorem and smoothness,
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\big(r_{U}(e_{K_{n}}*\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)\big)
converges to
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\big(r_{U}(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)\big)
pointwise.

Hence by continuity, the left hand side converges to

{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\bigg(r_{U}\big((\mathrm{J}_{G}*f)\big)\bigg)({\mathrm{Id}})

and the right hand side converges to

{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi^{-1}}){\mathcal{P}}_{\chi^{-1}}\big(r_{U}(f^{\vee})\big)({\mathrm{Id}})={\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi}){\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\big(r_{U}(f)\big)({\mathrm{Id}}),\quad i_{\chi}\simeq i_{\chi^{-1}}.

We complete the proof of the theorem.
∎

Based on Lemma
6.3.1
, to examine the action of
\mathrm{J}_{G}
on the principal series
\{i_{\chi}\}_{\chi\in\widehat{T}}
which provides the constant
{\gamma}(\mathrm{J}_{G},i_{\chi})
, it suffices to examine the action of
\mathrm{J}_{G/U}
on some special class of vectors in
i_{U}
, which we view as the
universal principal series model
.

We are going to establish Theorem
6.1.1
for principal series representations. We choose functions
f\in{\mathcal{S}}(G(F))
that are supported on the open dense Bruhat cell given by

(6.3.8)

\displaystyle f\bigg(\begin{pmatrix}1&\\
v&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\bigg)=\phi_{0}(v)\phi_{1}(t)\phi_{2}(u)

such that
\phi_{0},\phi_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(F)
and
\phi_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(F^{\times})
.
It is clear that
{\mathcal{P}}_{\chi}\big(r_{U}(f^{\vee})\big)
maps into a dense subspace of
i_{\chi}
. In the following, we examine the action of
\mathrm{J}_{G/U}
on
\big(r_{U}(f^{\vee})\big)
.

By the commutativity of the right
G
-equivariant diagram (
6.3
), it suffices to calculate
r_{U}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f\big)({\mathrm{Id}})
. By definition,

\displaystyle r_{U}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f\big)({\mathrm{Id}})=\int_{\widetilde{u}\in U(F)}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{u}\int_{h\in G(F)}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\circ\mathbf{c}(\widetilde{u}h^{-1})f(h)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}h.

It is worth mentioning that in general, the above integral might only be convergent as an iterated integral. Write
h
via the coordinate (
6.3.8
), then

r_{U}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f\big)({\mathrm{Id}})
is equal to

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{u}\in F}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{u}\int_{v,u\in F,t\in F^{\times}}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}\big(t^{-1}+t+tv(u-\widetilde{u})\big)\phi_{0}(v)\phi_{1}(t)\phi_{2}(u)|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v

(6.3.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{u}\in F}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{u}\int_{v,u\in F,t\in F^{\times}}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(t^{-1}+t+tvu)\phi_{0}(v)\phi_{1}(t)\phi_{2}(u+\widetilde{u})|t|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v\quad(u\mapsto u+\widetilde{u}).

Here we abuse the notation and write

\widetilde{u}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&\widetilde{u}\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\right).

By the unitarity of Fourier transform, we can write

\displaystyle\int_{v\in F}\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}}(t+t^{-1}+tvu)\phi_{0}(v)\,\mathrm{d}v=\int_{\xi\in F}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(\xi)\psi\big(\xi(t+t^{-1})\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{0})(tu\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

Hence (
6.3.9
) can be rewritten as the following iterated integral

\displaystyle r_{U}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f\big)({\mathrm{Id}})=\int_{\widetilde{u}\in F}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{u}

\displaystyle\int_{u\in F,t\in F^{\times}}\phi_{1}(t)|t|^{2}\phi_{2}(u+\widetilde{u})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\in F}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(\xi)\psi\big(\xi(t+t^{-1})\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{0})(tu\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

Motivated from the proof for the supercupsidal case, we introduce the following complex deformation of the above integral. Precisely, for
0<{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1
, consider the following integral

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}(s)=\int_{\widetilde{u}\in F}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{u}

\displaystyle\int_{u\in F,t\in F^{\times}}\phi_{1}(t)|t|^{2}\phi_{2}(u+\widetilde{u})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u

(6.3.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\in F}|\xi|^{-s}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(\xi)\psi\big(\xi(t+t^{-1})\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{0})(tu\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

It is clear that
{\mathrm{I}}(0)=r_{U}\big(\mathrm{J}_{G}*f\big)({\mathrm{Id}})
.

We establish the following theorem, which is one of the main results in this subsection.

Theorem 6.3.2
.

For
0\leq{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1
, the quadruple integral defining
{\mathrm{I}}(s)
is absolutely convergent. Moreover, the following identity holds

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}(s)=\int_{t\in F^{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{s+1}}\bigg)(t+t^{-1})|t|^{-1}r_{U}(f^{\vee})\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t,\quad 0\leq{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1.

Proof.

We first show that the quadruple integral defining
{\mathrm{I}}(s)
is absolutely convergent for
0\leq{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1
. Changing variable
u\mapsto u/(t\xi)
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}(s)=\int_{\widetilde{u}\in F}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{u}

\displaystyle\int_{u\in F,t\in F^{\times}}\phi_{1}(t)|t|\phi_{2}\big(\frac{u}{t\xi}+\widetilde{u}\big)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\int_{\xi\in F}|\xi|^{-s-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(\xi)\psi\big(\xi(t+t^{-1})\big){\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi_{0})(u)\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

The integration in
\widetilde{u}
and
u
are clearly absolutely integrable since
\phi_{0},\phi_{2}
are both Schwartz-Bruhat functions. Similarly the integration in
t
-variable is also absolutely convergent since
\phi_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(F^{\times})
. Finally, based on Theorem
5.5.1
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})(\xi)|\xi|^{-(s+1)}
is absolutely integrable when
0\leq{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1
. It follows that the above quadruple integral defining
{\mathrm{I}}(s)
is indeed absolutely convergent.

Now the integration in
\widetilde{u}
provides
\int_{\widetilde{u}\in F}\phi_{2}(\widetilde{u})\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{u}
, the integration in
u
provides
\phi_{0}(0)
by Fourier inversion, and the integration in
\xi
yields a Fourier transform, which, in conclusion, provides

\displaystyle{\mathrm{I}}(s)=

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(0)\int_{u\in F}\phi_{2}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\int_{t\in F^{\times}}\phi_{1}(t)|t|{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{s+1}}\bigg)(t+t^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t\in F^{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{s+1}}\bigg)(t+t^{-1})|t|^{-1}r_{U}(f^{\vee})\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t.

It follows that we complete the proof of the proposition.
∎

Now to finalize the proof of Theorem
6.1.1
for principal series representations, it suffices to show that both sides of the equality in Theorem
6.3.2
has a continuous extension to
s=0
, which is the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3.3
.

Both sides of the equality in Theorem
6.3.2
has a continuous extension to
s=0
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the right hand side has a continuous extension to
s=0
. In other words,

\displaystyle\lim_{s\mapsto 0^{+}}

\displaystyle\int_{t\in F^{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|^{s+1}}\bigg)(t+t^{-1})|t|^{-1}r_{U}(f^{\vee})\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{t\in F^{\times}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}\bigg(\frac{{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})}{|\cdot|}\bigg)(t+t^{-1})|t|^{-1}r_{U}(f^{\vee})\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,\mathrm{d}^{*}t.

Precisely, the function
r_{U}(f^{\vee})
, when restricted to the diagonal torus, is a test function on
F^{\times}
. Hence the above identity follows from the continuity of Fourier transform and the continuous
s
-family of tempered distributions
{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\mathrm{J}_{\mathfrak{c}})/|\cdot|^{s+1}
for
0\leq{\mathrm{Re}}(s)<1
.

∎

7.
Bernstein decomposition of the cocenter

The stable transfer gives rise to certain quotients of the stable cocenter
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
. From Corollary
3.5.4
, at least when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
or
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, we know that
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
is a module over the stable Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st}
. It turns out that some of those quotients are canonically direct summands of
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
. In this section, we construct these summands by explicit sections and integral transforms based on the orthogonality of elliptic characters for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
. The existence of explicit sections realizing these direct summands was inspired by the work of Johnstone and the first-named author (
[
JL23
]
).

7.1.
Total transfer to tori

In this subsection, following Remark
4.3.6
, we describe the total stable transfer map explicitly. Let
F
be a local field whose residual characteristic is not equal to two. Following (
3.1.8
) up to stable conjugacy, there are four, two or one maximal tori in
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
depending on
F
being non-archimedean, real, or complex. As in (
3.1.8
) we let
{\mathcal{I}}
be the index set parametrizing étale quadratic
F
-algebras and let
\{T_{\alpha}\}_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}
be the set of maximal tori in
G(F)
up to stable conjugacy. Let
\eta_{\alpha}
be the corresponding quadratic characters of
F^{\times}
attached to
E_{\alpha}
. For
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}
, there is a homomorphism of algebras

\rho_{\alpha}^{*}:{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm fin,st}\to{\mathcal{Z}}_{\alpha}^{\rm fin,st}={\mathcal{Z}}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\rm fin,st}

whose image is the subring
({\mathcal{Z}}_{\alpha}^{\rm fin,st})^{\tau_{\alpha}}
of
\tau_{\alpha}
-invariant elements, where
\tau_{\alpha}
is the unique nontrivial involution over
F
for
E_{\alpha}
. Its kernel are given by elements
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm fin,st}
such that
\gamma(J,\pi)=0
for all representations
\pi
belonging to an
L
-packet
[\pi]
coming from
T_{\alpha}
by dihedral lifting.

We also have the Langlands stable transfer map

{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}:{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T_{\alpha}(F))

whose image is the space of
\tau_{\alpha}
-invariants
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}
. When
F
is archimedean, the Langlands stable transfer map also extends to the Schwartz algebra
{\mathcal{S}}

{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}:{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})\to{\mathcal{S}}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}

which follows from the following adjoint identity and the Paley-Wiener theorem for the Schwartz algebras (
[
Bar88
, p.100]
[
GGV66
, Chap. IV, §5.6]
and the variant for
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
)

\langle{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(f),\chi\rangle=\langle f,\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}\rangle,\quad f\in{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})\supset{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}).

In this subsection, we consider the total transfer to tori

(7.1.1)

{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}:{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{c}^{\infty}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}

The following theorem reflects our knowledge of
L
-packets of
G(F)
by means of the dihedral representations, which are reviewed in subsection
4.2
, notably Proposition
4.2.1
and Theorem
4.2.2
(
[
Cas72
, Thm. 1.7]
).

Theorem 7.1.1
.

Let
F
be non-archimedean of odd residual characteristic.

(1)

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
. The kernel of the total transfer map
{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}
is the one-dimensional vector space generated by the normalized elliptic character of the Steinberg representation. The image of the total transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}
is the finite-codimensional subspace of
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}}\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}
consisting of quadruples

(q_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}})\in\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{c}^{\infty}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}

satisfying the equations

(a)

\langle q_{0},\eta_{\alpha}\rangle=\langle q_{\alpha},1_{\alpha}\rangle
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
, where
1_{\alpha}
are the trivial characters of
T_{\alpha}=E_{\alpha}^{1}
and
\eta_{\alpha}
is the quadratic character of
T_{0}=F^{\times}=E_{0}^{1}
corresponding to the quadratic extension
E_{\alpha}
of
F
.

(b)

\langle q_{\alpha},\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\alpha}\rangle=\langle q_{\beta},\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\beta}\rangle
for all
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
, where
\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\alpha}
and
\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\beta}
are the unique nontrivial quadratic characters of
T_{\alpha}=E_{\alpha}^{1}
and
T_{\beta}=E_{\beta}^{1}
respectively.

Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the standard pairing between characters and test functions on
T_{\alpha}
.

(2)

Let
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
. The kernel of the total transfer map
{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}
is the one-parameter family generated by normalized elliptic characters of twisted Steinberg representations. The image of the total transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}
is the subspace of
\bigoplus_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}
consisting of quadruples

(q_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}})\in\bigoplus_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}}\mathcal{{\mathcal{C}}}_{c}^{\infty}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}

satisfying the equations

(a)

\langle q_{0},(\chi,\chi\otimes\eta_{\alpha})\rangle=\langle q_{\alpha},\chi\circ\mathrm{Nr}_{\alpha}\rangle
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
, where
\chi
is a character of
F^{\times}
and
\mathrm{Nr}_{\alpha}:E^{\times}_{\alpha}\to F^{\times}
is the norm map.

(b)

\langle q_{\alpha},\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\alpha}\rangle=\langle q_{\beta},\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\beta}\rangle
for all
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
, where
\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\alpha}
and
\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{\beta}
are the characters of
E^{\times}_{\alpha}
and
E^{\times}_{\beta}
such that
\chi_{\alpha}
and
\chi_{\beta}
does not factor through the norm map but their square does, and their dihedral lifting to
G(F)
are isomorphic.

Proof.

We only prove the statement for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and the case for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
is similar. By the density of stable tempered characters from Theorem
3.5.1
, an element
f\in{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
is zero if and only if its stable trace on tempered representations are zero. For
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, it is equivalent to say that
f=0
if and only if
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}(f)=0
for all
\alpha\in\{0,1,\pm 1/2\}
and if the trace
{\mathrm{tr}}{\rm St}(f)
of
f
on the Steinberg representation is zero. Thus the kernel of the total transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}
is at most one-dimensional generated by the (stable) orbital integral of the pseudo-coefficient of Steinberg representation, which is exactly equal to the normalized elliptic character of Steinberg representation.

The equations describing the compatibility between different tori follows from the explicit description of the dihedral lifting, in particular Theorem
4.2.2
and
[
JL70
, Thm. 4.6]
.
∎

We have the parallel theorem for
F
archimedean. The proof is identical and we omit.

Theorem 7.1.2
.

Let
F
be archimedean.

(1)

When
F
is the field of complex numbers, the stable transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{0}
is the identity map. Hence
{\mathcal{T}}_{0}:{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})\simeq{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T_{0}(F))^{\tau_{0}}
.

(2)

When
F
is the field of real numbers, the total transfer map
{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}
is injective. The image of the total transfer
{\mathcal{T}}_{\oplus}
is the subspace of
\bigoplus_{{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}
enjoying the same characterization as Theorem
7.1.1
.

(3)

The above statements also hold if we replace
{\mathcal{H}}
by
{\mathcal{S}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}
by
{\mathcal{S}}(T_{\alpha}(F))^{\tau_{\alpha}}
.

7.2.
The discrete part of the cocenter

Let
F
be non-archimedean. The action of the Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}
on the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}
preserves the commutatator
[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
and hence induces an action on the cocenter
{\rm O}({\mathcal{H}})={\mathcal{H}}/[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{H}}]
. Assuming the stable center conjecture and the identification of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{{\mathrm{st}},\prime}
with the stable Bernstein center
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
that is discussed in Remark
3.5.2
, which in particular is valid for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
by Theorem
3.5.1
, we derive an action of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\rm st}
on the stable cocenter
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
which induces a decomposition of
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
according to the connected components of the stable Bernstein variety
\Omega^{\rm st}(G(F))
. In particular, for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
, the stable Bernstein variety
\Omega^{\rm st}(G(F))
is the disjoint union of the supercuspidal components and the union of parabolically induced components,

\Omega^{\rm st}(G(F))=\Omega^{\rm st}(G(F))_{\rm cus}\sqcup\Omega^{\rm st}(G(F))_{\rm ind},

which induces a decomposition of the stable cocenter
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
into a direct sum

{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})={\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm cus}\oplus{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm ind}.

When
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
,
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm cus}
is supported by the union of zero-dimensional components and
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm ind}
is supported by the union of one-dimensional components. We stress that
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm ind}
may and does contain elements supported by zero-dimensional subschemes of
\Omega^{\rm st}(G(F))
which are contained in one-dimensional components. These functions are multiple of the normalized elliptic character of the Steinberg representation.

We have the following theorem.

Theorem 7.2.1
.

The kernel of the stable transfer factor for the split torus which is the restriction map

{\mathcal{T}}_{0}:{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{c}(T_{0}(F))^{\tau_{0}}

consists of elements
f\in{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
which, as functions on the Steinberg-Hitchin base, are supported on the elliptic locus

{\rm supp}(f)\subset{\mathfrak{c}}(F)^{\rm ell}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}}\pi_{\alpha}(T_{\alpha}(F)).

We will call the discrete part of the cocenter the kernel of
{\mathcal{T}}_{0}

{\rm ker}({\mathcal{T}}_{0})={\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm dis}.

When
F
is non-archimedean, there is a direct sum decomposition

{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm dis}={\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm cus}\oplus{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm St}.

Moreover,

{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm cus}
is the direct summand of
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
supported by the cuspidal components of the stable Bernstein variety, and
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})_{\rm St}
is
the subspace of
{\rm SO}({\mathcal{H}})
that are annihilated by the stable dihedral characters, and is precisely generated by the normalized elliptic Steinberg characters
(
twisted by
{\mathcal{H}}(F^{\times})
along the center when
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the decomposition for
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})_{{\mathrm{disc}}}
. We prove it for
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and the case for
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
is similar. Let
F
be local non-archimedean. For
J\in{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
and
f\in{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})
, by the spectral characterization of distributions in the (stable) Bernstein center,

(7.2.1)

J(f)=\int_{\pi\in\widehat{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}}{\gamma}(J,\pi)\langle f,\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\pi}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\pi

where
\langle f,\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\pi}\rangle
is the stable trace pairing between
f
and the stable tempered character
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\pi}
. Here we use the fact that the Plancherel measure is constant on the tempered local
L
-packet of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
(
[
GGPS69
]
). For
f\in{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathrm{disc}}
vanishing on the split locus, its trace pairing vanishes for principal series representations. Hence (
7.2.1
) can be rewritten as

J(f)=\int_{\pi\in\widehat{G}^{\mathrm{disc}}}{\gamma}(J,\pi)\langle f,\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\pi}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\pi.

where
\widehat{G}^{{\mathrm{disc}}}
is the set of discrete series representations of
G(F)
that can be written as the disjoint union
=\widehat{G}^{{\mathrm{cus}}}\sqcup\mathrm{St}
. Here
\widehat{G}^{\mathrm{cusp}}
is the set of supercuspidal representations of
G(F)
and
\mathrm{St}
is the Steinberg representation. Hence
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathrm{St}}
is stable under the action of
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{st}}
and is the one-dimensional complement of
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathrm{cus}}
, the cuspidal support of
{\Omega}^{\mathrm{st}}(G(F))_{\mathrm{cus}}
. It follows that we complete the proof of the theorem.
∎

Similarly, for
F
archimedean, we also have a decomposition

{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})={\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})_{\mathrm{disc}}\oplus{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})_{\mathrm{ind}}

with
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})_{\mathrm{disc}}
the subspace of functions supported on elliptic locus of
G(F)
, and
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{S}})_{\mathrm{ind}}
the subspace supported on split locus only.

7.3.
Scalar product on the discrete part of the cocenter

In this subsection, we will reformulate the orthogonality of elliptic characters (
[
Kaz86b
]
[
Art93
]
) as a scalar product on the discrete part of the cocenter.

Let
G
be a reductive group over a local field
F
. Let
Z
be the split part of the center of
G
over
F
. For two discrete series representations
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2}
of
G(F)
with the same unitary central character, let
\Theta_{i}
be their distribution characters and
\Theta^{C}_{i}
be the induced function on
C(F)
, the set of strongly regular semi-simple conjugacy classes of
G(F)
. Then the following identity holds

(7.3.1)

\int_{\overline{C}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}}\Theta^{C}_{1}(c)\overline{\Theta}^{C}_{2}(c){\Delta}(c)\mathbf{v}(c){\omega}_{\overline{C}}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}1&\pi_{1}\simeq\pi_{2}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}.

The identity follows from the orthogonality of elliptic tempered characters. Here following subsection
3.1
,
\overline{C}(F)=C(F)/Z(F)
is the set of strongly regular semi-simple conjugacy classes of
\overline{G}(F)=G(F)/Z(F)
equipped with the induced measure
{\omega}_{\overline{C}}
,
\overline{C}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}
is the subset of elliptic locus, and
\mathbf{v}(c)
is the inverse of the volume of the connected centralizer of
c\in\overline{C}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}
in
\overline{G}(F)
. The pairing (
7.3.1
) can be upgraded to a scalar product on the discrete part of the cocenter as follows.

Theorem 7.3.1
.

For any unitary character
\chi
of
Z(F)
, let
{\mathcal{H}}_{\chi}
be the space of smooth functions on
G(F)
that are compactly supported modulo
Z(F)
, and are
(Z(F),\chi)
-equivariant. Let
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\chi})
be the
(Z(F),\chi)
-equivariant cocenter and let
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\chi})_{\mathrm{disc}}
be the subspace of
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\chi})
consisting of functions on
C(F)
whose restriction to the hyperbolic locus vanishes. Then
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\chi})_{\mathrm{disc}}
is a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module. Moreover,
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\chi})_{\mathrm{disc}}
has a Hermitian definite inner product

(f_{1},f_{2})=\int_{\overline{C}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}}f_{1}(c)\overline{f}_{2}(c){\Delta}(c)^{-1}\mathbf{v}(c){\omega}_{\overline{C}}.

Comparing with (
7.3.1
), it is
{\Delta}(\cdot)^{-1}
rather than
{\Delta}(\cdot)
appearing in the above pairing because of the normalization in Corollary
3.2.2
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathrm{disc}}
, the subspace of
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})
consisting of functions vanishing on the hyperbolic locus, is a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module. By the density of principal series characters on the split locus,
f\in{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})
lies in
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathrm{disc}}
if and only if

\langle f,\Theta^{C}_{\pi}\rangle=0

for any principal series representation
\pi
of
G(F)
. But since
{\mathcal{Z}}
acts via scaling on any principal series representations, it follows that
{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})_{\mathrm{disc}}
is a
{\mathcal{Z}}
-module.
∎

Similarly, we can introduce a inner product pairing on the discrete part of the stable cocenter. The split center
Z
acts on the Steinberg-Hitchin base
{\mathfrak{c}}={\mathfrak{c}}_{G}
. Let
\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)={\mathfrak{c}}_{\overline{G}}(F)={\mathfrak{c}}_{G}(F)/Z(F)
which can be viewed as the Steinberg-Hitchin base for
\overline{G}
equipped with the induced measure
{\omega}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}}
. Let
\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}
be the subset of
\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)
corresponding to elliptic locus. For
a\in\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}
, let
\mathbf{v}(a)
be the inverse of the volume of the connected centralizer of
a\in\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}
in
\overline{G}(F)
. For any unitary character
\chi
of
Z(F)
, there is the following Hermitian definite inner product on
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\chi})_{\mathrm{disc}}
, which is the subspace of
{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\chi})
vanishing on the split locus of
\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)
,

(7.3.2)

(f_{1},f_{2})=\int_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}}f_{1}(a)\overline{f}_{2}(a){\Delta}(a)^{-1}\mathbf{v}(a){\omega}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}}.

Remark 7.3.2
.

For the purpose of next subsection, we introduce the following inner product pairing that is parallel to (
7.3.2
): For two stable distribution characters
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
of
G(F)
with the same unitary central character,

(7.3.3)

(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=\int_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}}\theta_{1}(a)\overline{\theta}_{2}(a){\Delta}(a)\mathbf{v}(a){\omega}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}}.

Similarly, for two functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathrm{S}}{\mathrm{O}}({\mathcal{H}})
, define

(7.3.4)

(f_{1},f_{2})_{{\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=\int_{{\mathfrak{c}}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}}f_{1}(a)\overline{f}_{2}(a){\Delta}(a)\mathbf{v}(a){\omega}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}.

7.4.
Another unitary structure on the affine line

Starting from this subsection, we focus on
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}
over a local field
F
. Let
Z
be the split center of
G
and
\overline{G}(F)=G(F)/Z(F)
. In particular, when
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
,
\overline{G}(F)=G(F)
.

The Gelfand-Graev transform for
G(F)
defined by the formulae (
4.3.10
) (
4.3.11
) is clearly unitary with respect to the standard measure on the affine line since both the Fourier transform and the multiplication by the quadratic characters are unitary. It is a remarkable fact that it is unitary with respect to another measure derived from the orthogonality of elliptic tempered characters, which is stated below.

Theorem 7.4.1
.

Let
G={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
over a local field
F
of residual characteristic other than two. For any
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}
, let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{E_{\alpha}}={\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}:L^{2}(F,\,\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(F,\,\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto{\lambda}_{{\alpha}}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\psi^{-1}}(\eta_{\alpha}\cdot{\mathcal{F}}_{\psi}(\phi))\big)

Then the following statements hold.

(1)

Fix a quadratic field extension
E=E_{\alpha}
of
F
with
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
. Let
\phi_{i}

(i=1,2)
be two functions supported on
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1})
that are square-integrable with respect to the measure
{\Delta}(a)\,\mathrm{d}a
, and
(\phi_{i},\phi_{\mathbbm{1}_{{\alpha}}})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=0
for
i=1
or
2
, where
\mathbbm{1}_{{\alpha}}
is the trivial character of
E^{1}_{\alpha}
and
\phi_{\mathbbm{1}}
is the function supported on
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})\subset{\mathfrak{c}}_{G}(F)=F
defined by (
4.3.4
), i.e. the constant Fourier coefficient of
\phi_{1}
or
\phi_{2}
, viewed as a function on
E^{1}_{\alpha}
, vanishes. Then the following identity holds.

\big({\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\phi_{1}),{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\phi_{2})\big)_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}.

(2)

Fix
{\alpha},{\beta}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
with
{\alpha}\neq{\beta}
. For
\phi
supported on
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})
that is square-integrable with respect to the measure
{\Delta}(a)\,\mathrm{d}a
, the following identity holds

\big({\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\alpha}}(\phi_{1}),{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\beta}}(\phi_{\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{{\beta}}})\big)_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=(\phi,\phi_{\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{{\alpha}}})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}

where
\chi^{\mathrm{qd}}_{*}
is the unique non-trivial character of
E^{1}_{*}
whose square is trivial. In particular, it happens only when
F
is non-archimedean.

We first treat the case when
F={\mathbb{R}}
,
{\alpha}=1
and hence
E_{\alpha}={\mathbb{C}}
. For two characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
of
E^{1}_{\alpha}
, let
\theta_{\chi_{i}}^{\mathrm{st}}
be the stable character for the discrete series character for
G(F)
. From the orthogonality of elliptic discrete series characters for
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, or based on the stable character formula in
[
Lan13
, (1.3.1)]
, the following identity holds

\int_{\overline{C}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}=C(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}}\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}(c)\overline{\theta}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}(c){\Delta}(c)\mathbf{v}(c){\omega}_{\overline{C}}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}2&\phi_{\chi_{1}}=\phi_{\chi_{2}}\neq\phi_{\mathbbm{1}}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

where
\mathbbm{1}
is the trivial character of
E^{1}_{\alpha}={\mathbb{C}}^{1}
and
\phi_{\chi}
is defined in (
4.3.4
) attached to
\chi
. Descending down to the Steinberg-Hitchin base, and using the fact that the trace map is generically a
2
-fold cover, the elliptic orthogonality can be reformulated as follows

\displaystyle(\theta_{\chi_{1}}^{\mathrm{st}},\theta_{\chi_{2}}^{\mathrm{st}})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}1&\phi_{\chi_{1}}=\phi_{\chi_{2}}\neq\phi_{\mathbbm{1}}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

By the Gelfand-Graev stable character formula (
4.3.10
), the above identity can be reformulated as follows

(7.4.1)

\displaystyle\big({\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\phi_{\chi_{1}}),{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\phi_{\chi_{2}})\big)_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=\bigg\{\begin{matrix}(\phi_{\chi_{1}},\phi_{\chi_{2}})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}&\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}=\mathbbm{1}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

By Mellin transform (which boils down to Fourier series in this case), the characters
\{\chi\}_{\chi\in\widehat{E}^{1}_{\alpha}}
form an
L^{2}
-basis for square integrable functions on
E^{1}_{\alpha}
. In particular, for functions descending down to
{\mathrm{tr}}(E^{1}_{\alpha})
,
\{\phi_{\chi}\}_{\chi\in\widehat{E}^{1}_{\alpha}}
is a spanning set. Hence as a corollary, for two smooth (or even square-integrable) functions on
E^{1}_{\alpha}
, by linearity and continuity, the following identity holds as long as
(\phi_{i},\phi_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=0
for
i=1
or
2
(i.e. the constant Fourier coefficient of
\phi_{1}
or
\phi_{2}
vanishes),

(7.4.2)

\displaystyle\big({\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\phi_{1}),{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\phi_{2})\big)_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}},\quad\text{if}\quad(\phi_{i},\phi_{\mathbbm{1}})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=0\text{ for $i=1$ or $2$}.

As a consequence, we have established the following theorem.

Theorem 7.4.2
.

For
F={\mathbb{R}}
and
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
and hence
E_{\alpha}={\mathbb{C}}
, the operator
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{C}}:={\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbb{C}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathrm{tr}}({\mathbb{C}}^{1})}
is unitary on

L^{2}_{0}({\mathrm{tr}}({\mathbb{C}}^{1}),{\Delta}(a)\,\mathrm{d}a):=\{\phi\in L^{2}({\mathrm{tr}}({\mathbb{C}}^{1}),{\Delta}(a)\,\mathrm{d}a)\mid(\phi,\phi_{\mathbbm{1}})=0\}.

Here
\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathrm{tr}}({\mathbb{C}}^{1})}
is the characteristic function of
{\mathrm{tr}}({\mathbb{C}}^{1})\subset{\mathbb{R}}
.

Notice that when
F={\mathbb{R}}
, up to stable conjugacy, there is only one elliptic torus in
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Hence we can drop the volume factor
\mathbf{v}(a)
.

Remark 7.4.3
.

It would be interesting to find a direct proof of Theorem
7.4.2
without referring to the orthogonality of elliptic tempered characters.

Next, we assume that
F
is non-archimedean with odd residual characteristic. There is a parallel unitary structure on
F
with mild modification (the modification is needed due to the existence of the unique supercuspidal local
L
-packet of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
of cardinality four).

We follow the same idea as the real case. Let
E
be a quadratic
F
-algebra. The stable distribution character of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
attached to a character
\chi:E^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
is given by

(7.4.3)

\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}(g)={\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{E}(\phi_{\chi})({\mathrm{tr}}(g)),\quad g\in{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)

From
[
JL70
, Thm. 4.6]
[
Cas72
, Thm. 1.9]
[
She79b
, §11]
, the stable distribution character
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}
admits the following descriptions:

(7.4.4)

\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}=\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi^{-1}}=\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi^{\iota}}
for any
\chi:E^{1}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
. Here
\iota=\iota_{E}
is the unique nontrivial involution of
E
over
F
;

(7.4.5)

\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}
is the restriction of the distribution character of a principal series of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
when
\chi=\mathbbm{1}
, which in particular vanishes over elliptic locus;

(7.4.6)

\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}
is the restriction of the distribution character of a supercuspidal representation of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{2}(F)
when
\chi\neq\mathbbm{1}
. Moreover, the following statements hold:

(i)

If
\chi^{2}\neq\mathbbm{1}
, then
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}
is the sum of the distribution characters of two supercuspidal representations of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
lying in the same local
L
-packet of cardinality two with the local
L
-parameter of dihedral type determined by
\chi
. For
\phi_{\chi_{1}}\neq\phi_{\chi_{2}}
with
\chi_{i}^{2}\neq\mathbbm{1}
, the corresponding supercuspidal representations of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
are not isomorphic to each other;

(ii)

If
\chi
is the unique nontrivial character of
E^{1}
with
\chi^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
, then
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi}
is the sum of the distribution characters of four supercuspidal representations of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
lying in the unique local
L
-packet with four elements. Moreover, for two different quadratic extensions
E_{\alpha}
,
E_{\beta}
of
F
with
{\alpha},{\beta}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
and
{\alpha}\neq{\beta}
, let
\chi_{*}
be the unique character of
E^{1}_{*}
such that
\chi_{*}\neq\mathbbm{1}
and
\chi_{*}^{2}=\mathbbm{1}
, then
\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi_{\alpha}}=\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi_{\beta}}
.

The elliptic orthogonality for the stable distribution characters provides the following identity

\int_{\overline{C}(F)^{\mathrm{ell}}}\theta^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi_{1}}(c)\overline{\theta}^{\mathrm{st}}_{\chi_{2}}(c){\Delta}(c)\mathbf{v}(c){\omega}_{\overline{C}}=\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}2&\phi_{\chi_{1}}=\phi_{\chi_{2}}\text{ and }\chi^{2}_{i}\neq\mathbbm{1}\\
4&\chi_{i}\neq\mathbbm{1}\text{ and }\chi^{2}_{i}=\mathbbm{1}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

From
[
MT05a
, p.335]
, up to conjugation by
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
there are four (resp. six) elliptic tori in
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(F)
within three stable conjugacy classes parametrized by quadratic extensions
E/F
when
-1\notin F^{\times 2}
(resp.
-1\in F^{\times 2}
). Moreover the number of tori within its stable conjugacy class is equal to the cardinality of the Weyl group of the tori. Hence, by (
7.4.3
) and the fact that the trace map is generically a
2
-fold cover, the elliptic orthogonality can be reformulated as follows:

•

For
*={\alpha},{\beta}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
,

(7.4.7)

\displaystyle\big({\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\alpha}(\phi_{\chi_{\alpha}}),{\mathcal{G}}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\beta}}(\phi_{\chi_{\beta}})\big)_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}1&\phi_{\chi_{\alpha}}=\phi_{\chi_{\beta}}\text{ and }\chi^{2}_{*}\neq\mathbbm{1}\\
2&\chi_{*}\neq\mathbbm{1}\text{ and }\chi^{2}_{*}=\mathbbm{1}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\begin{matrix}(\phi_{\chi_{\alpha}},\phi_{\chi_{\beta}})_{\overline{{\mathfrak{c}}}^{\mathrm{ell}}}&\phi_{\chi_{\alpha}}=\phi_{\chi_{\beta}}\text{ and }\chi^{2}_{*}\neq\mathbbm{1}\\
2&\chi_{*}\neq\mathbbm{1}\text{ and }\chi^{2}_{*}=\mathbbm{1}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}

Notice that when
\chi^{2}_{*}=\mathbbm{1}
,
\chi_{*}=\chi_{*}^{-1}
.

For
{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{I}}\backslash\{0\}
, following the same idea as the real case, the set of characters of
E^{1}_{\alpha}
,
\{\chi\}_{\chi\in\widehat{E}^{1}_{\alpha}}
, forms an
L^{2}
-basis for square-integrable functions on
E^{1}_{\alpha}
. Hence for smooth functions on
